# GGI Lectures on Large-Scale Structure Perturbation Theory (Effective Field Theory)

**arXiv ID**: 2607.28314v1
**Authors**: Mikhail M. Ivanov
**Published**: 2026-07-30
**Categories**: astro-ph.CO, gr-qc, hep-ph, hep-th
**Comments**: 87 pages, 19 figures, an extended version of lectures delivered at the "New Physics from Galaxy Clustering'' school at the Galileo Galilei Institute, Florence, in August 2025
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.28314v1

## Abstract

These notes are an introduction to non-linear perturbation theory for cosmological large-scale structure. They are aimed at undergraduate and beginning graduate students and do not require any cosmology or quantum field theory background. All necessary concepts are developed from scratch. The lectures are intended to explain all key ingredients needed to model the observed clustering of galaxies in real and redshift spaces. After a brief pedagogical introduction to the ideas of effective field theory (EFT), we develop large-scale structure EFT in the context of Newtonian cosmology using symmetry principles. We discuss in detail the shortcomings of Standard Perturbation Theory, the non-linear evolution of baryon acoustic oscillations and its relation to the equivalence principle, counterterms and renormalization of the loop diagrams, and stochastic effects. Then we develop EFT for galaxy bias and redshift space distortions. We also highlight some important facts about redshift-space stochasticity, relevant for ongoing and future galaxy surveys. Finally, we introduce Lagrangian Perturbation Theory.

## MIT-CTP/6084GGI Lectures on
Large-Scale Structure Perturbation Theory (Effective Field Theory)
Mikhail M. Ivanov

## Abstract

These notes are an introduction to non-linear perturbation
theory for cosmological large-scale structure.
They are aimed at undergraduate
and beginning graduate students and do not require any cosmology or
quantum field theory background. All necessary concepts are developed
from scratch.
The lectures are intended to explain all key ingredients needed to
model the observed clustering of galaxies in real and redshift spaces.
After a brief pedagogical introduction to the ideas of
effective field theory (EFT), we develop large-scale structure
EFT in the context of Newtonian cosmology
using symmetry principles.
We discuss in detail
the shortcomings of Standard Perturbation Theory,
the non-linear evolution of baryon acoustic oscillations
and its relation to the equivalence principle,
counterterms and renormalization of the loop diagrams,
and stochastic effects. Then we develop EFT for galaxy bias
and redshift space distortions. We also highlight
some important facts about redshift-space stochasticity,
relevant for ongoing and future galaxy surveys. Finally, we
introduce Lagrangian Perturbation Theory.

## 1Chapter 1: Structure Formation and the EFT Ideology

The basic empirical picture of structure formation in our Universe
goes as follows: small, linear dark-matter overdensities grow under
gravity, become non-linear at small scales, collapse to form halos,
and then complex baryonic physics inside halos produces the galaxies
we observe. Galaxies are extremely non-linear objects. There is
absolutely zero hope to understand this chain in full generality
from first principles.
Nevertheless, on sufficiently large scales thestatistical
distributionof galaxies is predictable. This observation is the
central motivation for applying effective-field-theory (EFT) ideas
to large-scale structure: on large scales, perturbation theory and
the EFT go hand in hand, just as they do in fluid mechanics,
statistical physics, or particle physics.

What is an EFT? It is an approach in which one isolates the physics
on large scales from the small-scale complications. Specifically,
one builds a simplified description of the large-scale part of the
system. The EFTideologyrests on the following assumptions:
- •

There is a separation of scales: a long-wavelength scaleLlongL_{\mathrm{long}}, which we are interested in, and a short scaleLshortL_{\mathrm{short}}, where the complicated dynamics happens. They
satisfyLlong≫LshortL_{\mathrm{long}}\gg L_{\mathrm{short}}.
- •

Some simplified dynamics emerges on scales∼Llong\sim L_{\mathrm{long}}. It is captured by macroscopic,
coarse-grained dynamical fields{Fi}\{F_{i}\}, called “degrees of
freedom.”
- •

Observables on large scales, at a given finite accuracy,
depend on the small-scale dynamics only through afiniteset of parameters and functions (“operators”) built from the
macroscopic fields and their derivatives.
- •

This simplified description is organized as an analytic
(derivative) expansion in the small parametersLshort/LlongL_{\mathrm{short}}/L_{\mathrm{long}}andFiF_{i}.
- •

The effective description is completely constrained by
symmetries.

In short: the answer for any large-scale observable is ananalytic functionofLshort/LlongL_{\mathrm{short}}/L_{\mathrm{long}}in
the limitLshort/Llong→0L_{\mathrm{short}}/L_{\mathrm{long}}\to 0, with a finite
number of coefficients encoding the short-scale physics.

## 1.1A Simple Example: Multipole Expansion in Electrostatics

A textbook example is the multipole expansion in electrostatics.
For a localized (and arbitrarily complicated) charge distribution
of spatial sizeaa, the potential at distanceR≫aR\gg aadmits the
expansionΦ​(𝑹)=QR+𝑫⋅𝒏^R2+Qi​j​n^i​n^jR3+⋯,𝒏^=𝑹/R,\Phi({\bm{R}})=\frac{Q}{R}+\frac{{\bm{D}}\cdot\hat{{\bm{n}}}}{R^{2}}+\frac{Q_{ij}\,\hat{n}^{i}\hat{n}^{j}}{R^{3}}+\cdots\,,\qquad\hat{{\bm{n}}}={\bm{R}}/R\,,(1)

with the monopole (total charge), dipole and quadrupole momentsQ=∫d3​x​ρQ​(𝒙),𝑫=∫d3​x​𝒙​ρQ​(𝒙),Qi​j=∫d3​x​(xi​xj−δi​j​𝒙23)​ρQ​(𝒙),Q=\int d^{3}x\;\rho_{Q}({\bm{x}})\,,\qquad{\bm{D}}=\int d^{3}x\;{\bm{x}}\,\rho_{Q}({\bm{x}})\,,\qquad Q^{ij}=\int d^{3}x\left(x^{i}x^{j}-\frac{\delta^{ij}{\bm{x}}^{\,2}}{3}\right)\rho_{Q}({\bm{x}})\,,(2)

built from the charge densityρQ\rho_{Q}. At a given order this
expansion involves only a few parameters. At very large distances
(R→∞R\to\infty) it does not matter how complicated the distribution
of the charges is: the answer depends on a single parameter, the
system’s total charge. At second order we need four parameters —
the charge and the three components of the dipole moment𝑫{\bm{D}};
the quadrupole adds five more (a symmetric3×33\times 3tensor has
six components, minus one for tracelessness), and so on — always
afinitenumber. The power counting reads𝑫⋅𝒏^R2∼Q​aR2≪QR,Qi​j​n^i​n^jR3∼Q​a2R3≪Q​aR2≪QR,\frac{{\bm{D}}\cdot\hat{{\bm{n}}}}{R^{2}}\sim\frac{Q\,a}{R^{2}}\ll\frac{Q}{R}\,,\qquad\frac{Q_{ij}\,\hat{n}^{i}\hat{n}^{j}}{R^{3}}\sim\frac{Q\,a^{2}}{R^{3}}\ll\frac{Q\,a}{R^{2}}\ll\frac{Q}{R}\,,(3)

i.e. as long asa/R≪1a/R\ll 1we expect the expansion to converge
rapidly. Note also the role of the symmetries: charge conservation
fixes the leading coefficient, and rotational invariance dictates
the tensor structures — the1/R21/R^{2}term requires a vector, the1/R31/R^{3}term a symmetric traceless tensor.

The key EFT lessons from the multipole expansion are:
- 1.

Analyticity:the expansion is in powers ofa/Ra/R.
- 2.

Universality:different microscopic realizations
share the same long-distance description — only the multipole
moments matter, regardless of the small-scale details of the
charge distribution.
- 3.

Afinite number of parametersis sufficient at a
given order.

The multipole expansion is, however, too simple: the problem is
static, so we do not yet see the role of the dynamical degrees of
freedom. Let us consider a slightly more complicated EFT, which
will be directly relevant for structure formation.

## 1.2Fluid Dynamics as an EFT: Navier–Stokes and Symmetry Constraints

Fluid dynamics is another canonical EFT. The short scale here is
the microscopic mean free path,ℓmfp≃1n¯​⟨σ⟩,\ell_{\mathrm{mfp}}\simeq\frac{1}{\bar{n}\,\langle\sigma\rangle}\,,(4)

wheren¯\bar{n}is the number density of particles and⟨σ⟩\langle\sigma\ranglethe collision cross-section. On scales much larger
thanℓmfp\ell_{\mathrm{mfp}}, a many-body system with arbitrarily complicated
interactions can be described by coarse-grained fields: the mass
densityρ​(𝒙,t)\rho({\bm{x}},t), the velocity𝒗​(𝒙,t){\bm{v}}({\bm{x}},t), and the pressurep​(𝒙,t)p({\bm{x}},t)(for an adiabatic fluid,p=cs2​ρp=c_{s}^{2}\rho). The emergent
simplified dynamics is governed by the continuity and Navier–Stokes
equations,∂ρ∂t+∇⋅(ρ​𝒗)=0,\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}+\nabla\cdot(\rho\,{\bm{v}})=0\,,(5)ρ​D​viD​t≡ρ​(∂vi∂t+vj​∂jvi)=−∂ip+ν​ρ​Δ​vi+ρ​(ν3+ζ)​∂i∂kvk,\displaystyle\rho\,\frac{Dv^{i}}{Dt}\equiv\rho\left(\frac{\partial v^{i}}{\partial t}+v^{j}\partial_{j}v^{i}\right)=-\partial^{i}p+\nu\rho\,\Delta v^{i}+\rho\left(\frac{\nu}{3}+\zeta\right)\partial^{i}\partial_{k}v^{k}\,,(6)

whereΔ=∂i∂i\Delta=\partial^{i}\partial_{i}, andν\nuandζ\zetaare the
shear and bulk viscosity coefficients.

To decide which operators are important at a given scale, we
perform simple scaling estimates. Consider a state with constant
background densityρ0\rho_{0}and velocity fluctuations characterized
by a wavenumberkk, frequencyω\omegaand velocity amplitudevkv_{k}, which we take to be of the order of the sound speedcsc_{s}.
These are just acoustic waves, with the dispersion relationω=cs​k\omega=c_{s}k. Then:
- •

inertial (time-derivative) term:ρ​∂tv∼ρ0​ω​vk∼ρ0​k​cs2\rho\,\partial_{t}v\sim\rho_{0}\,\omega\,v_{k}\sim\rho_{0}\,k\,c_{s}^{2},
- •

advection (non-linear) term:ρ​v⋅∇v∼ρ0​k​cs2\rho\,v\cdot\nabla v\sim\rho_{0}\,k\,c_{s}^{2},
- •

pressure gradient:∇p∼k​cs2​ρ0\nabla p\sim k\,c_{s}^{2}\,\rho_{0},
- •

viscous term:ν​ρ​Δ​v∼ρ0​ν​k2​vk\nu\rho\,\Delta v\sim\rho_{0}\,\nu\,k^{2}\,v_{k}.

On dimensional groundsν∼cs​ℓmfp\nu\sim c_{s}\,\ell_{\mathrm{mfp}}, hence the ratio of the
viscous to the perfect-fluid terms scales asν​k2ω∼k​ℓmfp∼ℓmfpλ≪1.\frac{\nu k^{2}}{\omega}\sim k\,\ell_{\mathrm{mfp}}\sim\frac{\ell_{\mathrm{mfp}}}{\lambda}\ll 1\,.(7)

This is the expansion parameter of the imperfect-fluid description:
the derivative expansion is controlled byk​ℓmfp≪1k\ell_{\mathrm{mfp}}\ll 1. In the
large-scale limitk→0k\to 0the viscous terms vanish and we are left
with the perfect-fluid equations. The viscous stress captures the
leading non-vanishing corrections to this description. The
structure is thus very similar to the multipole expansion: the
perfect-fluid equations are the analog of the monopole
approximation, while the viscosity terms in the Navier–Stokes
equation play the role of the dipole.

It is instructive toderivethe Navier–Stokes equation from
the EFT principles. This exercise shows that this equation is
inevitable if we stick to the EFT ideology. Once we have determined
that the density and the velocity are the relevant degrees of
freedom, mass conservation and momentum balance immediately give∂ρ∂t+∇⋅(ρ​𝒗)=0,\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}+\nabla\cdot(\rho\,{\bm{v}})=0\,,(8)ρ​D​viD​t=∂jτi​j,\displaystyle\rho\,\frac{Dv^{i}}{Dt}=\partial_{j}\tau^{ij}\,,(9)

whereτi​j\tau^{ij}is the effective stress tensor that encodes the
forces generated by the complicated short-scale physics. On large
scales we can express it throughρ\rho,𝒗{\bm{v}}and their
derivatives. The symmetries then do all the work.τi​j\tau^{ij}is a
rank-2 tensor with respect to 3D rotations, and its dependence on
the velocity must respectGalilean symmetry, i.e. it must
be invariant under constant velocity shifts𝒗→𝒗+𝒄{\bm{v}}\to{\bm{v}}+{\bm{c}}. This implies that the velocity field can only
appear with a gradient acting on it — terms like a barevi​vjv^{i}v^{j}without derivatives are forbidden. The only options through orderk​ℓmfpk\ell_{\mathrm{mfp}}areτi​j=A​ρ​δi​j+B​ℓmfp​ρ​∂ivj+C​ℓmfp​ρ​δi​j​∂kvk+⋯,\tau^{ij}=A\,\rho\,\delta^{ij}+B\,\ell_{\mathrm{mfp}}\,\rho\,\partial^{i}v^{j}+C\,\ell_{\mathrm{mfp}}\,\rho\,\delta^{ij}\,\partial_{k}v^{k}+\cdots\,,(10)

whereA∼cs2A\sim c_{s}^{2}andB,C∼csB,C\sim c_{s}are constants fixed by the
microscopic dynamics, and the ellipsis stands for higher-order
terms. Symmetrizing this expression and renaming the constants, we
arrive at the familiar formτi​j=−cs2​ρ​δi​j⏟“monopole”+ρ​ν​(∂ivj+∂jvi−23​δi​j​∂kvk)+ρ​ζ​δi​j​∂kvk⏟“dipole”.\tau^{ij}=\underbrace{-c_{s}^{2}\,\rho\,\delta^{ij}}_{\text{``monopole''}}\;+\;\underbrace{\rho\,\nu\left(\partial^{i}v^{j}+\partial^{j}v^{i}-\tfrac{2}{3}\,\delta^{ij}\,\partial_{k}v^{k}\right)+\rho\,\zeta\,\delta^{ij}\,\partial_{k}v^{k}}_{\text{``dipole''}}\,.(11)

The pressure is the only term allowed at zeroth order in gradients.
The shear and bulk viscous terms involveonespatial
derivative acting on the velocity and describe momentum flux; they
are the leading derivative corrections and thus play the role of
the “dipole” in the organized expansion. If the precision of our
experiment is high enough, we may need to include higher-order
terms like∂ivj​∂kvk\partial_{i}v^{j}\,\partial_{k}v^{k}, analogous to the
quadrupole.111Beyond the leading order two separate
expansions appear: one in the gradients, like∂i∂jρ\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\rhoor∂i∂j(∂kvk)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}(\partial_{k}v^{k}),
and one in the number of fields, like the term∂ivj​∂kvk\partial_{i}v^{j}\,\partial_{k}v^{k}above. New scales may also arise,
such as the relaxation time — see e.g. the Burnett equations,
the Müller–Israel–Stewart theory, and the hydrodynamics of
heavy ions and holographic fluids.

The upshot is that the symmetries (Galilean and rotational
invariance) together with the gradient-plus-field expansion
completely fix the leading form ofτi​j\tau^{ij}: the Navier–Stokes
equation is determined by symmetries and dimensional analysis —
itisan EFT. Since the long-wavelength dynamics depends
only on a few coefficients (csc_{s},ν\nu,ζ\zeta), many different
microscopic systems are described by the same macroscopic
equations: this is the statement ofuniversality. We have an
amazing equation that describesanymany-body interacting
dynamics with only three parameters. From the EFT point of view,
the only thing that distinguishes air from tar is the values of the
fluid coefficients; otherwise the dynamical equations of the two
systems are completely equivalent and fully determined by
symmetries.Key ConceptsSimplicity:3 parameters characterise any fluid (csc_{s},ν\nu,ζ\zeta).Universality:any fluid is described by 3 parameters, e.g. air & tar.

## 2Chapter 2: Recap of Linear Cosmology and Gaussian Random Fields

## 2.1Linear Perturbations

We will review now some basic information about matter perturbations
in linear cosmology. More details can be found in excellent textbooks such as
Dodelson, Schmidt, “Modern Cosmology”[1].
In what follows all numerical quantities and plots
are computed for thePlanck2018baselineΛ\LambdaCDM cosmological
model[2],
unless we fit data from a simulation, in which case
we use the fiducial cosmology of that simulation.
The baseΛ\LambdaCDM parameters are:ParameterSymbolValueBaryon densityωb≡Ωb​h2\omega_{b}\equiv\Omega_{b}h^{2}0.022370.02237Cold dark matter densityωc≡Ωc​h2\omega_{c}\equiv\Omega_{c}h^{2}0.12000.1200Hubble constanthh0.67360.6736Scalar spectral indexnsn_{s}0.96490.9649Amplitude of scalar perturbationsln⁡(1010​As)\ln(10^{10}A_{s})3.0443.044Optical depth to reionisationτreio\tau_{\rm reio}0.05440.0544

The derived parameters relevant for large-scale structure are:
total matter fraction todayΩm=0.3153\Omega_{m}=0.3153,
the linear mass fluctuation amplitudeσ8=0.8111\sigma_{8}=0.8111(defined shortly).
The sum of neutrino masses is fixed to the minimal value∑mν=0.06​eV\sum m_{\nu}=0.06\;\mathrm{eV}, with one massive and two
massless species.

The key object of interest is the overdensity contrast
of matter defined asδ=ρ​(𝒙,t)−ρ¯​(t)ρ¯​(t),\delta=\frac{\rho({\bm{x}},t)-\bar{\rho}(t)}{\bar{\rho}(t)}\,,(12)

whereρ​(t)\rho(t)is the background matter density that satisfies the Friedmann equations:H2≡(a˙a)2=8​π​G3​(ρ​(t)+ρΛ),ρ˙+3​H​ρ=0,H^{2}\equiv\left(\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\right)^{2}=\frac{8\pi G}{3}(\rho(t)+\rho_{\Lambda})\,,\quad\dot{\rho}+3H\rho=0\,,(13)

where we have ignored the contributions of radiation and neutrinos.
Likewise, one can define the spatial fluctuations of galaxy number densityngn_{g}through he
viaδg=ng​(𝒙,t)−n¯g​(t)n¯g​(t);n¯g=NV,\delta_{g}=\frac{n_{g}({\bm{x}},t)-\bar{n}_{g}(t)}{\bar{n}_{g}(t)}\,;\qquad\bar{n}_{g}=\frac{N}{V}\,,(14)

whereNNis the total number of galaxies in an observed comoving volumeVV.δ\delta,δg\delta_{g}arerandom stochastic fields.
This means their observed phases do not carry any interesting information.
To link these observables to fundamental physics, we need to study theircorrelations.
The simplest one is the
two-point function of matter:⟨δ​(𝒙)​δ​(𝒙+𝒓)⟩=ξ​(|𝒓|),\langle\delta({\bm{x}})\,\delta({\bm{x}}+{\bm{r}})\rangle=\xi(|{\bm{r}}|)\,,(15)

where the dependence only the module of the separation between the
two points follows from the statistical homogeneity and isotropy of our Universe:
all directions look the same (hence no dependence on the vector𝒓{\bm{r}})
and different patches of the sky also look the same (hence no dependence on the exact position
of the galaxy𝒙{\bm{x}}). Due to these arguments the one-point correlator can only be a constant which
we can absorb into the background density, which then ensures that⟨δ​(𝒙)⟩=0.\langle\delta({\bm{x}})\rangle=0\,.(16)

It is convenient to work in Fourier space, where the Fourier image of the matter
density contrast looks like this:δ​(𝒙)=∫d3​k(2​π)3​ei​𝒌⋅𝒙​δ​(𝒌),\delta({\bm{x}})=\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}}\;\delta({\bm{k}})\,,(17)

The two correlation function of the Fourier-space density field reads⟨δ​(𝒌)​δ​(𝒌′)⟩=(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌+𝒌′)​P​(k).\langle\delta({\bm{k}})\,\delta({\bm{k}}^{\prime})\rangle=(2\pi)^{3}\,\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}+{\bm{k}}^{\prime})\,P(k)\,.(18)

whereP​(k)P(k)is called thepower spectrum.
The power spectrum is a function of the wavenumberkkonly. This is another consequence
of the statistical homogeneity and isotropy.
The Dirac delta function above reflects the momentum conservation
which is a consequence of the translational invariance suggested by the statistical homogeneity.
It is convenient to remove it (and(2​π)3(2\pi)^{3}) by introducing the
primed correlator, which simply strips off the delta function:⟨δ​(𝒌)​δ​(𝒌′)⟩′≡P​(k).\langle\delta({\bm{k}})\,\delta({\bm{k}}^{\prime})\rangle^{\prime}\equiv P(k)\,.(19)

From eq. (17) and eq. (18) it is easy to show that the correlation function
is simply a Fourier transform of the power spectrum, which turns into a spherical Fourier transform
thanks to the cosmological principle:ξ​(r)=∫d3​k(2​π)3​ei​𝒌⋅𝒓​P​(k)=∫k2​d​k2​π2​P​(k)​sin⁡(k​r)k​r.\xi(r)=\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{r}}}\,P(k)=\int\frac{k^{2}\,dk}{2\pi^{2}}\;P(k)\;\frac{\sin(kr)}{kr}\,.(20)

The reverse us also true: the power spectrum is a spherical Fourier image of the two-point
correlation function:P​(k)=4​π​∫𝑑r​r2​ξ​(r)​sin⁡(k​r)k​r.P(k)=4\pi\int dr\;r^{2}\;\xi(r)\;\frac{\sin(kr)}{kr}\,.(21)

Let us discuss now the properties of the matter fluctuations
in linear cosmological perturbation theory. For the usual baryons and cold dark matter
the linear solution of the cosmological perturbation theory for the matter field after the recombination
reads:δ1​(𝒌,z)=D+​(z)​δ0​(𝒌),\delta_{1}({\bm{k}},z)=D_{+}(z)\,\delta_{0}({\bm{k}})\,,(22)

whereδ1​(k,z)\delta_{1}(k,z)is the linear matter density, andδ0\delta_{0}is (up to a normalization) the initial matter density,
which in the large-scale structure context means the matter density after recombination, when
baryons and dark matter form a single fluid to a very good approximation. A nice feature of linear matter
perturbations is that their equation of motion does not depend on wavenumber, hence
the time-evolution is simply captured by a functionD+​(z)D_{+}(z), called the “growth factor.”
In the standard cosmology matter grows uniformly on all scales.
Let us discuss now thelinear matter power spectrum:⟨δ1​(𝒌,z)​δ1​(𝒌′,z)⟩′=P11​(k,z).\langle\delta_{1}({\bm{k}},z)\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}^{\prime},z)\rangle^{\prime}=P_{11}(k,z)\,.(23)

Cosmological evolution generates many interesting features in thekk-dependence ofP11P_{11}, see
the left panel of fig.1.
They can be summarized as follows.Shape ofP11​(k,z)P_{11}(k,z):Figure 1:Left:the linear matter power spectrum as a function of wavenumberkkfor several values of redshift, computed with theclasscode[3].Right:thez=0z=0linear matter power spectrum and the asymptotic behaviours∝k\propto kask→0k\to 0and∝k−3​ln⁡(k/keq)\propto k^{-3}\ln(k/k_{\rm eq}),keq≈0.01​h/Mpck_{\rm eq}\approx 0.01~\,h/\mathrm{Mpc}. We ignore the power spectrum tiltnsn_{s}.
- •

Peak atk∼keq≈0.02​h/Mpck\sim k_{\mathrm{eq}}\approx 0.02\,h/\mathrm{Mpc}.
- •

Lowkk(k→0k\to 0):P11​(k)∝kP_{11}(k)\propto k(goes to zero linearly).
- •

Highkk(k→∞k\to\infty):P​(k)∼k−3​ln2⁡(k/keq)P(k)\sim k^{-3}\ln^{2}(k/k_{\mathrm{eq}}).
- •

Fork∼0.1​h/Mpck\sim 0.1~\,h/\mathrm{Mpc}there are wiggles produced by baryon acoustic oscillations (BAO). Their typical frequency iskBAO=1/(100​Mpc/h)≃10−2​h/Mpck_{\mathrm{BAO}}=1/(100\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h)\simeq 10^{-2}\,h/\mathrm{Mpc}. The BAO is a relatively small feature∼5%\sim 5\%of the total power, but it is incredibly important for cosmological constraints.

The right panel of fig.1demonstrates the above high-kkand low-kkasymptotics.
This funny shape is the consequence of different dynamics of the dark matter field
during the radiation and matter domination. This is why it has a peak at the
equality scale, which is imprinted during the transition between the two epochs.Figure 2:Linear correlation functionξlin​(r)\xi_{\rm lin}(r)of the matter overdensity in position space. The bump atr≃100​Mpc/hr\simeq 100~\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h{}is the peak
of baryon acoustic oscillations (BAO).

Fig.2shows the linear correlation function (i.e. the Fourier transformed power spectrum). Crucially, one
notices that all the BAO wiggles combine into a position-space
peak aroundℓBAO=100​Mpc/h\ell_{\rm BAO}=100\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h. The BAO have a paramount
significance in cosmology since they provide a standard ruler,
which can be observed at different redshifts and thus used
to study the geometry of the Universe. The BAO also
exhibit a peculiar evolution in non-linear perturbation theory,
which we will discuss shortly. To facilitate this discussion,
it is useful to separate the total linear matter power spectrum
into the “wiggly” partPwP_{w}that carries the wiggles and the
“smooth,” i.e. “non-wiggly” partPsP_{s}, see Fig.3.
Such separation
is non-unique in practice since the slope of the smooth power
spectrum varies considerably over the range of the wiggles.
Nevertheless, it is a nice tool that neatly highlights the important
physics. Note that the “wiggly” power spectrum represents
only a small,∼5%\sim 5\%fraction of the total power.Figure 3:Left:the decomposition of the linear matter power spectrum into the “wiggly” and “smooth” components. The results are multiplied bykkto enhance the visibility of the wiggles.Right:the ratio of the wiggly to the
smooth power spectrum, which is about∼5%\sim 5\%.

The linear matter power spectrum inΛ\LambdaCDM has a very simple time-dependence:
for a redshiftzzit is simply the initial power spectrum rescaled by the growth factor squaredD+2​(z)D^{2}_{+}(z), as can be appreciated in the left panel of fig.1. This simplicity allows one to chooseδ0​(𝒌)\delta_{0}({\bm{k}})to be the linear matter
densityextrapolatedto redshift zero, in which case the growth factor now can be normalized
to unity,D+​(z=0)=1D_{+}(z=0)=1.Figure 4:Left:the growth factor as a function of redshiftzz. We show the results for the fiducialΛ\LambdaCDM cosmology and for a matter dominated Universe, whereD+=1/(1+z)D_{+}=1/(1+z).Right:the logarithmic growth factorf​(z)f(z)along with the approximate
function[Ωm​(z)]0.5[\Omega_{m}(z)]^{0.5}.

In a matter-dominated Universe (H2=8​π​G3​ρm​(t)H^{2}=\frac{8\pi G}{3}\,\rho_{m}(t)) the growth factor is simply given by the scale factora​(z)a(z)in the Friedman-Robertson-Walker metric, so we haveP11​(k,z)=P11​(k,z=0)​D+2​(z),D+​(z)=a​(z),a=11+z.P_{11}(k,z)=P_{11}(k,z\!=\!0)\;D_{+}^{2}(z)\,,\qquad D_{+}(z)=a(z)\,,\quad a=\frac{1}{1+z}\,.(24)

ForΩm<1\Omega_{m}<1, i.e. in the presence of dark energy,D+​(z)D_{+}(z)deviates froma​(z)a(z).
This is shown in the left panel of figure4. Atz≈2z\approx 2the two functions are very
different, which is a consequence of adopting the unit normalization atz=0z=0. The scale factor
grows with redshift much faster thanD+​(z)D_{+}(z), which reflects that dark energy slows down
the growth of structure.

Departures of the growth factor from the scale factor can be captured by thelogarithmicgrowth factorf=d​ln⁡D+/d​ln⁡af=d\ln D_{+}/d\ln a, see the right panel of figure4.f=1f=1during matter domination, and when dark energy kicks in, it decreases tof≈0.5f\approx 0.5atz=0z=0. Approximately, the logarithmic growth factor
behaves as[Ωm​(z)]0.5[\Omega_{m}(z)]^{0.5}, whereΩm​(z)\Omega_{m}(z)is the redshift-dependent abundance
of matter,Ωm​(z)≡Ωm​(1+z)3Ωm​(1+z)3+ΩΛ.\Omega_{m}(z)\equiv\frac{\Omega_{m}(1+z)^{3}}{\Omega_{m}(1+z)^{3}+\Omega_{\Lambda}}\,.(25)

One can see thatΩm​(z)→Ωm\Omega_{m}(z)\to\Omega_{m}atz=0z=0andΩm​(z)→1\Omega_{m}(z)\to 1asz→∞z\to\infty.

Due to a simple time-dependence of linear theory, in what follows we will often suppress the explicit time
dependence, assuming that unless stated otherwise,
everything is computed at redshift zero, or at a specified redshift of
interest.

## 2.2Useful Quantities

Useful quantities.Linear cosmological perturbation theory tells us a lot about the properties
of matter clustering in our Universe. Let’s introduce some useful quantities
that will prove instrumental in what follows.Figure 5:Left:The dimensionless power spectrumΔ2​(k,z)=P11​(k)​k3/(2​π2)\Delta^{2}(k,z)=P_{11}(k)\,k^{3}/(2\pi^{2})atz=0z=0(blue) andz=1z=1(orange).
The nonlinear scalekNL​(z)k_{\mathrm{NL}}(z)is defined byΔ2​(kNL)=1\Delta^{2}(k_{\mathrm{NL}})=1(black horizontal line); one findskNL≈0.25​h​Mpc−1k_{\mathrm{NL}}\approx 0.25\,h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}(atz=0z=0) andkNL≈0.75​h​Mpc−1k_{\mathrm{NL}}\approx 0.75\,h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}(atz=0z=0).
Fork≪kNLk\ll k_{\mathrm{NL}}the density field is perturbative
(Δ2≪1\Delta^{2}\ll 1).Right:The linear power spectrumP11​(k,z=0)P_{11}(k,z=0)computed
withclass(blue) compared to the power-law approximationP11∝k−1.5P_{11}\propto k^{-1.5}(orange), normalised to match atk=kNL=0.25​h/Mpck=k_{\mathrm{NL}}=0.25~\,h/\mathrm{Mpc}.
This scale-free approximation with effective slopeneff≈−1.5n_{\mathrm{eff}}\approx-1.5is used throughout these lectures for
power-counting estimates (e.g. to relate the time dependence of
counterterms tocs2∝D4/(3+n)c_{s}^{2}\propto D^{4/(3+n)}).
The approximation is accurate to order one in the range0.05≲k/(h​Mpc−1)≲0.30.05\lesssim k/(h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1})\lesssim 0.3relevant for
one-loop calculations.

a) Dimensionless power spectrum:Δ2​(k,z)=k32​π2​P​(k,z).\Delta^{2}(k,z)=\frac{k^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}\,P(k,z)\,.(26)

This measures the contribution of a logarithmic interval of modes around∼k\sim kto the total
variance of matter fluctuations:⟨δ2​(𝒙)⟩=ξ​(0)=12​π2​∫d​ln⁡k​Δ2​(k).\langle\delta^{2}({\bm{x}})\rangle=\xi(0)=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int d\ln k\;\Delta^{2}(k)\,.(27)

Roughly, the dimensional power spectrum tells how much a mode with wavenumberkkfluctuates
in position space.Δ2​(k,z=0)\Delta^{2}(k,z=0)in ourΛ\LambdaCDM model is shown in the left panel
of fig.5. It crosses unity on small scales, which means that the
matter fluctuations there exceed unity, which invalidates perturbation theory.
To quantify this better we introduce

b) Non-linear scale:kNLk_{\mathrm{NL}}is the momentum for which the fluctuations become𝒪​(1)\mathcal{O}(1), i.e.δ1∼1\delta_{1}\sim 1(perturbation theory breakdown):Δ2​(kNL)=1,i.e.kNL32​π2​P​(kNL)=1.\Delta^{2}(k_{\mathrm{NL}})=1\,,\qquad\text{i.e.}\quad\frac{k_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}\,P(k_{\mathrm{NL}})=1\,.(28)

For our fiducial PlanckΛ\LambdaCDM atz=0z=0:kNL≈0.25​h/Mpck_{\mathrm{NL}}\approx 0.25\,h/\mathrm{Mpc}. Note that the non-linear scale
increases at high redshift, i.e. atz=1z=1one findskNL≈0.74​h/Mpck_{\mathrm{NL}}\approx 0.74\,h/\mathrm{Mpc}. This makes sense:
matter fluctuations are smaller at high redshift and hence are more perturbative.

c) Power-law universe (PLU) approximation.For a narrow range of scales the power spectrum
can be well approximated as a power law,P11PLU​(k)≈2​π2kNL3​(kkNL)n.P_{11}^{\mathrm{PLU}}(k)\approx\frac{2\pi^{2}}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3}}\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{n}\,.(29)

Note that this approximation automatically satisfiesΔ11PLU=1\Delta_{11}^{\mathrm{PLU}}=1atk=kNLk=k_{\mathrm{NL}}. If we want to match the actual power spectrum in the range0.05≲k/(h​Mpc−1)≲0.30.05\lesssim k/(h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1})\lesssim 0.3we need to choose the following parameters:n≈−1.5​for​k∼0.1​h/Mpc,kNL=0.3​h/Mpc.\quad n\approx-1.5\;\;\text{for }k\sim 0.1\,h/\mathrm{Mpc}\,,\quad k_{\mathrm{NL}}=0.3\,h/\mathrm{Mpc}\,.(30)

Note thatkNLk_{\mathrm{NL}}normalized to match a particular region
ofkkmight be somewhat different from the actualkNLk_{\mathrm{NL}}because the effective spectral indexnnreduces at largerkk, producing a somewhat softer spectrum there. We will use the PLU spectrum
for order-of-magnitude estimates where this small discrepancy would not matter.Figure 6:Left:The root-mean-square density fluctuationσ​(R)=[∫d3​q(2​π)3​P11​(q)​W~R2​(q)]1/2\sigma(R)=\left[\int\frac{d^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,P_{11}(q)\,\tilde{W}_{R}^{2}(q)\right]^{1/2}as a function of the smoothing scaleRRatz=0z=0, using a top-hat window function. The familiar normalisation
conventionσ8≡σ​(R=8​h−1​Mpc)≈0.8\sigma_{8}\equiv\sigma(R=8\,h^{-1}\mathrm{Mpc})\approx 0.8is visible atR=8​h−1​MpcR=8\,h^{-1}\mathrm{Mpc}.
AsR→0R\to 0,σ​(R)\sigma(R)diverges, reflecting the UV sensitivity of
the unsmoothed variance — precisely the divergence that the EFT
counterterms are designed to absorb.Right:The dimensionless power spectrumΔ2​(k)=P​(k)​k3/(2​π2)\Delta^{2}(k)=P(k)\,k^{3}/(2\pi^{2})atz=0z=0for three cases:
the full linear spectrumP11P_{11}fromclass(blue),
and the smoothed spectrumP11​(k)​W~R2​(k)P_{11}(k)\,\tilde{W}_{R}^{2}(k)with a top-hat window atR=8​h−1​MpcR=8\,h^{-1}\mathrm{Mpc}(green),
and the smoothed
power-law approximationPPLU∝k−1.5P_{\mathrm{PLU}}\propto k^{-1.5}(orange).
The window function suppresses power on scalesk≳1/Rk\gtrsim 1/R,
illustrating how spatial smoothing acts as a UV cutoff.
The oscillatory features in the green curve are characteristic zeros
of the Fourier-space top-hat windowW~R​(k)=3​[sin⁡(k​R)−k​R​cos⁡(k​R)]/(k​R)3\tilde{W}_{R}(k)=3[\sin(kR)-kR\cos(kR)]/(kR)^{3}.

d) Mass variance:⟨δ2​(𝒙)⟩=ξ​(0)=12​π2​∫d​ln⁡q​Δ2​(q)∝limq→∞ln3⁡(q)→∞,\langle\delta^{2}({\bm{x}})\rangle=\xi(0)=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int d\ln q\;\Delta^{2}(q)\propto\lim_{q\to\infty}\ln^{3}(q)\to\infty\,,(31)

where in the last limit we took into account that the integrandΔ2​(q)∝ln2⁡(q)\Delta^{2}(q)\propto\ln^{2}(q)logarithmically
diverges in the UV.

We have already seen the above quantity.ξ​(0)\xi(0)measures a variance of density fluctuations
at the same point, which can be used to estimate a typical amplitude of matter fluctuations. The problem
is that this quantity diverges if we include contributions from arbitrarily
small-scale (short wavelength) modes. In the particle physics speak
one says the mass variance diverges in the UV. A simple fix is to cut this off at∼kNL\sim k_{\mathrm{NL}}, where linear theory breaks down.
More systematically, one cansmooth the density field, say using a spherical top-hat
filter,WR​(𝒙)=34​π​R3​ΘH​(R−|𝒙|),W_{R}({\bm{x}})=\frac{3}{4\pi R^{3}}\;\Theta_{H}(R-|{\bm{x}}|)\,,(32)

whereΘH\Theta_{H}is the Heaviside step function. The smoothed fieldδR\delta_{R}δR​(𝒙)=∫d3​y​δ​(𝒙+𝒚)​WR​(𝒚)\delta_{R}({\bm{x}})=\int d^{3}y\;\delta({\bm{x}}+{\bm{y}})\,W_{R}({\bm{y}})\,(33)

does not have fluctuations on scales smaller
thanRR.
In Fourier space we haveδR​(𝒌)=δ​(𝒌)​W~R​(k​R)\delta_{R}({\bm{k}})=\delta({\bm{k}})\,\widetilde{W}_{R}(kR)withW~R=3​j1​(k​R)k​R≡3​sin⁡(k​R)−k​R​cos⁡(k​R)(k​R)3.\widetilde{W}_{R}=\frac{3\,j_{1}(kR)}{kR}\equiv 3\frac{\sin(kR)-kR\cos(kR)}{(kR)^{3}}\,.(34)

The smoothed variance reads:σ2​(R)≡⟨δR2​(𝒙)⟩=12​π2​∫d​ln⁡k​k3​P11​|W~R|2.\sigma^{2}(R)\equiv\langle\delta_{R}^{2}({\bm{x}})\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int d\ln k\;k^{3}\,P_{11}\,|\widetilde{W}_{R}|^{2}\,.(35)

The r.m.s.σ​(R)\sigma(R)is shown in the left panel of fig.6. We see thatσ​(R)<1\sigma(R)<1forR≲5​Mpc/hR\lesssim 5~\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h. As long as we are doing the calculations
for distances larger than this, perturbation theory should work.
Finally, a familiar choice from textbook cosmology is to pickR=8​h/MpcR=8~\,h/\mathrm{Mpc},

Example:σ​(R=8)≈0.8\sigma(R\!=\!8)\approx 0.8,σ2≈0.64\sigma^{2}\approx 0.64→\tosmall-ish. Next-to-leading order:σ4≈0.41\sigma^{4}\approx 0.41.

It is also instructive to take a look
at the integrandk3​P11​|W~R|2k^{3}\,P_{11}\,|\widetilde{W}_{R}|^{2}, shown in the right panel of fig.6forR=8​h/MpcR=8~\,h/\mathrm{Mpc}.
First, we see
that the low-pass filterWRW_{R}indeed suppresses the small scale power so that we get a finite
answer for the mass variance.
Second, numerically, the integrand peaks atk≈1.22/R=π/(2​R)k\approx 1.22/R=\pi/(2R), which tells us how to
convert position space distances to the Fourier space wavenumbers.
In addition, and more importantly, because of this sharp peak around1.22/R1.22/R,
we see that even the very crude PLU actually provides a very good approximation to the smoothed density variance integrand, which can be contrasted with the broadband power in fig.5.

e) Displacement field.It is useful to know how much a typical matter particle moves during the age of the Universe.
Since we have a huge number of particles, they form a fluid with a continuous density,
so it makes sense to talk about displacement field as opposed to displacements
of individual particles.
In conformal timeτ\tau, the comoving displacement of a fluid element with the initial position𝒙ini{\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{ini}}is defined as𝚿=𝒙−𝒙ini=∫τiniτ𝑑τ1​𝒗​(𝒙ini,τ1),{\bm{\Psi}}={\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{ini}}=\int_{\tau_{\mathrm{ini}}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\;{\bm{v}}({\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{ini}},\tau_{1})\,,(36)

where𝒗​(𝒙ini,τ1){\bm{v}}({\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{ini}},\tau_{1})is the velocity of the fluid element
with the initial position𝒙ini{\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{ini}}along its trajectory parameterized by
conformal
timeτ1\tau_{1}.

In linear theory for the adiabatic mode initial conditions
the velocity is curl-free, and its divergence is proportional to the density field by the continuity equation,
i.e. we havevi=−a​H​f​∂iδΔ,𝒗​(𝒌)=a​H​f​D+​i​𝒌k2​δ0​(𝒌)=a​H​f​i​𝒌k2​δ1​(τ,𝒌),v^{i}=-a\,H\,f\;\frac{\partial^{i}\delta}{\Delta}\,,\qquad{\bm{v}}({\bm{k}})=aHf\,D_{+}\frac{i{\bm{k}}}{k^{2}}\;\delta_{0}({\bm{k}})=aHf\,\frac{i{\bm{k}}}{k^{2}}\;\delta_{1}(\tau,{\bm{k}})\,,(37)

(note the appearance of theconformalHubble parameterℋ=∂τa/a≡a​H\mathcal{H}=\partial_{\tau}a/a\equiv aH),
which implies𝚿(𝒌)=i​𝒌k2δ1(τ,𝒌).\boxed{{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{k}})=\frac{i{\bm{k}}}{k^{2}}\,\delta_{1}(\tau,{\bm{k}})\,.}(38)

From this we compute the displacement variance:⟨Ψi​(𝒙)​Ψj​(𝒙)⟩=δi​j3​∫P11​(k)k2​d3​k(2​π)3=δi​j6​π2​∫d​ln⁡k​k​P11​(k)≡δi​j​σd2,\langle\Psi_{i}({\bm{x}})\,\Psi_{j}({\bm{x}})\rangle=\frac{\delta_{ij}}{3}\int\frac{P_{11}(k)}{k^{2}}\;\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}=\frac{\delta_{ij}}{6\pi^{2}}\int d\ln k\;k\,P_{11}(k)\equiv\delta_{ij}\,\sigma_{d}^{2}\,,(39)

where we introduced the 1D displacement varianceσd2≡16​π2​∫d​ln⁡k​k​P11​(k).\sigma_{d}^{2}\equiv\frac{1}{6\pi^{2}}\int d\ln k\;k\,P_{11}(k)~\,.(40)

Since the integrandk​P11​(k)∝k−2​ln2⁡kk\,P_{11}(k)\propto k^{-2}\ln^{2}kask→∞k\to\infty,σd2\sigma_{d}^{2}converges in the UV.
Atz=0z=0one findsσd2≈34​(Mpc/h)2\sigma_{d}^{2}\approx 34\;(\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h)^{2}.
Can we trust this result? No, because
the integration abovekNLk_{\mathrm{NL}}is not legitimate.
Instead, we should stop aroundkNLk_{\mathrm{NL}}, which gives us an estimate16​π2​∫0kNLd​ln⁡k​k​P11​(k)=29​(Mpc/h)2.\frac{1}{6\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}d\ln k\;k\,P_{11}(k)=29\;(\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h)^{2}\,.(41)

From the 1D variance we can estimate a typical 3D r.m.s. displacement as⟨|Δ​x|2⟩=⟨Ψi​Ψi⟩=3​σd2\langle|\Delta x|^{2}\rangle=\langle\Psi^{i}\Psi_{i}\rangle=3\,\sigma_{d}^{2}, implyingΔ​x∼3​σd∼9.3​Mpc/h\Delta x\sim\sqrt{3}\,\sigma_{d}\sim 9.3~\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h. Thus
a typical matter particle travels by about10​Mpc/h10~\,\mathrm{Mpc}/hduring the age of the Universe.

Note that the displacement variance is closely related to the
velocity dispersion:⟨vi​vj⟩=ℋ2​f2​δi​j​σd2.\langle v_{i}v_{j}\rangle=\mathcal{H}^{2}f^{2}\,\delta_{ij}\,\sigma_{d}^{2}\,.(42)

## 2.3Gaussian Random Fields and the Wick theorem

The final important ingredient of our lectures is the Wick theorem.
Observational data suggests that the initial conditions in our
Universe are Gaussian, i.e.δ1\delta_{1}is aGaussian random field. This implies that all odd-point
correlation functions of this field vanish, while the even-point correlation
functions can be expressed through the two point functions. At the lowest orders we have:⟨δ1​(𝒌1)​δ1​(𝒌2)⟩\displaystyle\langle\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{1})\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{2})\rangle=(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌1+𝒌2)​P11​(k1),\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}_{1}+{\bm{k}}_{2})\;P_{11}(k_{1})\,,(43)⟨δ1​δ1​δ1⟩\displaystyle\langle\delta_{1}\delta_{1}\delta_{1}\rangle=0.\displaystyle=0\,.(44)

Wick’s theoremprecisely tells us how to compute higher order correlators.
In general, an even2​N2N-point correlator of a Gaussian random fieldϕ\phiis given by⟨ϕi1​⋯​ϕi2​N⟩=∑ordered pairings​Pi∏pairs​i​jin​PiCi​j,Ci​j=⟨ϕi​ϕj⟩.\langle\phi_{i_{1}}\cdots\phi_{i_{2N}}\rangle=\sum_{\text{ordered pairings }P_{i}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{pairs }ij\\
\text{in }P_{i}\end{subarray}}C_{ij}\,,\qquad C_{ij}=\langle\phi_{i}\,\phi_{j}\rangle\,.(45)

A derivation of this theorem from the Gaussian generating functional
is given in AppendixA.
In the cosmological context we have:⟨δ1​(𝒌1)​δ1​(𝒌2)​δ1​(𝒌3)​δ1​(𝒌4)⟩=⟨δ1​(𝒌1)​δ1​(𝒌2)​δ1​(𝒌3)​δ1​(𝒌4)⟩+⟨δ1​(𝒌1)​δ1​(𝒌2)​δ1​(𝒌3)​δ1​(𝒌4)⟩+⟨δ1​(𝒌1)​δ1​(𝒌2)​δ1​(𝒌3)​δ1​(𝒌4)⟩,\begin{split}&\langle\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{1})\,\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{2})\,\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{3})\,\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{4})\rangle\;=\;\big\langle\hbox to114.22pt{\vbox to20.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 57.11118pt\lower-5.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\pgfsys@beginscope\pgfsys@invoke{ }\definecolor{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,0}\pgfsys@color@rgb@stroke{0}{0}{0}\pgfsys@invoke{ }\pgfsys@color@rgb@fill{0}{0}{0}\pgfsys@invoke{ }\pgfsys@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\pgfsys@invoke{ }\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\pgfsys@beginscope\pgfsys@invoke{ }

where we explicitly show different pairings that contract two fields
into a power spectrum, yielding=(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌1+𝒌2)​P11​(k1)×(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌3+𝒌4)​P11​(k3)\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}_{1}+{\bm{k}}_{2})\;P_{11}(k_{1})\times(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}_{3}+{\bm{k}}_{4})\;P_{11}(k_{3})+(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌1+𝒌3)​P11​(k1)×(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌2+𝒌4)​P11​(k2)\displaystyle\quad+(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}_{1}+{\bm{k}}_{3})\;P_{11}(k_{1})\times(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}_{2}+{\bm{k}}_{4})\;P_{11}(k_{2})+(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌1+𝒌4)​P11​(k1)×(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌2+𝒌3)​P11​(k2).\displaystyle\quad+(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}_{1}+{\bm{k}}_{4})\;P_{11}(k_{1})\times(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}_{2}+{\bm{k}}_{3})\;P_{11}(k_{2})\,.(47)

## 3Chapter 3: EFT of Dark Matter Clustering

After this solid build-up, we are ready to develop non-linear perturbation theory
for matter clustering. As before, we are working in the approximation
where dark matter and baryons form a single fluid (total matter).
In this approximation there is no distinction between total matter and dark matter,
so we will use these terms interchangeably.

## 3.1Equations of Motion

Let’s derive the equation of motion for matter fluctuations.
We will follow the symmetry-based derivation of fluid equations from Chapter 1.
On scales much inside the horizon (k/a≫Hk/a\gg H) we can work in the regime ofNewtonian cosmology.
In physical coordinates and time, matter denvity and velocity fields satisfy thecontinuity equation:∂ρ∂t+∇𝒓⋅(ρ​𝑽)=0,\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}+\nabla_{\bm{r}}\cdot(\rho\,{\bm{V}})=0~\,,(48)

the Euler equation:ρ​D​ViD​t=−ρ​∂∂ri​Φ+∂∂rj​τji,\rho\;\frac{DV^{i}}{Dt}=-\rho\,\frac{\partial}{\partial r^{i}}\Phi+\frac{\partial}{\partial r^{j}}\tau^{i}_{~j}\,,(49)

whereDD​t=∂∂t|𝒓+Vj​∂∂rj\frac{D}{Dt}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigg|_{{\bm{r}}}+V^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial r^{j}}(50)

is the material (convective) derivative andτji\tau^{i}_{~j}is a general stress-energy tensor. Gravity is captured by thePoisson equation:∇𝒓2Φ=4​π​G​∑a(ρa+3​pa)=4​π​G​ρ−Λ,\nabla_{\bm{r}}^{2}\Phi=4\pi G\sum_{a}\left(\rho_{a}+3p_{a}\right)=4\pi G\rho-\Lambda\,,(51)

where the sum runs over all components of the Universe, andρa+3​pa\rho_{a}+3p_{a}is the the
correct source of the Newtonian potential in the
appropriate (sub-horizon) limit
of general relativity. For non-relativistic matterp=0p=0and one recovers the
usual source4​π​G​ρ4\pi G\rho; a cosmological constant,pΛ=−ρΛ=−Λ/8​π​Gp_{\Lambda}=-\rho_{\Lambda}=-\Lambda/8\pi G, contributes the constant−Λ-\Lambdadisplayed in the second equality. The crucial point for
what follows is that at late times all components other than matter
aresmooth: they affect the background expansion, but carry
no fluctuations.
In what follows we will use
conformal timeτ\tau:a​d​τ=d​ta\,d\tau=dt, and comoving coordinatesxix^{i}defined by the Friedmann metricd​s2=−d​t2+a2​(t)​d​𝒙2=a2​(−d​τ2+d​𝒙2),ds^{2}=-dt^{2}+a^{2}(t)\,d{\bm{x}}^{\,2}=a^{2}\!\left(-d\tau^{2}+d{\bm{x}}^{\,2}\right)\,,(52)

i.e.𝒓=a​(τ)​𝒙{\bm{r}}=a(\tau)\,{\bm{x}}. We will keep using the conformal Hubble parameterℋ≡a′/a\mathcal{H}\equiv a^{\prime}/a, with primes denotingd/d​τ\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}\tau.
The usual dots will denote the derivatives w.r.t. cosmic timett.
It is
convenient to express the peculiar velocity in terms of conformal-time
derivatives.
Sinced​t=a​d​τ\mathrm{d}t=a\,\mathrm{d}\tau, one has𝒙˙=𝒙′/a\dot{\bm{x}}={\bm{x}}^{\prime}/a, so that𝒗=𝒙′{\bm{v}}={\bm{x}}^{\prime}. The peculiar velocity in
our convention therefore has dimensions of velocity (comoving
displacement per conformal time).
The gradient operators are related by∇𝒓=a−1​∇𝒙\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{r}}}=a^{-1}\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{x}}}, and a time derivative at fixed𝒓{\bm{r}}transforms as∂∂t|𝒓=1a​∂∂τ|𝒙−H​𝒙⋅∇𝒙,\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bigg|_{{\bm{r}}}=\frac{1}{a}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\bigg|_{{\bm{x}}}-H\,{\bm{x}}\cdot\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{x}}}\,,(53)

where the second term accounts for the fact that a point of fixed𝒙{\bm{x}}corresponds to a time-dependent𝒓{\bm{r}}.

We decompose now the velocity, density, and gravitational potentials into the background
and fluctuations as𝑽=H​𝒓+𝒗​(𝒙,τ),ρ=ρ¯​(1+δ​(𝒙,τ)),Φ=−12​a¨​a​|𝒙|2+ϕ​(𝒙,τ).\begin{split}&{\bm{V}}=H{\bm{r}}+{\bm{v}}({\bm{x}},\tau)\,,\quad\rho=\bar{\rho}\,(1+\delta({\bm{x}},\tau))\,,\quad\Phi=-\frac{1}{2}\ddot{a}a|{\bm{x}}|^{2}+\phi({\bm{x}},\tau)\,.\end{split}(54)

The background density satisfiesρ¯˙+3​H​ρ¯=0\dot{\bar{\rho}}+3H\bar{\rho}=0.
The full velocity can be decomposed into the Hubble flow++peculiar velocity via𝑽=H​𝒓+𝒗{\bm{V}}=H{\bm{r}}+{\bm{v}}. Note that this is always possible to do
by means of simple ensemble averaging.

It is straighforward to show that the perturbations satisfy the following
equations (comoving coordinates, conformal
time):Perturbed continuity equation:∂δ∂τ+∂i[(1+δ)vi]=0,\boxed{\dfrac{\partial\delta}{\partial\tau}+\partial_{i}\!\left[(1+\delta)\,v^{i}\right]=0\,,}(55)Perturbed Euler equation:∂vi∂τ+ℋvi+vj∂jvi=−∂iϕ+1ρ∂jτi,j\boxed{\dfrac{\partial v^{i}}{\partial\tau}+\mathcal{H}\,v^{i}+v^{j}\partial_{j}v^{i}=-\partial_{i}\phi+\frac{1}{\rho}\,\partial_{j}\tau^{i}{}_{j}\,,}(56)Perturbed Poisson equation:∇x2ϕ=32ℋ2Ωm(τ)δ.\boxed{\nabla^{2}_{x}\phi=\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\Omega_{m}(\tau)\delta\,.}(57)

When the physical stress-tensor is set to zero,τi​j=0\tau_{ij}=0, the above equations
are simply equations for pressureless perfect fluid
in cosmological perturbation theory.
These equations are appropriate for a fluid of non-relativistic
non-interacting or weakly interacting particles, like dark matter.
That’s why histrionically, people assumed that this was the equation that describes
structure formation in our Universe.

Our Universe, however, looks very different from a fluid of free
dark matter particles. We know that most of dark matter actually resides in bound structures
such as dark matter halos, so the actual small-scale behavior of the matter fluid
is strongly influenced by these non-linear bound objects. It is not obvious then
thatτi​j=0\tau_{ij}=0for a gas of dark matter halos. In fact it’s not.
In addition, dark matter in our Universe can be described by scenarios
different from that of non-relativistic collisionless particles. For instance,
dark matter can be an axion-like field with a quantum pressure,
which then will have a non-zeroτi​j\tau_{ij}even if we ignore
the halos. But even in the absence of these physical scenarios,
simply from symmetries and dimensional analysis (EFT–!) we must conclude
that the most general description
must involve a non-zeroτi​j\tau_{ij}.

One can also obtain justification in favor of non-zeroτi​j\tau_{ij}from the UV point of view. This is the approach adopted in the original EFT papers[4,5]. If we assume that
dark matter is just a collection of non-relativistic particles,
we’d get a velocity dispersion⟨vi​vj⟩−⟨vi⟩​⟨vj⟩\langle v_{i}v_{j}\rangle-\langle v_{i}\rangle\langle v_{j}\ranglein the right hand side of
the Euler equation following from
the taking the moments of the Boltzmann equation for the DM particles. Even if we start from
collisionless CDM particles with well defined velocities, a dispersion
will be generated dynamically through orbit crossing (also known as shell-crossing).
Alternatively, one could start from the collisionless Boltzmann equation, coarse-grain
it over non-linear small scale modes, take moments, and end up with a nonzeroτi​j\tau_{ij}in the r.h.s. of the Euler equation. In either caseτi​j\tau_{ij}is interpreted
as an effective stress-tensor generated by non-linear dynamics.

Historically, people started building the non-linear theory for matter clustering assumingτi​j=0\tau_{ij}=0.
This happened to be a good starting point because the contribution ofτi​j\tau_{ij}is suppressed by gradients, just like in the case of usual fluids.
This approximation went down in the literature as “standard perturbation theory” (SPT)[6].
SPT represents the leading order approximation, while the terms coming fromτi​j\tau_{ij}capture a non-trivial gradient expansion.
These terms are often called “EFT corrections,”
because they appear when you start taking dimensional analysis and
symmetries seriously.
Thus, SPT can be thought of as an analog of the“monopole”approximation, while the gradient contributions can be thought of as higher order moments, i.e.dipole, etc.Schematically we have:∂vi∂τ+ℋ​vi+vj​∂jvi+∂iΦ⏟“monopole,” SPT=∂jτji⏟“dipole+higher orders,” EFT.\underbrace{\frac{\partial v^{i}}{\partial\tau}+\mathcal{H}v^{i}+v^{j}\partial_{j}v^{i}+\partial^{i}\Phi}_{\text{``monopole,'' SPT}}=\quad\underbrace{\partial^{j}\tau^{i}_{~j}}_{\text{``dipole+higher orders,'' EFT}}\,.(58)

Let us start with the “monopole” approximation. Unlike the monopole of
electrostatic, it captures a highly non-trivial and rich dynamics. Importantly,
unlike the case of the perfect fluid, we will see that this dynamics willrequireincluding the higher order EFT corrections. So let’s start with SPT.

## 3.2Standard Perturbation Theory (SPT)

To simplify our life going forward, let’s introduce a new time variable:η=ln⁡D+,(D+=eη).\eta=\ln D_{+}\,,\quad(D_{+}=e^{\eta})\,.(59)

In addition, let’s use a new variable for the velocity divergence:Θ=−∂ivif​ℋ.\Theta=-\frac{\partial_{i}v^{i}}{f\mathcal{H}}\,.(60)

Transforming eqs. (55,56,57)
into
Fourier space and using the Poisson equation to solve forϕ\phi(and droppingτi​j\tau_{ij})
we get a closed system of equations forδ\deltaandΘ\Thetaonly:∂ηδ𝒌−Θ𝒌\displaystyle\partial_{\eta}\delta_{{\bm{k}}}-\Theta_{{\bm{k}}}=∫𝒒1∫𝒒2(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌−𝒒12)​α​(𝒒1,𝒒2)​Θ𝒒1​δ𝒒2,\displaystyle=\int_{{\bm{q}}_{1}}\int_{{\bm{q}}_{2}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}_{12})\;\alpha({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})\;\Theta_{{\bm{q}}_{1}}\,\delta_{{\bm{q}}_{2}}\,,(61)∂ηΘ𝒌−3​Ωm2​f2​δ𝒌+(3​Ωm2​f2−1)​Θ𝒌\displaystyle\partial_{\eta}\Theta_{{\bm{k}}}-\frac{3\Omega_{m}}{2f^{2}}\,\delta_{{\bm{k}}}+\left(\frac{3\Omega_{m}}{2f^{2}}-1\right)\Theta_{{\bm{k}}}=∫𝒒1∫𝒒2(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌−𝒒12)​β​(𝒒1,𝒒2)​Θ𝒒1​Θ𝒒2,\displaystyle=\int_{{\bm{q}}_{1}}\int_{{\bm{q}}_{2}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}_{12})\;\beta({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})\;\Theta_{{\bm{q}}_{1}}\,\Theta_{{\bm{q}}_{2}}\,,(62)

with the coupling kernels:α​(𝒒1,𝒒2)=𝒒12⋅𝒒1q12,β​(𝒒1,𝒒2)=q122​(𝒒1⋅𝒒2)2​q12​q22.\alpha({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})=\frac{{\bm{q}}_{12}\cdot{\bm{q}}_{1}}{q_{1}^{2}}\,,\qquad\beta({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})=\frac{q_{12}^{2}\;({\bm{q}}_{1}\cdot{\bm{q}}_{2})}{2\,q_{1}^{2}\,q_{2}^{2}}\,.(63)

The presence of time-dependent coefficients∝Ωmf2\propto\frac{\Omega_{m}}{f^{2}}makes life
somewhat hard for us. The equation simplifies in the twocases:
- 1.

Ωm=1\Omega_{m}=1,f=1f=1: matter-dominated universe (MDU), also known in the general cosmology literature as an Einstein–de Sitter (EdS) Universe. Since we know we have a significant fraction
of dark energy in our Universe, it would be a terrible approximation if used naively.
- 2.

Ωm=f2≠1\Omega_{m}=f^{2}\neq 1: EdS in LSS. We’ve seen in the previous section
that this is a relatively good approximation even in the presence of dark energy. This is what in the LSS literature called the EdS approximaiton because
effectively it produces the same answer asΩm=1\Omega_{m}=1,f=1f=1. In this case time & space dependence factorize.

We will use the latter approximation in what follows.
It is easy to see now that the linearized EdS equations∂ηδ𝒌−Θ𝒌\displaystyle\partial_{\eta}\delta_{{\bm{k}}}-\Theta_{{\bm{k}}}=0,\displaystyle=0\,,(64)∂ηΘ𝒌−32​δ𝒌+12​Θ𝒌\displaystyle\partial_{\eta}\Theta_{{\bm{k}}}-\frac{3}{2}\,\delta_{{\bm{k}}}+\frac{1}{2}\Theta_{{\bm{k}}}=0,\displaystyle=0\,,(65)

supplemented with the initial conditionΘ=δ=eη​δ0,a​sη→−∞,\Theta=\delta=e^{\eta}\delta_{0}\,,\quad as\quad\eta\to-\infty\,,(66)

easily recovers the linear theory adiabatic mode solution:δ1=D+​δ0;Θ1=δ1;vi=−f​ℋ​∂iδΔ.\delta_{1}=D_{+}\,\delta_{0}\,;\;\Theta_{1}=\delta_{1}\,;\;v^{i}=-f\mathcal{H}\;\dfrac{\partial^{i}\delta}{\Delta}\,.(67)

Note that one can also easily derive the equation for the curl part the velocity field𝝎=∇×𝒗{\bm{\omega}}=\nabla\times{\bm{v}}:∂η𝝎+1f​𝝎=0.\partial_{\eta}{\bm{\omega}}+\frac{1}{f}{\bm{\omega}}=0\,.(68)

In SPT the curl decays as∝1/a\propto 1/asince it does not have a source. The gravitational potential is scalar so it supports
only gradient modes.
The situation, however, is different if we includeτi​j\tau_{ij}, which
generates voracity at the non-linear level[7,8]. This is, however, a higher order effect irrelevant for
our lectures.
We can focus on the scalar componentΘ\Thetaand develop a general perturbative solution to the non-linear equations.

Perturbative expansion.It turns out that the following expansion solves the SPT equations
perturbatively:δ=D+​δ0+D+2​(τ)​∫d3​q​F2​(𝒌−𝒒,𝒒)​(2​π)3​δ0​(𝒌−𝒒)​δ0​(𝒒)++D+3​(τ)​∫d3​q1​d3​q2​d3​q3​δD(3)​(𝒌−𝒒123)​F3​(𝒒1,𝒒2,𝒒3)​(2​π)3​δ0​(𝒒1)​δ0​(𝒒2)​δ0​(𝒒3)+⋯Θ=D+​δ0+D+2​(τ)​∫d3​q​G2​(𝒌−𝒒,𝒒)​(2​π)3​δ0​(𝒌−𝒒)​δ0​(𝒒)++D+3​(τ)​∫d3​q1​d3​q2​d3​q3​δD(3)​(𝒌−𝒒123)​G3​(𝒒1,𝒒2,𝒒3)​(2​π)3​δ0​(𝒒1)​δ0​(𝒒2)​δ0​(𝒒3)+⋯\begin{split}&\delta=D_{+}\,\delta_{0}+D_{+}^{2}(\tau)\int d^{3}q\;F_{2}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}})\;(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{0}{}({{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}})\,\delta_{0}({{\bm{q}}})+\;\\
&+D_{+}^{3}(\tau)\int d^{3}q_{1}\,d^{3}q_{2}\,d^{3}q_{3}\;\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}_{123})F_{3}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2},{\bm{q}}_{3})\;(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{0}({\bm{q}}_{1})\delta_{0}({\bm{q}}_{2})\delta_{0}({\bm{q}}_{3})+\cdots\\
&\Theta=D_{+}\,\delta_{0}+D_{+}^{2}(\tau)\int d^{3}q\;G_{2}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}})\;(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{0}({{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}})\,\delta_{0}({{\bm{q}}})+\;\\
&+D_{+}^{3}(\tau)\int d^{3}q_{1}\,d^{3}q_{2}\,d^{3}q_{3}\;\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}_{123})G_{3}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2},{\bm{q}}_{3})\;(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{0}({\bm{q}}_{1})\delta_{0}({\bm{q}}_{2})\delta_{0}({\bm{q}}_{3})+\cdots\end{split}(69)

the the kernelsFnF_{n},GnG_{n}can be recursively
defined by plugging the above ansatz into eqs. (61)–(62), with the result:Fn​(𝒒1,…​𝒒n)=∑m−1n−1Gm​(𝒒1,…,𝒒m)(2​n+3)​(n−1)[(2n+1)α(𝒒1​m,𝒒(m+1)​…​n)Fn−m(𝒒m+1,…𝒒n)+2β(𝒒1​m,𝒒(m+1)​n)Gn−m(𝒒m+1,…𝒒n)],Gn​(𝒒1,…​𝒒n)=∑m−1n−1Gm​(𝒒1,…,𝒒m)(2​n+3)​(n−1)[3α(𝒒1​m,𝒒(m+1)​…​n)Fn−m(𝒒m+1,…,𝒒n)+2nβ(𝒒1​m,𝒒(m+1)​…​n)Gn−m(𝒒m+1,…,𝒒n)],\begin{split}F_{n}({\bm{q}}_{1},...{\bm{q}}_{n})=&\sum_{m-1}^{n-1}\frac{G_{m}({\bm{q}}_{1},...,{\bm{q}}_{m})}{(2n+3)(n-1)}[(2n+1)\alpha({\bm{q}}_{1m},{\bm{q}}_{(m+1)...n})F_{n-m}({\bm{q}}_{m+1},...{\bm{q}}_{n})\\
&+2\beta({\bm{q}}_{1m},{\bm{q}}_{(m+1)n})G_{n-m}({\bm{q}}_{m+1},...{\bm{q}}_{n})]\,,\\
G_{n}({\bm{q}}_{1},...{\bm{q}}_{n})=&\sum_{m-1}^{n-1}\frac{G_{m}({\bm{q}}_{1},...,{\bm{q}}_{m})}{(2n+3)(n-1)}[3\alpha({\bm{q}}_{1m},{\bm{q}}_{(m+1)...n})F_{n-m}({\bm{q}}_{m+1},...,{\bm{q}}_{n})\\
&+2n\beta({\bm{q}}_{1m},{\bm{q}}_{(m+1)...n})G_{n-m}({\bm{q}}_{m+1},...,{\bm{q}}_{n})]\,,\end{split}(70)

andF1=G1=1F_{1}=G_{1}=1(recovering the linear growing mode).
In particular, at first non-trivial order we findF2​(𝒒1,𝒒2)=57+(𝒒1⋅𝒒2)q1​q2​(q1q2+q2q1)+27​(𝒒1⋅𝒒2)2q12​q22,G2​(𝒒1,𝒒2)=37+(𝒒1⋅𝒒2)q1​q2​(q1q2+q2q1)+47​(𝒒1⋅𝒒2)2q12​q22.\begin{split}F_{2}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})=\frac{5}{7}+\frac{({\bm{q}}_{1}\cdot{\bm{q}}_{2})}{q_{1}q_{2}}\left(\frac{q_{1}}{q_{2}}+\frac{q_{2}}{q_{1}}\right)+\frac{2}{7}\frac{({\bm{q}}_{1}\cdot{\bm{q}}_{2})^{2}}{q_{1}^{2}q_{2}^{2}}\,,\\
G_{2}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})=\frac{3}{7}+\frac{({\bm{q}}_{1}\cdot{\bm{q}}_{2})}{q_{1}q_{2}}\left(\frac{q_{1}}{q_{2}}+\frac{q_{2}}{q_{1}}\right)+\frac{4}{7}\frac{({\bm{q}}_{1}\cdot{\bm{q}}_{2})^{2}}{q_{1}^{2}q_{2}^{2}}\,.\end{split}(71)

Note that all kernels by default are symmetrized w.r.t. their arguments.
An instructive alternative derivation ofF2F_{2}andG2G_{2}, based on the
retarded Green’s functions of the fluid equations in the variableη\eta, is presented in AppendixB.
Another useful way to write down the above expansion isδ=∑n=1δ(n),Θ=∑n=1Θ(n),\delta=\sum_{n=1}\delta^{(n)}\,,\quad\Theta=\sum_{n=1}\Theta^{(n)}\,,(72)

whereδ(n)\delta^{(n)}terms explicitly absorb the time dependence:δ(n)​(𝒌,a)\displaystyle\delta^{(n)}({\bm{k}},a)=∫d3​q1(2​π)3​⋯​d3​qn(2​π)3​(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌−𝒌1​⋯​n)​Fn​(𝒌1,…,𝒌n)​δ1​(𝒌1)​⋯​δ1​(𝒌n),\displaystyle=\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\cdots\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q_{n}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{k}}_{1\cdots n})\;F_{n}({\bm{k}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{k}}_{n})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{1})\cdots\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{n})\,,(73)Θ(n)​(𝒌,a)\displaystyle\Theta^{(n)}({\bm{k}},a)=∫d3​q1(2​π)3​⋯​d3​qn(2​π)3​(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌−𝒌1​⋯​n)​Gn​(𝒌1,…,𝒌n)​δ1​(𝒌1)​⋯​δ1​(𝒌n),\displaystyle=\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\cdots\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q_{n}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{k}}_{1\cdots n})\;G_{n}({\bm{k}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{k}}_{n})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{1})\cdots\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{n})\,,(74)

with the identificationδ(1)=δ1\delta^{(1)}=\delta_{1}.

We can see that the time dependence of the solution
factorizes in front of each term in the series
thanks to the EdS approximation.
Using𝒒1⋅𝒒2=q1​q2​cos⁡θq1​q2{\bm{q}}_{1}\cdot{\bm{q}}_{2}=q_{1}q_{2}\cos\theta_{q_{1}q_{2}}we observe a re-appearance of the
gravitational
multipole expansion in theF2F_{2}kernel. In particular we see:
- •

5/75/7: monopole,
- •

terms∝cos⁡θ\propto\cos\theta: dipole,
- •

term∝cos2⁡θ\propto\cos^{2}\theta: quadrupole.

Einstein–de Sitter approximation in LSS:The coefficients5/75/7,1/21/2, and2/72/7etc. are specific to the EdS approximation.
In general they will be time-dependent functions.
In the LSS context the term “EdS” approximation refers to the procedure when one solves
the equations of motion perturbatively, as above, and then replacesD+D_{+}in the above answer by the actual
linear growth factorD+D_{+}computed by Boltzmann codes. In other words, one
uses theexactD+D_{+},fffrom the linear theory equations, but theFnF_{n},GnG_{n}kernels obtained in the limitΩm=f2=1\Omega_{m}=f^{2}=1.
This version of the EdS approximation actually gives an
exact answer in linear theory (i.e. at the leading approximation), and significantly suppresses the error at higher orders.
That’s why it has become standard in the field.

Before we proceed, let us mention that it is possible to solve the pressure-less perfect fluid
equations even without the EdS approximation[9,5,10,11,12,13],
but the exact result matches the approximate one to
better than 1%. Given that this≲1%\lesssim 1\%mismatch appears only
starting at the one-loop order,
they are likely to be
totally negligible in all
practical applications both for current and future galaxy surveys,
though there are
efficient ways to compute
these effects if needed[14].

One-loop power spectrum.We are in the position to compute thenon-linearmatter power spectrum in SPT. The fluid equations have the following perturbative solution for the density field:δNL=δ=δ(1)+δ(2)+δ(3)+⋯,\delta_{\mathrm{NL}}=\delta=\delta^{(1)}+\delta^{(2)}+\delta^{(3)}+\cdots\,,(75)

which we can square now to get the matter power spectrum,⟨δNL​(𝒌)​δNL​(𝒌′)⟩=(2​π)3​δD​(𝒌+𝒌′)​P​(k)\langle\delta_{\rm NL}({\bm{k}})\,\delta_{\rm NL}({\bm{k}}^{\prime})\rangle=(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}({\bm{k}}+{\bm{k}}^{\prime})\,P(k).
Keeping terms through orderO​(δ13)O(\delta_{1}^{3})we get,⟨δNL​(𝒌)​δNL​(𝒌′)⟩′\displaystyle\langle\delta_{\rm NL}({\bm{k}})\,\delta_{\rm NL}({\bm{k}}^{\prime})\rangle^{\prime}=⟨δ(1)​δ(1)⟩′⏟P11+2​⟨δ(1)​δ(2)⟩′⏟= 0 (odd)+⟨δ(2)​δ(2)⟩′⏟P22+2​⟨δ(3)​δ1⟩′⏟2​P13+…\displaystyle=\underbrace{\langle\delta^{(1)}\,\delta^{(1)}\rangle^{\prime}}_{P_{11}}+\underbrace{2\langle\delta^{(1)}\,\delta^{(2)}\rangle^{\prime}}_{\text{= 0 (odd)}}+\underbrace{\langle\delta^{(2)}\delta^{(2)}\rangle^{\prime}}_{P_{22}}+\underbrace{2\langle\delta^{(3)}\delta_{1}\rangle^{\prime}}_{2P_{13}}+\ldots(76)

where the factor of 2 above comes from the fact that there are two symmetric terms
from eachδNL\delta_{\mathrm{NL}}above. All expectation values are
computed viaWick’s theorem: for Gaussian initial conditions,
everynn-point function ofδ1\delta_{1}factorizes into a sum over all
distinct pairings (contractions), with each pair giving a linear
power spectrum:​δ1​(𝒌a)​δ1​(𝒌b)≡⟨δ1​(𝒌a)​δ1​(𝒌b)⟩=(2​π)3​δD​(𝒌a+𝒌b)​P11​(ka).\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.46529pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=29.60153pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 6.94444pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.46529pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=29.60153pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 6.94444pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 3.76878pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=25.13887pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 4.8611pt}}{\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 3.58684pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\vrule width=23.3167pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}\vskip 2.15277pt\vskip 3.47221pt}}\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{a})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{b})\;\equiv\;\langle\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{a})\,\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}_{b})\rangle=(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}({\bm{k}}_{a}+{\bm{k}}_{b})\,P_{11}(k_{a})\,.(77)

⟨δ1​δ(2)⟩\langle\delta_{1}\,\delta^{(2)}\rangleis the vanishing term.
It involves threeδ1\delta_{1}fields (one external, two fromF2F_{2}). An odd number of Gaussian fields cannot be fully paired, so we have⟨δ(1)​(𝒌)​δ(2)​(𝒌′)⟩′=∫d3​q(2​π)3​F2​(𝒒,𝒌′−𝒒)​⟨δ(1)​(𝒌)​δ(1)​(𝒒)​δ(1)​(𝒌′−𝒒)⟩=0.\langle\delta^{(1)}({\bm{k}})\;\delta^{(2)}({\bm{k}}^{\prime})\rangle^{\prime}=\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;F_{2}({\bm{q}},{\bm{k}}^{\prime}{-}{\bm{q}})\;\langle\delta^{(1)}({\bm{k}})\;\delta^{(1)}({\bm{q}})\;\delta^{(1)}({\bm{k}}^{\prime}{-}{\bm{q}})\rangle=0\,.(78)

Obviously the above term is non-zero fornon-Gaussianinitial conditions.
This case will be discussed elsewhere.

The next term is called the “22” term orP22P_{22}.
This correlator involves two copies ofδ(2)\delta^{(2)}:⟨δ(2)​(𝒌)​δ(2)​(𝒌′)⟩′=∫d3​q1(2​π)3​∫d3​q2(2​π)3​F2​(𝒒1,𝒌−𝒒1)​F2​(𝒒2,𝒌′−𝒒2)\displaystyle\langle\delta^{(2)}({\bm{k}})\;\delta^{(2)}({\bm{k}}^{\prime})\rangle^{\prime}=\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\!\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q_{2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;F_{2}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{k}}{-}{\bm{q}}_{1})\;F_{2}({\bm{q}}_{2},{\bm{k}}^{\prime}{-}{\bm{q}}_{2})×⟨δ1​(𝒒1)​δ1​(𝒌−𝒒1)​δ1​(𝒒2)​δ1​(𝒌′−𝒒2)⟩′.\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt\times\;\langle\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}_{1})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}{-}{\bm{q}}_{1})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}_{2})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}^{\prime}{-}{\bm{q}}_{2})\rangle^{\prime}\,.(79)

Wick’s theorem produces three pieces:

3.47221pt}}\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}_{1})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}{-}{\bm{q}}_{1})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}_{2})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}^{\prime}{-}{\bm{q}}_{2})(80)

generates terms contributing only at𝒌=0{\bm{k}}=0,𝒌′=0{\bm{k}}^{\prime}=0. This is thedisconnectedpiece
that vanishes once we take into account thatF2​(𝒒,−𝒒)=0F_{2}({\bm{q}},-{\bm{q}})=0. Then there are two identical
connected pieces from(13)​(24)(13)(24)and(14)​(23)(14)(23)contractions, givingP22​(k)=2​∫d3​q(2​π)3​[F2​(𝒒,𝒌−𝒒)]2​P11​(q)​P11​(|𝒌−𝒒|).\boxed{\;P_{22}(k)=2\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\bigl[F_{2}({\bm{q}},{\bm{k}}{-}{\bm{q}})\bigr]^{2}\;P_{11}(q)\;P_{11}(|{\bm{k}}{-}{\bm{q}}|)\;}\,.(81)

TheP13P_{13}term is computed in the same fashion. Starting from⟨δ(3)​(𝒌)​δ1​(𝒌′)⟩′=∫d3​q1(2​π)3​∫d3​q2(2​π)3​F3​(𝒒1,𝒒2,𝒌−𝒒1−𝒒2)\displaystyle\langle\delta^{(3)}({\bm{k}})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}^{\prime})\rangle^{\prime}=\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\!\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q_{2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;F_{3}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2},{\bm{k}}{-}{\bm{q}}_{1}{-}{\bm{q}}_{2})×⟨δ1​(𝒒1)​δ1​(𝒒2)​δ1​(𝒌−𝒒1−𝒒2)​δ1​(𝒌′)⟩′,\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\times\;\langle\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}_{1})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}_{2})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}{-}{\bm{q}}_{1}{-}{\bm{q}}_{2})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}^{\prime})\rangle^{\prime}\,,(82)

and collecting three identical pieces connected by the Wick pairing we arrive atP13​(k)=3​P11​(k)​∫d3​q(2​π)3​F3​(𝒌,𝒒,−𝒒)​P11​(q)\boxed{\;P_{13}(k)=3\,P_{11}(k)\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;F_{3}({\bm{k}},{\bm{q}},-{\bm{q}})\;P_{11}(q)\;}(83)

Summing the two we end up withPNL​(k)=Plin​(k)+Δ​P1​-loop​(k)=P11​(k)+P22​(k)+2​P13​(k).\begin{split}&P_{\rm NL}(k)=P_{\rm lin}(k)+\Delta P_{1\text{-loop}}(k)=P_{11}(k)+P_{22}(k)+2\,P_{13}(k)\,.\\
\end{split}(84)

The sum of the two terms on the right are called the “one-loop matter power spectrum correction:”Δ​P1−loop​(k)=P22​(k)+2​P13​(k).\boxed{\Delta P_{\rm 1-loop}(k)=P_{22}(k)+2\,P_{13}(k)}\,.(85)

The Wick contractions computed above can be organised systematically
using a diagrammatic technique analogous to Feynman diagrams in
quantum field theory.

## Feynman rules
- 1.

Vertex:an open square (Fig.7,
top) denotes the symmetrized kernelFn​(𝒒1,…,𝒒n)F_{n}({\bm{q}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n}), with one outgoing line of momentum𝒌=𝒒1+⋯+𝒒n{\bm{k}}={\bm{q}}_{1}+\cdots+{\bm{q}}_{n}andnnincoming lines, each carrying
one factor of the linear fieldδ1​(𝒒i)\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}_{i}).
- 2.

Line (P11P_{11}insertion):a filled circle
(Fig.7, bottom) joining two legs is a Wick
contraction of two linear fields,⟨δ1​(𝒒)​δ1​(−𝒒)⟩′=P11​(q)\langle\delta_{1}({\bm{q}})\,\delta_{1}(-{\bm{q}})\rangle^{\prime}=P_{11}(q). The arrows
on the two joined legs both pointintothe circle: the
contracted fields carry momenta𝒒{\bm{q}}and−𝒒-{\bm{q}}, so momentum
flows into the insertion from both sides. Each internal momentum
left free by momentum conservation is integrated with∫d3​q/(2​π)3\int\mathrm{d}^{3}q/(2\pi)^{3}; the number of such integrals is the
number of loopsLL, and anLL-loop diagram scales asP11L+1P_{11}^{L+1}.
- 3.

Multiplicity:multiply each diagram by the number
of distinct Wick pairings that produce the same topology.FnF_{n}𝒌{\bm{k}}𝒒n{\bm{q}}_{n}𝒒2{\bm{q}}_{2}𝒒1{\bm{q}}_{1}P11P_{11}F1F_{1}F1F_{1}PlinP_{\rm lin}P22P_{22}F2F_{2}F2F_{2}PlinP_{\rm lin}PlinP_{\rm lin}P13P_{13}F3F_{3}F1F_{1}PlinP_{\rm lin}PlinP_{\rm lin}Figure 7:Top:the SPT vertexFn​(𝒒1,…,𝒒n)F_{n}({\bm{q}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n})with

Reading the two one-loop diagrams is a matter of routing the momenta.
InP22P_{22}(the “sunset”), momentum conservation at the twoF2F_{2}vertices fixes the arcs to carry𝒒{\bm{q}}and𝒌−𝒒{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}, leaving one
free integral; inP13P_{13}(the “tadpole”), two legs ofF3F_{3}close
into a loop carrying𝒒{\bm{q}}and−𝒒-{\bm{q}}, while the third leg connects
to the external point through the bridgeP11​(k)P_{11}(k).

(22forP22P_{22};33per ordering forP13P_{13}, hence66in2​P132P_{13}).

The two topologies play very different roles, and this distinction
will organize much of what follows. InP13P_{13}one factor ofP11​(k)P_{11}(k)sitsoutsidethe loop:P13P_{13}-type diagrams are
corrections to thepropagationof the modekk, multiplicative
in the linear spectrum. InP22P_{22}bothP11P_{11}’s are evaluated at
loop momenta:P22P_{22}-type diagrams describe genuinemode coupling— power generated at𝒌{\bm{k}}by pairs of modes𝒒{\bm{q}},𝒌−𝒒{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}. We will meet this dichotomy again in the IR limits
below, and in the EFT renormalization: the UV sensitivity of the1313-type diagrams is absorbed by thek2​P11​(k)k^{2}P_{11}(k)counterterm
(Chapter 3), while that of the2222-type diagrams requires the
stochastick4k^{4}term (Chapter 4).

Finally, let us compute the one-loop integral.
One may notice that
our propagatorsP11P_{11}are complicated numerical functions
of momenta, in contrast to the usual QFT diagrams (e.g. the propagators
typically scale ask−2k^{-2}in the massless case). Because of this we need to do the loop
integrals numerically for a given set ofΛ\LambdaCDM model parameters.Figure 8:The one-loop matter power spectrumΔ​P1−loop\Delta P_{\rm 1-loop}, which is a sum of the2​P132P_{13}andP22P_{22}diagrams.
Dot-dashed lines show the leading infrared (IR) asymptotics of the loop integrals proportional to the displacement varianceσd2\sigma_{d}^{2}.
The computation is carried out numerically withclass-ptcode[15].

The numerical evaluation for our fiducial cosmology yields the result
displayed in Fig.8.
One important observation is that the total power spectrum
is a result of a large cancellation betweenP13P_{13}andP22P_{22}contributions.
Each of this diagrams individually is a factor of ten greater than
the residual.
This, in fact, is symptomatic of the problems with the
convergence in the
infrared limit, which we will discuss in detail shortly.Figure 9:Matter power spectrum atz=0z=0: SPT loop corrections versus Horizon Run N-body simulation data[16]. The three-loop SPT power spectrum is adapted from Ref.[17].

In principle, nothing stops us from computing more SPT loops: atLLloops the diagrams involve the correlators⟨δ(m)​δ(n)⟩\langle\delta^{(m)}\delta^{(n)}\ranglewithm+n=2​L+2m+n=2L+2and kernels up toF2​L+1F_{2L+1}; at two loops these areP33−IP_{33-I},P33−I​IP_{33-II}(there are two distinct topologies in the 3-3 sector),P24P_{24}andP15P_{15},
with two free loop momenta.
Such calculations have been done through the three loop
order even before the full development of EFT[18,19,20,17](see Figure9).
Back then the pioneers used to think that SPT is a full solution
to non-linear perturbation theory. In this logic, computing higher
loops will lead to a better match to data.
To test this,
Figure9shows the SPT performance
against the N-body data. There we show the linear theory prediction
and SPT corrections through the three loop order.
We see that computing more loops is simply not helpful
– the two-loop corrections
happened to be of the same order of magnitude as the one-loop,
and the three-loop SPT power spectrum is greater than the first two
corrections combined, on all scales.
In addition, these corrections don’t look like the data, e.g.
the tree-loop power spectrum grows atk∼0.1​h​Mpc−1k\sim 0.1~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}, while the
N-body data actually shows the decrease ofP​(k)P(k).
Clearly, the naive SPT
expansion is in trouble.

Historically, the pioneers thought that SPT diverges like an
asymptotic series, and that some kind of resummation might improve
its convergence properties. This logic often works for asymptotic
series, as e.g. in the case of the anharmonic oscillator in
ordinary quantum mechanics. This line of thinking produced a vast
literature on resummations of SPT. These resummations, however,
were a red herring — the real issue was the fundamental
inconsistency of the perfect fluid equations. This is nicely
demonstrated, e.g., in Ref.[21], which focused
on matter clustering in 1+1 dimensions. The perfect pressureless
fluid equations can be solved exactly in this case, but the exact
solution still fails to describe the data — a clear demonstration
that no resummation of diagrams within the wrong theory can
compensate for the absence of the correct physical ingredients.

Now we know that the breakdown of the SPT loop expansion
happens due to the absence of a proper renormalization
of the corresponding loop diagrams.
This renormalization is provided by the effective stress tensor,
which we have argued must be present in the equations of motion
purely on symmetry grounds.
It’s instructive nevertheless
to study the IR and UV issues of the SPT expansion more
carefully in order to understand how the full EFT treatment fixes them.

## 3.3IR Limit of Loop Corrections and the Equivalence Principle

Consider the limits of the loop integrals when the loop momenta
are parametrically smaller than the external momentumkk. Formally this can be done by introducing an IR scalekIRk_{\mathrm{IR}}such thatq≤kIRq\leq k_{\mathrm{IR}}andk≫kIRk\gg k_{\mathrm{IR}}.
These are calledIR limits:
- •

P13P_{13}:k≫qk\gg q,
- •

P22P_{22}:k≫qk\gg qandk≫|𝒌−𝒒|k\gg|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|

In theP22P_{22}case the two IR domains are related by symmetry𝒒↔𝒌−𝒒{\bm{q}}\leftrightarrow{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}, so it sufficient to just consider one and multiply the answer by 2.
Theleading-order IR limitsread:P22IR,LO\displaystyle P_{22}^{\mathrm{IR,LO}}≈k23​P11​(k)​∫0kIRd3​q​P11​(q)q2=k2​σd,IR2​P11​(k),\displaystyle\approx\frac{k^{2}}{3}\,P_{11}(k)\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{IR}}}d^{3}q\;\frac{P_{11}(q)}{q^{2}}=k^{2}\,\sigma_{d,\mathrm{IR}}^{2}\;P_{11}(k)\,,(86)2​P13IR,LO\displaystyle 2P_{13}^{\mathrm{IR,LO}}=−k23​P11​(k)​∫0kIRd3​q​P11​(q)q2=−k2​σd,IR2​P11​(k).\displaystyle=-\frac{k^{2}}{3}\,P_{11}(k)\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{IR}}}d^{3}q\;\frac{P_{11}(q)}{q^{2}}=-k^{2}\,\sigma_{d,\mathrm{IR}}^{2}\;P_{11}(k)\,.(87)

When summed together, the two leading IR corrections cancel:2P13IR,LO+P22IR,LO=0.(Leading-order IR cancellation)\boxed{2P_{13}^{\mathrm{IR,LO}}+P_{22}^{\mathrm{IR,LO}}=0\,.}\qquad\text{(Leading-order IR cancellation)}(88)

This is illustrated in Fig.8, where one can see
that the IR corrections actually
dominate the individualP13P_{13}andP22P_{22}diagrams. To produce figure Fig.8we had to take the formal limitkIR→∞k_{\mathrm{IR}}\to\inftyyieldingσd,IR2→σd2\sigma_{d,\mathrm{IR}}^{2}\to\sigma_{d}^{2}. This might seem to be at odds with the IR limitq/k→0q/k\to 0.
However, the displacement variance integrals actually saturate atq∼keq≃0.02​h​Mpc−1q\sim k_{\rm eq}\simeq 0.02~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}, so extrapolation
ofkIRk_{\mathrm{IR}}to small scales produces only a tiny error.

The first important consequence of the above cancellation
result is that
the physical non-linear coupling of
a modekkto large scalesis notcontrolled by the large-scale displacement field, as might be naively
inferred from the SPT expansion.
We will see momentarily that such coupling, in fact, is forbidden by the equivalence principle.
The second important consequence of this cancellation is
that in practice we need to keep high precision
of our calculation of individual SPT integrals
if we want to compute the totalΔ​P1−loop\Delta P_{\rm 1-loop}accurately.
Indeed, if the target
precision onΔ​P1−loop\Delta P_{\rm 1-loop}isO​(1%)O(1\%), the
error on our individualP22,P13P_{22},P_{13}computations
must beO​(0.1%)O(0.1\%). While this is not a big problem at the one-loop
order, the situation becomes more difficult at higher orders where
the infrared domains become more complicated and the computations
become entangled by sub-divergences. In this case the development
of infrared-safe integrands (i.e. integrands with offending divergences subtracted) becomes vital[20,17,22,23].

The origin of IR cancellation: a toy model.To dive deeper into the nature of this cancellation, let us consider a toy model of a shift
of a density fieldδ1​(𝒌)\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})from its initial
position𝒙{\bm{x}}by a homogeneous displacementΔ​xLi\Delta x^{i}_{L}sourced by the large-scale (long-wavelength) modes. In Fourier space the shifted field can be written asδ~1​(𝒌)=∫d3​x​e−i​𝒌​(𝒙+Δ​𝒙L)​δ1​(𝒙)=e−i​𝒌​Δ​𝒙L​δ1​(𝒌).\tilde{\delta}_{1}({\bm{k}})=\int d^{3}x~e^{-i{\bm{k}}({\bm{x}}+\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L})}\delta_{1}({\bm{x}})=e^{-i{\bm{k}}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}}\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})~\,.(89)

Let us now Taylor expand this inΔ​𝒙L\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}, as one does in
SPT:δ~1​(𝒌)=δ1​(𝒌)​(1−i​𝒌​Δ​𝒙L−12​ki​kj​Δ​xLi​Δ​xLj+…)≡δ1​(𝒌)+δ2​(𝒌)+δ3​(𝒌)+…,δ2​(𝒌)≡−i​δ1​(𝒌)​ki​Δ​xLi,δ3​(𝒌)≡−12​δ1​(𝒌)​ki​kj​Δ​xLi​Δ​xLj.\begin{split}&\tilde{\delta}_{1}({\bm{k}})=\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\left(1-i{\bm{k}}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}-\frac{1}{2}k_{i}k_{j}\Delta x^{i}_{L}\Delta x^{j}_{L}+...\right)\equiv\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})+\delta_{2}({\bm{k}})+\delta_{3}({\bm{k}})+...\,,\\
&\delta_{2}({\bm{k}})\equiv-i\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})k_{i}\Delta x^{i}_{L}\,,\quad\delta_{3}({\bm{k}})\equiv-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})k_{i}k_{j}\Delta x^{i}_{L}\Delta x^{j}_{L}~\,.\end{split}(90)

Let’s compute now the power spectrum of the shifted fieldδ~1\tilde{\delta}_{1}toO​(Δ​xL2​k2)O(\Delta x_{L}^{2}k^{2}), equivalent to
a one-loop order. We have:⟨δ~1​(𝒌)​δ~1​(−𝒌)⟩′=⟨δ1​(𝒌)​δ1​(−𝒌)⟩′+⟨δ2​(𝒌)​δ2​(−𝒌)⟩′+2​⟨δ1​(𝒌)​δ3​(−𝒌)⟩′,\langle\tilde{\delta}_{1}({\bm{k}})\tilde{\delta}_{1}(-{\bm{k}})\rangle^{\prime}=\langle\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\delta_{1}(-{\bm{k}})\rangle^{\prime}+\langle\delta_{2}({\bm{k}})\delta_{2}(-{\bm{k}})\rangle^{\prime}+2\langle\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\delta_{3}(-{\bm{k}})\rangle^{\prime}\,,(91)

where one can see the emergence of the baby versions of theP22P_{22}andP13P_{13}integrals. Specifically, we have:P~22​(k)≡⟨δ2​(𝒌)​δ2​(−𝒌)⟩′=Δ​xLi​Δ​xLj​ki​kj​P11​(k),P~13​(k)≡⟨δ1​(𝒌)​δ3​(−𝒌)⟩′=−12​Δ​xLi​Δ​xLj​ki​kj​P11​(k).\begin{split}&\tilde{P}_{22}(k)\equiv\langle\delta_{2}({\bm{k}})\delta_{2}(-{\bm{k}})\rangle^{\prime}=\Delta x^{i}_{L}\Delta x^{j}_{L}k^{i}k^{j}P_{11}(k)\,,\\
&\tilde{P}_{13}(k)\equiv\langle\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\delta_{3}(-{\bm{k}})\rangle^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{2}\Delta x^{i}_{L}\Delta x^{j}_{L}k^{i}k^{j}P_{11}(k)\,.\end{split}(92)

P~22​(k)\tilde{P}_{22}(k)is a positive contribution
that describes the increase of power due to
a coherent shift of bothδ1\delta_{1}’s.
The displacement of eachδ1\delta_{1}at this order is reminiscent
of a gravitational pull, which increases clustering.
In contrast,P~13​(k)\tilde{P}_{13}(k)describes the apparent reduction of power
when one shifts oneδ1\delta_{1}at a time. At this order in
perturbation it looks as if the matter
particles were moved away from
each other, hence the suppression of clustering.
The two effects cancel each other exactly,P~22+2​P~13=0\tilde{P}_{22}+2\tilde{P}_{13}=0. This happens for a very good reason: the position
space correlation function depends only on a relative distance between the two particles:⟨δ​(𝒙+Δ​𝒙L)​δ​(𝒙+Δ​𝒙L+𝒓)⟩=ξ​(|𝒓|),\langle\delta({\bm{x}}+\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L})\,\delta({\bm{x}}+\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}+{\bm{r}})\rangle=\xi(|{\bm{r}}|)\,,(93)

hence a constant displacement can’t change the power spectrum.
In our toy model the cancellation of the displacement contributions
happened, in effect, thanks to the homogeneity of the Universe,
related to momentum conservation. In the actual problemΔ​xj\Delta x^{j}itself is sourced by the underlying density fieldδ1\delta_{1}, and the cancellation happens due to the
equivalence principle. While our toy model can’t show this,
it exhibits the key mechanism for this cancellation: the
immunity of the power spectrum to homogeneous shifts of matter by virtue of underlying symmetries.
We now sharpen this statement and show that the cancellation
follows from an exact symmetry of the equations of motion.Figure 10:One-loop power spectrum and its asymptotic IR and UV limits atz=0z=0.

The boost symmetry and the equivalence principle.The toy model relied on aconstantshift, which is removed by
ordinary translation invariance.
The displacement generated by a
physical long mode grows in time, so we need to use a
more physical expression
forΔ​𝒙L\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}.
In addition, we need to prove that the displaced
density is actually a solution of the fluid equations.
The crucial
observation is that the full non-linear system
(eqs.55,56,57) isexactlyinvariant under a time-dependent uniform boost[18,24,25,26,27,28],𝒙~=𝒙+𝒏​(τ),𝒗~​(𝒙~,τ)=𝒗​(𝒙,τ)+𝒏′​(τ),Φ~​(𝒙~,τ)=Φ​(𝒙,τ)−(𝒏′′+ℋ​𝒏′)⋅𝒙~,\tilde{\bm{x}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{n}}(\tau)\,,\qquad\tilde{\bm{v}}(\tilde{\bm{x}},\tau)={\bm{v}}({\bm{x}},\tau)+{\bm{n}}^{\prime}(\tau)\,,\qquad\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{\bm{x}},\tau)=\Phi({\bm{x}},\tau)-\left({\bm{n}}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}\,{\bm{n}}^{\prime}\right)\cdot\tilde{\bm{x}}\,,(94)

withδ\deltaandτi​j\tau_{ij}transforming without extra terms, and𝒏​(τ){\bm{n}}(\tau)anarbitraryfunction of time. The check takes
two lines: in the Euler equation the frame acceleration𝒏′′+ℋ​𝒏′{\bm{n}}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}{\bm{n}}^{\prime}generated on the left-hand side is exactly
compensated by the uniform force from the shifted potential on the
right; the continuity equation involves only quantities that
transform as scalars; and the Poisson equation is untouched because
the extra piece ofΦ~\tilde{\Phi}is linear in𝒙~\tilde{\bm{x}}.222Strictly speaking, a potential growing linearly
with𝒙{\bm{x}}is not a standard perturbation. The transformation should
be understood as theq→0q\to 0limit of a physical long-wavelength
mode — an “adiabatic mode” in the terminology of Weinberg[29].

Let us now use this symmetry to construct the solution in the
presence of a long mode. Take a solutionδS\delta_{S},𝒗S{\bm{v}}_{S}of the
non-linear equations without the long mode, and apply the
boost (94) with𝒏​(τ)=Δ​𝒙L​(τ){\bm{n}}(\tau)=\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}(\tau), the
displacement of a long-wavelength linear growing modeδL​(𝒒)\delta_{L}({\bm{q}}),Δ​𝒙L​(𝒒,τ)=i​𝒒q2​δL​(𝒒,τ),\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}({\bm{q}},\tau)=\frac{i{\bm{q}}}{q^{2}}\,\delta_{L}({\bm{q}},\tau)\,,(95)

cf. Eq. (38). Since this mode is long-wavelength, it is appropriate to use a linear solution for it. The transformed configuration then
carries the extra uniform velocity𝒗L=Δ​𝒙L′{\bm{v}}_{L}=\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}^{\prime}.
Crucially, since the long growing mode obeys its own linearized
Euler equation,Δ​𝒙L′′+ℋ​Δ​𝒙L′=−∇ΦL,\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}\,\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}^{\prime}=-\bm{\nabla}\Phi_{L}\,,(96)

the shift of the potential in Eq. (94) is precisely the
long-mode potential in theq→0q\to 0limit: the transformation has
generatedboththe velocity and the gravitational force of
the physical long mode. This is where the equivalence principle does
real work — a single change of frame compensates the
