long-wavelength force for every fluid element at once only because
they all fall identically. We conclude that, to leading order inqq, the solution in the presence of the long mode is the translated
short-scale solution:δS​(𝒌,τ)|L=e−i​𝒌⋅Δ​𝒙L​(τ)​δS​(𝒌,τ)+O​(∂i∂jΦL),\delta_{S}({\bm{k}},\tau)\Big|_{L}=e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}(\tau)}\;\delta_{S}({\bm{k}},\tau)+O\!\left(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\Phi_{L}\right)\,,(97)

where the corrections — the density and tidal couplings∂i∂jΦL∼δL\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\Phi_{L}\sim\delta_{L}— arenotenhanced
by1/q1/q. Note that Eq. (97) is
non-perturbative inΔ​𝒙L\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}; the
only expansion we’ve been using isq→0q\to 0.
The cancellation is now manifest. At equal times, and foreach realizationof the long mode,P​(k,τ)|L=|e−i​𝒌⋅Δ​𝒙L​(τ)|2​PS​(k,τ)=PS​(k,τ):P(k,\tau)\Big|_{L}=\left|e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}(\tau)}\right|^{2}P_{S}(k,\tau)=P_{S}(k,\tau)\,:(98)

the phases cancel betweenδ\deltaandδ∗\delta^{*}before any
averaging overδL\delta_{L}is performed, so no IR-enhanced
contribution can survive — at any order in perturbation theory. To
make contact with the diagrams, expand
Eq. (97) inΔ​𝒙L\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}, treat the latter
as a Gaussian random field with variance⟨Δ​xLi​Δ​xLj⟩=δi​j​σd,IR2\langle\Delta x_{L}^{i}\,\Delta x_{L}^{j}\rangle=\delta^{ij}\sigma^{2}_{d,\mathrm{IR}}(built from the modesq≤kIRq\leq k_{\mathrm{IR}}), and correlate. The term with one
power ofΔ​𝒙L\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}on each side is the IR limit ofP22P_{22},
while the term with(Δ​𝒙L)2(\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L})^{2}acting on one side is the IR
limit of2​P132P_{13}:P22IR,LO=⟨(𝒌⋅Δ​𝒙L)2⟩​P11​(k)=+k2​σd,IR2​P11​(k),2​P13IR,LO=2⋅(−12)​⟨(𝒌⋅Δ​𝒙L)2⟩​P11​(k)=−k2​σd,IR2​P11​(k),\begin{split}P_{22}^{\mathrm{IR,LO}}&=\langle({\bm{k}}\cdot\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L})^{2}\rangle\,P_{11}(k)=+\,k^{2}\,\sigma^{2}_{d,\mathrm{IR}}\,P_{11}(k)\,,\\
2P_{13}^{\mathrm{IR,LO}}&=2\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)\langle({\bm{k}}\cdot\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L})^{2}\rangle\,P_{11}(k)=-\,k^{2}\,\sigma^{2}_{d,\mathrm{IR}}\,P_{11}(k)\,,\end{split}(99)

reproducing the direct diagrammatic computation above and summing to
zero, Eq. (88).
Likewise, matching
the same expansion stemming from Eq. (97) to the
SPT solution fixes the soft limits of the kernels — a non-trivial
prediction of the symmetry:limq→02​F2​(𝒒,𝒌−𝒒)=𝒌⋅𝒒q2+O​(q0),limq→03​F3​(𝒌,𝒒,−𝒒)=−(𝒌⋅𝒒)22​q4+less singular,\lim_{q\to 0}\;2\,F_{2}({\bm{q}},{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}})=\frac{{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}{q^{2}}+O(q^{0})\,,\qquad\lim_{q\to 0}\;3\,F_{3}({\bm{k}},{\bm{q}},-{\bm{q}})=-\frac{({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{q}})^{2}}{2\,q^{4}}+\text{less singular}\,,(100)

where the factor of22counts the two orderings of the arguments inδ(2)\delta^{(2)}, and the factor of33the Wick contractions in⟨δ1​δ(3)⟩\langle\delta_{1}\,\delta^{(3)}\rangle.

Homework:verify from the
explicit kernels of §3.2 thatF2​(𝒒,𝒌−𝒒)→(𝒌⋅𝒒)/(2​q2)F_{2}({\bm{q}},{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}})\to({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{q}})/(2q^{2})andF3​(𝒌,𝒒,−𝒒)→−(𝒌⋅𝒒)2/(6​q4)F_{3}({\bm{k}},{\bm{q}},-{\bm{q}})\to-({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{q}})^{2}/(6q^{4}), in agreement with
Eq. (100).

The argument comes with a set of corollaries:
- 1.

Equal times only.At unequal times the phases
combine intoe−i​𝒌⋅[Δ​𝒙L​(τ1)−Δ​𝒙L​(τ2)]≠1e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot[\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}(\tau_{1})-\Delta{\bm{x}}_{L}(\tau_{2})]}\neq 1:
therelativedisplacement of the two time slices is
frame-independent and physical. Accordingly, unequal-time
correlators (propagators, response functions) are not protected by
the symmetry — they are exponentially damped by the large-scale
displacements.
- 2.

Adiabaticity and the equivalence principle.The
construction requires the long mode to be the adiabatic growing
mode (velocity locked to the gravitational force) and all species
to fall identically. A long-range fifth force with
species-dependent couplings, isocurvature velocity perturbations,
or the relative baryon–dark-matter velocity cannot be removed by
a single change of frame and leave residual IR effects in the
(cross-)correlations. Searching for the corresponding violations
of the IR structure (“consistency relations”) is a test of the
equivalence principle on cosmological scales.
- 3.

Only the displacement-enhanced terms are
protected.The symmetry removes the terms enhanced byσd,IR2\sigma^{2}_{d,\mathrm{IR}}and nothing else: theO​(q0)O(q^{0})couplings — the separate-universe response to the long-mode
density and tides — are physical. These are precisely the
subleadingσℓ2\sigma_{\ell}^{2}effects that we compute next.
- 4.

Coherence.The symmetry removes only displacements
that are coherent across the scales probed by the observable. For
an observable that compares points separated byℓBAO\ell_{\mathrm{BAO}}— the BAO wiggles — modes withq≳koscq\gtrsim k_{\mathrm{osc}}producerelativedisplacements of the two
points, which are physical and, as we will see, non-perturbative.
This is the origin of the IR resummation discussed below; the
equivalence principle still leaves its imprint there, forcing the
contribution of modesq≪koscq\ll k_{\mathrm{osc}}to the BAO damping to be
suppressed as(q/kosc)2(q/k_{\mathrm{osc}})^{2}.

## 3.4IR resummation of the BAO

Physical IR effects.Let us now compute the sub-leading IR limit that one gets after the
leading order cancellations. To that end one needs to Taylor expandP​(|𝒌−𝒒|)P(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)inP22P_{22}, so we assume at this point that this expansion
is convergent.
We find the followingsubleading contributions:Δ​P1​-loopIR​(k)=(569735​P11​(k)−47105​k​P11′​(k)+110​k2​P11′′​(k))⋅σℓ2,\Delta P^{\rm IR}_{1\text{-loop}}(k)=\left(\frac{569}{735}\,P_{11}(k)-\frac{47}{105}\,k\,P^{\prime}_{11}(k)+\frac{1}{10}\,k^{2}\,P^{\prime\prime}_{11}(k)\right)\cdot\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\,,(101)

where we observe the appearance of the large-scale mass variance:σℓ2=∫0kIRd3​q​P11​(q),\sigma_{\ell}^{2}=\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{IR}}}d^{3}q\;P_{11}(q)~\,,(102)

which is essentially the same object as
a smoothed mass variance introduced in Eq. (35).
The only formal difference is that the smoothing in (35)
was implemented by a position-space top hat filter, while
Eq. (102) uses a sharp cutoff in momentum space.
We see now that a modekkcouples to IR modesq≪kq\ll kvia the mass variance. Physically, a large-scale over-density
modeqqbehaves like a separate Universe which boosts the growth of
smaller overdensities of wavenumberkkin its background.
Likewise, a large-scale under-density will locally slow
down the growth of structure.
The large-scale coupling should then be proportional
to the average mass variance, i.e.σℓ2\sigma_{\ell}^{2}.
For reasonable valueskIR∼0.02​h​Mpc−1k_{\mathrm{IR}}\sim 0.02~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}, the mass fluctuation
is very small,σℓ2∼10−2\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\sim 10^{-2}. However,
askkincreases, more and more modes fall into the
IR domain, so thatkIRk_{\mathrm{IR}}should increase as well.
In this caseσℓ2\sigma_{\ell}^{2}grows rapidly and the IR limit (101) starts to dominate the entire
one-loop integral. For instance, usingkIR=k/10k_{\mathrm{IR}}=k/10we
see that the IR modes account to
the bulk ofΔ​P1−loop\Delta P_{\rm 1-loop}fork≳0.1​h​Mpc−1k\gtrsim 0.1~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1},
see Fig.10.

BAO and IR resummation.The subleading IR result (101) contains derivatives
of the linear power spectrum, so the actual size of the IR
corrections depends on how featurefulP11P_{11}is.
To quantify this, let us decompose the linear power spectrum
into a smooth (broadband) part and a wiggly part that contains
the baryon acoustic oscillations:P11​(k)=Ps​(k)+Pw​(k),Pw​(k)=fs​(k)​sin⁡(kkosc),kosc=(100​Mpc/h)−1≃10−2​h​Mpc−1,P_{11}(k)=P_{s}(k)+P_{w}(k)\,,\qquad P_{w}(k)=f_{s}(k)\,\sin\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}}\right)\,,\qquad k_{\mathrm{osc}}=(100\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h)^{-1}\simeq 10^{-2}\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}\,,(103)

wherefs​(k)f_{s}(k)is a smooth envelope function.
An important remark is in order. The derivation of
Eq. (101) relied on the Taylor expansion ofP11​(|𝒌−𝒒|)P_{11}(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)inq/kq/k. This expansion
has a different convergence rate for the smooth and wiggly parts.

For the smooth part the logarithmic derivatives are of the order
of the spectral tilt,d​ln⁡Psd​ln⁡k∼n=O​(1),\frac{d\ln P_{s}}{d\ln k}\sim n=O(1)\,,(104)

so all three terms in Eq. (101) are comparable and
the IR correction is safely small,Δ​P1​-loops​(k)|IR∼Ps​(k)​σℓ2≪Ps​(k).\Delta P^{s}_{1\text{-loop}}(k)\Big|_{\rm IR}\sim P_{s}(k)\,\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\ll P_{s}(k)\,.(105)

Hence, for the smooth part of the power spectrum the IR
corrections are perturbative.

The situation is very different for the wiggly part: every
derivative acting on the rapidly oscillating factor brings
down an enhancementk/kosc≫1k/k_{\mathrm{osc}}\gg 1,k​Pw′≈fs​kkosc​cos⁡(kkosc),k2​Pw′′≈−fs​k2kosc2​sin⁡(kkosc),k\,P_{w}^{\prime}\approx f_{s}\,\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}}\,\cos\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}}\right)\,,\qquad k^{2}P_{w}^{\prime\prime}\approx-f_{s}\,\frac{k^{2}}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}^{2}}\,\sin\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}}\right)\,,(106)

where we differentiate only the sine, as the envelopefsf_{s}is smooth.
Plugging these expressions into Eq. (101), we obtainΔ​P1​-loopw​(k)|IR=σℓ2​fs​[569735​sin⁡(kkosc)−47105​kkosc​cos⁡(kkosc)−110​k2kosc2​sin⁡(kkosc)].\Delta P^{w}_{1\text{-loop}}(k)\Big|_{\rm IR}=\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\,f_{s}\left[\frac{569}{735}\,\sin\!\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}}\right)-\frac{47}{105}\,\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}}\,\cos\!\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}}\right)-\frac{1}{10}\,\frac{k^{2}}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}^{2}}\,\sin\!\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}}\right)\right]\,.(107)

To interpret this result it is convenient to combine the tree-level
and one-loop wiggly contributions usingsin⁡X−ϵ​cos⁡X=sin⁡(X​(1−ϵ))+O​(ϵ2)\sin X-\epsilon\cos X=\sin\big(X(1-\epsilon)\big.)+O(\epsilon^{2}),
valid forϵ≪1\epsilon\ll 1. This givesPw​(k)+Δ​P1​-loopw​(k)|IR=fs​sin⁡(kkosc​(1−47105​σℓ2))​(1+569735​σℓ2−110​k2kosc2​σℓ2).P_{w}(k)+\Delta P^{w}_{1\text{-loop}}(k)\Big|_{\rm IR}=f_{s}\,\sin\!\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}}\Big(1-\frac{47}{105}\,\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\Big)\right)\left(1+\frac{569}{735}\,\sigma_{\ell}^{2}-\frac{1}{10}\,\frac{k^{2}}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}^{2}}\,\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\right)\,.(108)

We observe two distinct physical effects. First, the long-wavelength
modes shift the BAO phase byΔ=47105​σℓ2∼10−2\Delta=\frac{47}{105}\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\sim 10^{-2}.
This effect is small and fully perturbative.333The smallness of this effect is foundation of the BAO method of distance measurements
in cosmology.σℓ2≪1\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\ll 1is, however, somewhat of a coincidence
of ourΛ\LambdaCDM-like universe. If the matter power spectrum had been enhanced on large scales,σℓ2\sigma_{\ell}^{2}could have been much larger,
and the BAO scale would have been strongly shifted by the non-linear evolution.Second, the oscillations get suppressed
by the last term in the brackets, and this term is dangerous.
Indeed, already atk=0.1​h​Mpc−1k=0.1~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}we havek/kosc≃0.1×100=10k/k_{\mathrm{osc}}\simeq 0.1\times 100=10, so thatk2kosc2​σℓ2∼100×10−2∼1,\frac{k^{2}}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}^{2}}\,\sigma_{\ell}^{2}\sim 100\times 10^{-2}\sim 1\,,(109)

i.e. the “correction” to the wiggly part is of order one.
Perturbation theory breaks down for the BAO part of the power
spectrum even at wavenumbers where the broadband part is
perfectly under control.

Two comments are in order here.
The result for the phase shift above is exact.
The result for the suppression, however, is not: in deriving
Eq. (107) we have Taylor-expandedP11​(|𝒌−𝒒|)P_{11}(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)and hence
retained only the modes withq≤koscq\leq k_{\mathrm{osc}}, while
the modeskosc≲q≪kk_{\mathrm{osc}}\lesssim q\ll kalso contribute atO​(1)O(1).
To capture them one has to replace the Taylor expansion
of the wiggly component with a finite difference, which yields the full IR limitΔ​P1​-loopw​(k)|IR,full=2​∫q≤kIRd3​q​(𝒌⋅𝒒)2q4​P11​(q)​[Pw​(|𝒌−𝒒|)−Pw​(k)],\Delta P^{w}_{1\text{-loop}}(k)\Big|_{\rm IR,~full}=2\int_{q\leq k_{\mathrm{IR}}}d^{3}q\;\frac{({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{q}})^{2}}{q^{4}}\,P_{11}(q)\left[P_{w}(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)-P_{w}(k)\right]\,,(110)

whose structure makes two points manifest: (a) the non-linear
coupling of an oscillating mode𝒌{\bm{k}}to modeq≳koscq\gtrsim k_{\mathrm{osc}}is actuallyk2​σd2k^{2}\sigma_{d}^{2}and (b) a strictly homogeneous
displacement (Pw​(|𝒌−𝒒|)→Pw​(k)P_{w}(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)\to P_{w}(k)asq→0q\to 0) drops out,
in agreement with the equivalence principle.

Since the offending coupling isO​(1)O(1), the IR-enhanced
contributions must be resummed to all orders in perturbation
theory. This is calledIR resummation.
There are two ways to carry it out:
- 1.

Resum the IR-enhanced parts of loop diagrams
diagram by diagram directly in Eulerian PT
(systematically implemented in time-sliced perturbation theory, TSPT[30,31,32,33]);
- 2.

Use Lagrangian PT[34,35,36,37,28,38], based on𝒙=𝒙ini+𝚿​(τ,𝒙ini){\bm{x}}={\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{ini}}+{\bm{\Psi}}(\tau,{\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{ini}}),
where𝒙ini{\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{ini}}is an initial position and𝚿{\bm{\Psi}}is the dynamical
displacement field. There the displacement is kept non-perturbatively from the start and never Taylor-expanded.444A nice feature of LPT is that it does not require a “wiggly-smooth” split of the matter power spectrum, thereby removing any potential uncertainty associated with it.

Either way, the net result is the exponentiation of the
damping factor: at leading order the IR-resummed wiggly
spectrum readsPIR​-​res,LOw​(k)=e−Σ2​k2​Pw​(k),P^{w}_{\mathrm{IR\text{-}res,\,LO}}(k)=e^{-\Sigma^{2}k^{2}}\;P_{w}(k)\,,(111)

with the BAO damping factorΣ2=16​π2​∫0kIR𝑑q​P11​(q)​[1−j0​(qkosc)+2​j2​(qkosc)],\Sigma^{2}=\frac{1}{6\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{IR}}}dq\,P_{11}(q)\,\left[1-j_{0}\!\left(\frac{q}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}}\right)+2\,j_{2}\!\left(\frac{q}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}}\right)\right]\,,(112)

wherejℓj_{\ell}are spherical Bessel functions.
Forq≫koscq\gg k_{\mathrm{osc}}the expression in the square brackets
approaches unity and we recognize inΣ2\Sigma^{2}the dispersion
of the relative displacements: only the motions that do not
reduce to a homogeneous shift across one BAO wavelength
damp the wiggles. In the opposite limitq≪koscq\ll k_{\mathrm{osc}}the brackets reduce to310​(q/kosc)2\frac{3}{10}(q/k_{\mathrm{osc}})^{2},
so that this part of the integral givesΣ2≈110​σℓ2kosc2,\Sigma^{2}\approx\frac{1}{10}\,\frac{\sigma_{\ell}^{2}}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}^{2}}\,,(113)

reproducing the suppression term in Eq. (108):
the very long modes couple to the BAO through the mass
varianceσℓ2\sigma_{\ell}^{2}, and not through their displacements —
the equivalence-principle protection (corollary 4 of the boost-symmetry
argument above) at work inside the damping integral.

Finally, the one-loop power spectrum with IR resummation is
assembled as follows: the tree-level wiggly part is damped
(and corrected for the damping already generated at one loop),
while the loop integrals are evaluated on the leading-order
resummed input spectrum:P1−loopIR−res(k)=Ps+Pwe−Σ2​k2(1+Σ2k2)+P1​-loop[Ps+Pwe−Σ2​k2],\boxed{\;P_{\rm 1-loop}^{\rm IR-res}(k)=P_{s}+P_{w}\,e^{-\Sigma^{2}k^{2}}\!\left(1+\Sigma^{2}k^{2}\right)+P_{1\text{-loop}}\!\left[P_{s}+P_{w}\,e^{-\Sigma^{2}k^{2}}\right]\,,\;}(114)

whereP1​-loop​[P]P_{1\text{-loop}}[P]denotes the one-loop integrals
computed with the input spectrumPP. The factor(1+Σ2​k2)(1+\Sigma^{2}k^{2})removes the double counting of the damping:
expanding Eq. (114) to first order inΣ2\Sigma^{2}reproduces the fixed-order one-loop result, so the difference
is a two-loop effect.

Figure11illustrates the results
of this section at the level of the position-space two-point
correlation functionξ​(r)\xi(r). The left panel displays the Horizon Run
N-body data
atz=0z=0[16]. We see that the linear theory significantly over-predicts
the amplitude of the BAO peak in the correlation function.
The SPT one-loop correction, however, predicts a totally
incorrect shape and spurious oscillations.
These are produced byO​(1)O(1)SPT 1-loop corrections
toPwP_{w}, proportional to its logarithmic derivatives.
The IR-resummation formula (114) kills the spurious distortions, and provides
an accurate prediction for the shape of the BAO peak
at one-loop order seen in the right panel of Figure11. This prediction
slightly improves upon including the two-loop corrections.Figure 11:BAO in the position space correlation function atz=0z=0multiplied byr4r^{4}to enhance the visibility of the BAO peak.
The data is from the Horizon Run N-body simulation[16,31].Left:failure of 1-loop SPT to capture the shape of the BAO peak.Right:IR-resummed EFT captures the non-linear distortion of the peak. Note that the EFT predictions also include the UV counterterms (discussed below).

## 3.5UV Limit of Loop Corrections

Let us now consider the opposite regime, in which the loop momentum
is parametrically larger than the external one,q≫kq\gg k. In analogy
with the IR case, we introduce a scalekUVk_{\mathrm{UV}}such that the loop
momenta satisfyq≥kUV≫kq\geq k_{\mathrm{UV}}\gg k, and expand the loop integrands
accordingly. These are theUV limitsof the loop
corrections.
For theP22P_{22}diagram the expansion of theF2F_{2}kernels yieldsP22​(k)|q≫k=9196​π2​k4​∫kUV∞d​qq2​[P11​(q)]2.P_{22}(k)\Big|_{q\gg k}=\frac{9}{196\pi^{2}}\;k^{4}\int_{k_{\mathrm{UV}}}^{\infty}\frac{dq}{q^{2}}\;[P_{11}(q)]^{2}\,.(115)

This result has two remarkable features. First, it scales ask4k^{4}and carries no dependence onP11​(k)P_{11}(k)whatsoever: thesmall-scale modes decouplefrom the long-wavelength mode.
Whatever the short modes do, their imprint on the large-scale power
spectrum is a smooth, noise-like contribution∝k4\propto k^{4}. We will
see later (via Peebles’ momentum-conservation argument in the chapter on
stochastic effects) that this scaling is not an accident of SPT, but
is dictated by mass and momentum conservation. Second, we can ask
when this integral actually converges. For a power-law universe,P11​(q)∝qνP_{11}(q)\propto q^{\nu}, we getP22UV∝k4​∫𝑑q​q2​ν−2P_{22}^{\mathrm{UV}}\propto k^{4}\int dq\;q^{2\nu-2}, which
diverges forν≥1/2\nu\geq 1/2. This, however, does not happen
in our Universe, where the above integral converges quite quickly.

For theP13P_{13}diagram one finds in the same limit2​P13​(k)|q≫k=−61630​π2​k2​P11​(k)​∫kUV∞𝑑q​P11​(q).2P_{13}(k)\Big|_{q\gg k}=-\frac{61}{630\pi^{2}}\;k^{2}\,P_{11}(k)\int_{k_{\mathrm{UV}}}^{\infty}dq\;P_{11}(q)\,.(116)

In contrast toP22P_{22}, this contribution is proportional toP11​(k)P_{11}(k), i.e. it modulates the linear power spectrum. Recalling
the displacement dispersionσd2\sigma_{d}^{2}introduced in Chapter 2, we
can rewrite it as2​P13​(k)|q≫k=−61105​k2​σd,UV2​P11​(k),σd,UV2≡16​π2​∫kUV∞𝑑q​P11​(q),2P_{13}(k)\Big|_{q\gg k}=-\frac{61}{105}\,k^{2}\,\sigma_{d,\mathrm{UV}}^{2}\,P_{11}(k)\,,\qquad\sigma_{d,\mathrm{UV}}^{2}\equiv\frac{1}{6\pi^{2}}\int_{k_{\mathrm{UV}}}^{\infty}dq\;P_{11}(q)\,,(117)

i.e.2​P13UV∼k2​P11​(k)​⟨|𝚿|2⟩UV2P_{13}^{\rm UV}\sim k^{2}\,P_{11}(k)\,\langle|{\bm{\Psi}}|^{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{UV}}:
the linear spectrum is modulated by the displacements generated by
the small-scale modes. This is the same physics we encountered in the
IR discussion — random displacements smear the correlations of
modekk— except that now
the displacements are sourced by the short modes, so there is no
symmetry that forces this effect to cancel.
The upshot is that the leading coupling to small scale modes
is via the displacement variance. This leading asymptotic
displayed in Fig.10,
in which we setkUV=0k_{\mathrm{UV}}=0appropriate in thek→0k\to 0limit.
We see
that this UV asymptotic dominatesΔ​P1−loop\Delta P_{\rm 1-loop}fork≲10−2​h​Mpc−1k\lesssim 10^{-2}~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}.

As far as convergence is concerned,
for a power-law universe
the above integral diverges forν≥−1\nu\geq-1.
InΛ\LambdaCDM, whereP11​(q)P_{11}(q)decays asq−3​ln2⁡qq^{-3}\ln^{2}q,
this UV
integral happened to converge, just likeP22P_{22}.
Historically, the cosmological community at this point
made the same mistake as particle physicists some fifty years
earlier.
Whether the loop integrals converge or diverge
does not matter – their small scale part is not reliably
described by the underlying theory anyway. Therefore, one needs to
renormalize these integrals in any case.
Indeed,
the regionq≳kNLq\gtrsim k_{\mathrm{NL}}containsO​(1)O(1)fluctuations, for which
neither the perturbative expansion nor the underlying pressureless
perfect fluid equations are valid.
Whatever SPT returns for this part of the integral is simply wrong.555There were signs though: it was known that
the SPT predictions blow up for a power-law power spectrum withν≥−1\nu\geq-1,
while the N-body simulations produce a perfectly normal structure for these spectra, see e.g. Fig. 3 of[39]forν=0\nu=0.

The crucial observation is that we do not need to know the details
of the small-scale dynamics to understand its effects: as
Eqs. (115)–(116) show, the small-scale
region contributes to the large-scale power spectrum only through
terms with an analytic, universalkk-dependence,k2​P11​(k)k^{2}P_{11}(k)andk4k^{4}, multiplied by some unknown numbers that depend on the small scale power spectrum.
We can therefore cut off the loop
integrals at some scaleΛ\Lambdaand replace the untrusted
contribution by a term of exactly this form with a free coefficient,
e.g. forP13P_{13}:k2​P11​(k)​∫kUV∞𝑑q​P11​(q)⟶k2​P11​(k)​∫kUVΛ𝑑q​P11​(q)+CΛ​k2​P11​(k).k^{2}P_{11}(k)\!\int_{k_{\mathrm{UV}}}^{\infty}dq\;P_{11}(q)\;\longrightarrow\;k^{2}P_{11}(k)\!\int_{k_{\mathrm{UV}}}^{\Lambda}dq\;P_{11}(q)\;+\;C_{\Lambda}\,k^{2}\,P_{11}(k)\,.(118)

The coefficientCΛC_{\Lambda}encodes the true (non-perturbative)
small-scale physics; it cannot be computed within the theory and
must depend onΛ\Lambdain such a way that the total result is
cutoff-independent. In other words:

We need counterterms!

Where do such terms come from in the EFT? From the effective stress
tensorτi​j\tau_{ij}— the subject of the next chapter.

## 4Chapter 4: Effective Stress Tensor for Dark Matter

We concluded the previous chapter with the observation that the SPT
loop integrals receive contributions from UV modesq≳kNLq\gtrsim k_{\mathrm{NL}},
for which perturbation theory is meaningless, and that this UV
sensitivity shows up as cutoff-dependent terms of the formCΛ​k2​P11​(k)C_{\Lambda}\,k^{2}\,P_{11}(k). In this chapter we show that the
effective stress tensorτi​j\tau_{ij}, which we introduced on symmetry
grounds in the equations of motion but have ignored so far, supplies
precisely the counterterms needed to cure this problem. Along the way
we will encounter an important structural novelty of the EFT of LSS:
the stress tensor is non-local in time.

## 4.1The Effective Sound Speed

Our starting point is the Euler (Navier–Stokes) equation with the
effective stress tensor,ρ​D​viD​τ=−ρ​∂iΦ+∂jτi​j.\rho\frac{Dv^{i}}{D\tau}=-\rho\,\partial^{i}\Phi+\partial^{j}\tau_{ij}\,.(119)

What isτi​j\tau_{ij}? To build up some intuition, let us start
(naively) with the expression suggested by ordinary fluid dynamics,τi​j=−p​δi​j+ρ​ζ​δi​j​(∂kvk)+ρ​μ​(∂ivj+∂jvi−23​δi​j​∂kvk),\tau_{ij}=-p\,\delta_{ij}+\rho\,\zeta\,\delta_{ij}\,(\partial_{k}v^{k})+\rho\,\mu\!\left(\partial_{i}v_{j}+\partial_{j}v_{i}-\tfrac{2}{3}\,\delta_{ij}\,\partial_{k}v^{k}\right)\,,(120)

i.e. pressure plus bulk (ζ\zeta) and shear (μ\mu) viscosity terms.
For an adiabatic fluidp=cs2​ρp=c_{s}^{2}\rho. Let us now evaluate this
expression on the linear-theory growing mode, for whichvi(1)=−f​ℋ​∂iδ1Δ=−f​ℋ​∂iΦ^(1),Δ​Φ^≡δ,v^{(1)}_{i}=-f\mathcal{H}\,\frac{\partial_{i}\delta_{1}}{\Delta}=-f\mathcal{H}\,\partial_{i}\hat{\Phi}^{(1)}\,,\qquad\Delta\hat{\Phi}\equiv\delta\,,(121)

where we used the Poisson equationΔ​Φ=32​Ωm​ℋ2​δ\Delta\Phi=\frac{3}{2}\Omega_{m}\mathcal{H}^{2}\deltaand introduced the
rescaled potentialΦ^=2​Φ/(3​Ωm​ℋ2)\hat{\Phi}=2\Phi/(3\Omega_{m}\mathcal{H}^{2}). The
velocity gradients then reduce to second derivatives of the
potential, and Eq. (120) takes the formτi​jρ¯≃−cs2​δi​j​(1+δ)+ζ~​δi​j​δ+μ~​∂i∂jΦ^,\frac{\tau_{ij}}{\bar{\rho}}\simeq-c_{s}^{2}\,\delta_{ij}\,(1+\delta)+\tilde{\zeta}\,\delta_{ij}\,\delta+\tilde{\mu}\;\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\hat{\Phi}\,,(122)

where we absorbed the background factors (ff,ℋ\mathcal{H}, etc.) into
the redefined constantsζ~\tilde{\zeta},μ~\tilde{\mu}. This simple
exercise teaches us an important structural lesson: at leading order
in perturbations and gradients, the symmetries allow exactly two
operators,δi​j​δ\delta_{ij}\,\deltaand thetidal tensor∂i∂jΦ^\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\hat{\Phi}.
At the same timeδ≡Δ​Φ^\delta\equiv\Delta\hat{\Phi},
so the right building block, i.e. the degree of freedom,
is only∂i∂jΦ^\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\hat{\Phi}(at the lowest order).
Taking the divergence and using the
Poisson equation once again, both collapse onto a single structure,∂jτi​jρ¯=(−cs2+ζ~+μ~)​∂iδ≡−cs,eff2​(τ)​∂iδ.\frac{\partial^{j}\tau_{ij}}{\bar{\rho}}=\left(-c_{s}^{2}+\tilde{\zeta}+\tilde{\mu}\right)\partial_{i}\delta\equiv-c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(\tau)\;\partial_{i}\delta\,.(123)

Only one combination of the fluid parameters is observable at this
order: theeffective sound speedcs,eff2c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}. This
is the universality of the EFT at work — whatever the microscopic
physics generatingτi​j\tau_{ij}, its leading-order effect is captured
by a single number.

Inserting Eq. (123) into the Euler equation and
repeating the steps that led to the SPT system
(61)–(62) (Fourier transform, time
variableη=ln⁡D+\eta=\ln D_{+}, EdS approximation), we obtain∂ηΘ𝒌−32​δ𝒌+12​Θ𝒌=∫β​Θ𝒒1​Θ𝒒2−k2​cs,eff2ℋ2​δ𝒌,\partial_{\eta}\Theta_{{\bm{k}}}-\frac{3}{2}\,\delta_{{\bm{k}}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\Theta_{{\bm{k}}}=\int\beta\,\Theta_{{\bm{q}}_{1}}\Theta_{{\bm{q}}_{2}}-\frac{k^{2}\,c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}}{\mathcal{H}^{2}}\,\delta_{{\bm{k}}}\,,(124)

where the factors offf,Ωm\Omega_{m}have been absorbed intocs,eff2c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}; they will play no role in what follows.

The new term is a linear source, so its effect can be computed
exactly with the retarded Green’s function of the linearized
fluid equations. Writingδ=δSPT+Δ​δ(cs2)\delta=\delta_{\mathrm{SPT}}+\Delta\delta^{(c_{s}^{2})}and using the linear continuity equation∂ηδ𝒌=Θ𝒌\partial_{\eta}\delta_{{\bm{k}}}=\Theta_{{\bm{k}}}to eliminateΘ\Theta,
Eq. (124) reduces, at leading order in perturbations,
to a second-order equation withconstantcoefficients and a
source evaluated on the linear solution:(∂η2+12​∂η−32)​Δ​δ𝒌(cs2)​(η)=−k2​cs,eff2​(η)ℋ2​(η)​δ1​(𝒌,η).\left(\partial_{\eta}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{\eta}-\frac{3}{2}\right)\Delta\delta^{(c_{s}^{2})}_{{\bm{k}}}(\eta)=-\,k^{2}\,\frac{c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(\eta)}{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\eta)}\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}},\eta)\,.(125)

The retarded Green’s function of this operator is derived in
AppendixB: thanks to the constant coefficients in the EdS regime, it
depends only ons=η−η′s=\eta-\eta^{\prime},gη​(s)=25​ΘH​(s)​[es−e−3​s/2],g_{\eta}(s)=\frac{2}{5}\,\Theta_{H}(s)\left[e^{s}-e^{-3s/2}\right]\,,(126)

whereΘH\Theta_{H}is the Heaviside function. Hence, usingδ1​(𝒌,η′)=eη′​δ0​(𝒌)\delta_{1}({\bm{k}},\eta^{\prime})=e^{\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{0}({\bm{k}}),Δ​δ𝒌(cs2)​(η)=−k2​∫−∞η𝑑η′​gη​(η−η′)​cs,eff2​(η′)ℋ2​(η′)​eη′​δ0​(𝒌)≡−k2​γ​(η)​δ1​(𝒌),\Delta\delta^{(c_{s}^{2})}_{{\bm{k}}}(\eta)=-\,k^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\eta}d\eta^{\prime}\;g_{\eta}(\eta-\eta^{\prime})\;\frac{c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(\eta^{\prime})}{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\eta^{\prime})}\;e^{\eta^{\prime}}\,\delta_{0}({\bm{k}})\equiv-k^{2}\gamma(\eta)\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\,,(127)

where we made the factorization of time and scale dependence manifest.
In principle, we do not need to compute the time-dependence
ofγ​(η)\gamma(\eta), which isa priorinot calculable in EFT.
We can simply treat it as a non-perturbative
input to be extracted from data. However, it is
instructive to compute it explicitly in some simple cases.
Sincecs,eff2c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}is generated by the
small-scale fluctuations, whose amplitude grows with time, it is
natural to parameterize it as a power of the growth factor,cs,eff2​(η)ℋ2​(η)=ℓ02​em​η=ℓ02​D+m,m>0,\frac{c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(\eta)}{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\eta)}=\ell_{0}^{2}\,e^{m\eta}=\ell_{0}^{2}\,D_{+}^{m}\,,\qquad m>0\,,(128)

where positivity ofmmguarantees that the source switches off at
early times and the integral converges. The integral is then
elementary:∫−∞η𝑑η′​25​[eη−η′−e−32​(η−η′)]​e(m+1)​η′=25​[1m−1m+5/2]​e(m+1)​η=e(m+1)​ηm​(m+52),\int_{-\infty}^{\eta}d\eta^{\prime}\;\frac{2}{5}\left[e^{\eta-\eta^{\prime}}-e^{-\frac{3}{2}(\eta-\eta^{\prime})}\right]e^{(m+1)\eta^{\prime}}=\frac{2}{5}\left[\frac{1}{m}-\frac{1}{m+5/2}\right]e^{(m+1)\eta}=\frac{e^{(m+1)\eta}}{m\left(m+\tfrac{5}{2}\right)}\,,(129)

so that the correction is proportional to the linear field at thesametime,Δ​δ𝒌(cs2)​(η)=−k2​γ​(η)​δ1​(𝒌,η),γ​(η)=2m​(2​m+5)​cs,eff2​(η)ℋ2​(η)\Delta\delta^{(c_{s}^{2})}_{{\bm{k}}}(\eta)=-\,k^{2}\,\gamma(\eta)\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}},\eta)\,,\qquad\boxed{\;\gamma(\eta)=\frac{2}{m\,(2m+5)}\;\frac{c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(\eta)}{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\eta)}\;}(130)

Thusγ\gammais the instantaneous value ofcs,eff2/ℋ2c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}/\mathcal{H}^{2}(which has dimensions of length
squared), suppressed by anO​(1)O(1)number that encodes the memory of
the growth history.

Which value ofmmshould we use? Renormalization provides an
answer. As we will see below,γ\gammamust cancel a
cutoff-dependent term proportional to∫kNLΛ𝑑q​P11​(q;η)∝D+2​(η)\int_{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}^{\Lambda}dq\,P_{11}(q;\eta)\propto D_{+}^{2}(\eta), so
at least part of the counterterm must carrym=2m=2, for whichγ​(η)=19​cs,eff2​(η)ℋ2​(η).\gamma(\eta)=\frac{1}{9}\,\frac{c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(\eta)}{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\eta)}\,.(131)

Other values ofmmare relevant too, e.g. the finite part of the
countertermγfin\gamma_{\rm fin}(discussed shortly) capturing the physical
small scale response has a different scaling.
As a byproduct, the matrix propagator of
AppendixBalso delivers thevelocitycounterterm generated by the same source, withΔ​Θ(cs2)=(m+1)​Δ​δ(cs2)\Delta\Theta^{(c_{s}^{2})}=(m+1)\,\Delta\delta^{(c_{s}^{2})}: the ratio(m+1)≠1(m+1)\neq 1signals the decaying-mode admixture induced by the
stress tensor (a pure growing mode hasΘ/δ=1\Theta/\delta=1).

The correction to the power
spectrum is⟨(δSPT+Δ​δ(cs2))​(δSPT+Δ​δ(cs2))⟩′=P11+P22+2​P13−2​k2​γ​P11​(k)+k4​γ2​P11​(k).\langle\left(\delta_{\mathrm{SPT}}+\Delta\delta^{(c_{s}^{2})}\right)\left(\delta_{\mathrm{SPT}}+\Delta\delta^{(c_{s}^{2})}\right)\rangle^{\prime}=P_{11}+P_{22}+2P_{13}-2k^{2}\gamma\,P_{11}(k)+k^{4}\gamma^{2}\,P_{11}(k)\,.(132)

The last term is of the size of a two-loop contribution and can be
dropped at the one-loop order we work. The crucial observation is that thecounterterm−2​k2​γ​P11​(k)-2k^{2}\gamma\,P_{11}(k)has exactly the samekk-dependence as the UV limit (116) of2​P132P_{13}— precisely
what is needed to absorb the UV sensitivity of the loop. We will
complete this renormalization program below, but first we have to
deal with an important subtlety.Figure 12:Left:effective sound speedestimatorγ^\hat{\gamma}, showing the prediction for this parameter from individual (independent)kkbins from the N-body data of[16]. The sound speed is constantγ∼1.8​[h​Mpc−1]2\gamma\sim 1.8~[\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}]^{2}on large scales. The apparent scale-dependence
atk≳0.13​h​Mpc−1k\gtrsim 0.13~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}is the effect of two-loop corrections not included in the estimator.Right:the stochastic power spectrum from theQuijoteHR simulation suite[40]. On large scalesPstochP_{\rm stoch}follows the EFTk4k^{4}asymptotic behavior. Note thatPstochP_{\rm stoch}gets shallower aroundk∼0.1​h​Mpc−1k\sim 0.1~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}relevant for observations,
which implies a breakdown of the power-law expansion.

## 4.2Time Non-Locality

Hold on! In writing Eq. (120) we tacitly assumed thatτi​j\tau_{ij}at timeτ\taudepends on the fields at the same timeτ\tauonly. For ordinary fluids this is justified: the derivative
expansion is controlled byℓmfp​∂∂x∼k​ℓmfp≪1,τcoll​∂∂t∼ω​τcoll≪1,\ell_{\mathrm{mfp}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\sim k\,\ell_{\mathrm{mfp}}\ll 1\,,\qquad\tau_{\mathrm{coll}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\sim\omega\,\tau_{\mathrm{coll}}\ll 1\,,(133)

and sinceω=cs​k\omega=c_{s}kwhilecs​τcoll=ℓmfpc_{s}\tau_{\mathrm{coll}}=\ell_{\mathrm{mfp}}, the
two expansion parameters coincide: time derivatives and gradients are
equivalent, and both expansions converge. Physically, the fluid
relaxes to local equilibrium on the collision timeτcoll\tau_{\mathrm{coll}}, which is much shorter than the period of the
sound wave — the fluid response is effectively instantaneous.
That’s why we can use a local in time expansion for the stress tensor
from the get-go.

In LSS the analogue of the relaxation time is the dynamical time of
the small-scale structure formation, which is of the order of the Hubble time,
i.e. precisely the timescale on which the long-wavelength
perturbations evolve:Δ​τ​∂τδ1∼1ℋ⋅ℋ​δ1∼O​(1)⋅δ1.\Delta\tau\;\partial_{\tau}\,\delta_{1}\sim\frac{1}{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\mathcal{H}\,\delta_{1}\sim O(1)\cdot\delta_{1}\,.(134)

A Taylor expansion in time derivatives thereforedoes not
converge: the response of the small-scale structure to a
long-wavelength perturbation is spread over the entire history of the
Universe. The stress tensor must be written as a functional of the
fields on the past trajectory[23,41],666A remark on notation: the spatial derivatives inside the
memory integral are taken with respect to the spatial argument at
timeτ′\tau^{\prime}and only then evaluated on the trajectory, i.e.Δ​δ​(𝒙fl​[𝒙,τ;τ′],τ′)≡(Δ𝒚​δ)​(𝒚,τ′)|𝒚=𝒙fl​(τ′)\Delta\delta({\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}}[{\bm{x}},\tau;\tau^{\prime}],\tau^{\prime})\equiv\left(\Delta_{{\bm{y}}}\,\delta\right)({\bm{y}},\tau^{\prime})\big|_{{\bm{y}}={\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}}(\tau^{\prime})},
as dictated by the equivalence principle: the response is built from
quantities measurable in the local frame of the fluid element at the
past time. In contrast, the outer derivatives in∂i∂jτi​j​(𝒙,τ)\partial^{i}\partial^{j}\tau_{ij}({\bm{x}},\tau)act at the field point𝒙{\bm{x}}at timeτ\tau. Differentiating the composite function with
respect to𝒙{\bm{x}}instead would generate Jacobian factors∂xflk/∂xi=δik+O​(∂Ψ)\partial x^{k}_{\mathrm{fl}}/\partial x^{i}=\delta^{k}_{i}+O(\partial\Psi);
since∂iΨj\partial_{i}\Psi^{j}-type terms are themselves allowed operators,
the two conventions differ only by a reshuffling of higher-order
Wilson coefficients, and the distinction drops out entirely at the
linear order used below. The same convention is understood in the
bias functional of §6.2.∂i∂jτi​jρ¯​(𝒙,τ)=∫τiτ𝑑τ′​K​(τ,τ′)​Δ​δ​(𝒙fl​[𝒙,τ;τ′],τ′),\frac{\partial^{i}\partial^{j}\tau_{ij}}{\bar{\rho}}({\bm{x}},\tau)=\int_{\tau_{i}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\;K(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\;\Delta\delta\!\left({\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}}[{\bm{x}},\tau;\tau^{\prime}],\tau^{\prime}\right)\,,(135)

whereK​(τ,τ′)K(\tau,\tau^{\prime})is a memory kernel with support over a few
Hubble times. Crucially, by the equivalence principle the response is
carried along by the fluid element, so the density must be evaluated
on the fluid trajectory𝒙fl{\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}}defined byd​𝒙fl​(τ′)d​τ′=𝒗​[𝒙fl​(τ′),τ′],𝒙fl​(τ)=𝒙⟹𝒙−𝒙fl​(τ′)=∫τ′τ𝑑τ1​𝒗​[𝒙fl​(τ1),τ1].\frac{d{\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}}(\tau^{\prime})}{d\tau^{\prime}}={\bm{v}}[{\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}}(\tau^{\prime}),\tau^{\prime}]\,,\qquad{\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}}(\tau)={\bm{x}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad{\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}}(\tau^{\prime})=\int_{\tau^{\prime}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\;{\bm{v}}[{\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}}(\tau_{1}),\tau_{1}]\,.(136)

Since the displacements are perturbatively small, we can expand:∂i∂jτi​jρ¯\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{i}\partial^{j}\tau_{ij}}{\bar{\rho}}=∫τiτ𝑑τ′​K​(τ,τ′)​[Δ​δ​(𝒙,τ′)−∂iΔ​δ​(𝒙,τ′)​∫τ′τ𝑑τ1​vi​(𝒙,τ1)+⋯],\displaystyle=\int_{\tau_{i}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\;K(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\left[\Delta\delta({\bm{x}},\tau^{\prime})-\partial_{i}\Delta\delta({\bm{x}},\tau^{\prime})\int_{\tau^{\prime}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\;v^{i}({\bm{x}},\tau_{1})+\cdots\right]\,,(137)

where the second term isO​(δ12)O(\delta_{1}^{2})and contributes only at
higher orders in perturbation theory. At linear order we can useδ1​(𝒙,τ′)=D+​(τ′)​δ0​(𝒙)\delta_{1}({\bm{x}},\tau^{\prime})=D_{+}(\tau^{\prime})\,\delta_{0}({\bm{x}}), and the time
integral factorizes:∫τiτ𝑑τ′​K​(τ,τ′)​Δ​δ1​(𝒙,τ′)=[1D+​(τ)​∫τiτ𝑑τ′​K​(τ,τ′)​D+​(τ′)]​D+​(τ)​Δ​δ0​(𝒙)≡cs,eff2​(τ)​Δ​δ1​(𝒙,τ).\int_{\tau_{i}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\;K(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\;\Delta\delta_{1}({\bm{x}},\tau^{\prime})=\left[\frac{1}{D_{+}(\tau)}\int_{\tau_{i}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\;K(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\,D_{+}(\tau^{\prime})\right]D_{+}(\tau)\,\Delta\delta_{0}({\bm{x}})\equiv c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(\tau)\;\Delta\delta_{1}({\bm{x}},\tau)\,.(138)

The whole memory kernel has collapsed into a single time-dependent
coefficient: at this order the non-local-in-time stress tensor is
completely indistinguishable from the local sound speed ansatz of the
previous section.Locality in time emerges order by order in
perturbation theory, with the non-locality absorbed into the (time
dependence of the) EFT parameters.
Can we keep doing this simplifications forever?Yes,as long as perturbation theory is applicable.
Is it messy at higher order?Yes, but there exist efficient
algorithms to deal with it systematically[42],
which do not distinguish between the local and non-local in time
operators.
In addition,
there is a Lagrangian formulation of perturbation
theory and EFT, in which time non-locality
is automatically implemented. More on this in Chapter 7.

Notice that the term∼∂iδ⋅vi\sim\partial_{i}\delta\cdot v^{i}in the
expansion (137) naively “breaks” Galilean
symmetry (correlation functions are of course fine); it can be
eliminated by a coordinate redefinition, i.e. by passing to
Lagrangian coordinates.

## 4.3UV Matching

One-loop renormalization.Let us see how the sound speed fixes the UV problem
of SPT.
Consider the one-loop integralI13≡∫𝒒F3​(𝒌,𝒒,−𝒒)​P11​(q)I_{13}\equiv\int_{\bm{q}}F_{3}({\bm{k}},{\bm{q}},-{\bm{q}})\,P_{11}(q).
It receives contributions from all scales.
Let’s split the integration domain:I13=∫0kNLd3​q(2​π)3​F3​P11​(q)⏟calculable (perturbative)+∫kNLΛd3​q(2​π)3​F3​P11​(q)⏟not trustworthy.I_{13}=\underbrace{\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;F_{3}\,P_{11}(q)}_{\text{calculable (perturbative)}}+\underbrace{\int_{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}^{\Lambda}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;F_{3}\,P_{11}(q)}_{\text{not trustworthy}}\,.(139)

The first part of the integral is called “calculable” since
it depends on the perturbative modes for which our
theory is correct.
Hence, this part of the integral we can trust.
Note, however, that it’s not entirely clear if the right
scale there should bekNLk_{\mathrm{NL}}, or perhapskNL/5k_{\mathrm{NL}}/5. Any
uncertainty in this scale will be compensated by the
counterterm. At this point it is important to stress that
even divergent integrals have trustworthy pieces, which can be
roughly estimated by settingkUV∼kNLk_{\mathrm{UV}}\sim k_{\mathrm{NL}}. These kinds of estimates
are known as “naturalness arguments” in particle physics, and
they can be used to estimate the sizes of various effects, e.g.
the masses of the charm quark and the charged pions.

The UV part (q>kNLq>k_{\mathrm{NL}}) involves non-perturbative physics: the
modes there do not obey the perturbative equations of motion, so
this part of the integral is wrong and, on top of that, cutoff-dependent.
The cutoff is an artifact of our computation.
The physical prediction should not depend on it.
Using the UV
limit (116), the untrusted part of2​P132P_{13}is2​P13​(k)|kNLΛ≈−61630​π2​k2​P11​(k)​∫kNLΛ𝑑q​P11​(q),2P_{13}(k)\Big|_{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}^{\Lambda}\approx-\frac{61}{630\pi^{2}}\;k^{2}\,P_{11}(k)\int_{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}^{\Lambda}dq\;P_{11}(q)\,,(140)

which has exactly thekk-dependence of the counterterm in
Eq. (132). (The UV part ofP22P_{22}scales ask4k^{4}and
will be dealt with by the stochastic counterterm in the next
chapter.) We therefore split the EFT parameter asγ=γinf​(Λ)+γfin,γinf​(Λ)=−611260​π2​∫kNLΛ𝑑q​P11​(q),\gamma=\gamma_{\mathrm{inf}}(\Lambda)+\gamma_{\mathrm{fin}}\,,\qquad\gamma_{\mathrm{inf}}(\Lambda)=-\frac{61}{1260\pi^{2}}\int_{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}^{\Lambda}dq\;P_{11}(q)\,,(141)

so that theΛ\Lambda-dependent parts cancel, leavingPEFT1​-loop​(k)=P11​(k)+P22​(k)+2​P13​(k)|0kNL−2​γfin​(kNL)​k2​P11​(k).P_{\mathrm{EFT}}^{1\text{-loop}}(k)=P_{11}(k)+P_{22}(k)+2P_{13}(k)\Big|_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}-2\gamma_{\mathrm{fin}}(k_{\mathrm{NL}})\,k^{2}\,P_{11}(k)\,.(142)

The finite partγfin\gamma_{\mathrm{fin}}is a genuinely new input: it
encodes the true contribution of the non-perturbative small-scale
physics, which the EFT cannot predict. It has to be fitted to data or
measured inNN-body simulations.
This procedure is calledUV matching.
Note also thatγfin\gamma_{\mathrm{fin}}depends on the arbitrary split scalekNLk_{\mathrm{NL}}(such dependence is called the scheme-dependence); only the total, renormalized result is physical.

In practice one often does not bother introducing the intermediate scale at
all: the loop integrals are evaluated withΛ→∞\Lambda\to\infty, and
the fitted counterterm automatically absorbs both the UV garbage and
the true finite contribution. This is nothing but a particular
(and convenient) choice of scheme.

Higher-loop scaling problem.At each loop order, the SPT UV integrals introduce factors of the mass variance:σ2​(Q)=∫0Qd3​q(2​π)3​P11​(q),\sigma^{2}(Q)=\int_{0}^{Q}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;P_{11}(q)\,,(143)

which is𝒪​(1)\mathcal{O}(1)forQ∼kNLQ\sim k_{\mathrm{NL}}. Specifically we have the leading scaling:Δ​PL−loopSPT∼k2​P11​(k)​∫kNLΛ𝑑q​P11​(q)​σ2​L−2​(q).\Delta P^{\rm SPT}_{\rm L-loop}\sim k^{2}P_{11}(k)\int_{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}^{\Lambda}dq~P_{11}(q)\sigma^{2L-2}(q)~\,.(144)

Whileσ2​(q)\sigma^{2}(q)diverges∝ln3⁡(q)\propto\ln^{3}(q), the above
integrals converge, but to progressively larger numbers,
which grow as(3​L−1)!/23​L(3L-1)!/2^{3L}[17].
So the leading UV contributions at each loop order areall the same size, which explains the numerical result of Fig.9:P2​-loopUV∼P1​-loopUV,P3​-loopUV>P1​-loopUV+P2​-loopUV.P_{\mathrm{2\text{-loop}}}^{\mathrm{UV}}\sim P_{\mathrm{1\text{-loop}}}^{\mathrm{UV}}\,,\qquad P_{\mathrm{3\text{-loop}}}^{\mathrm{UV}}>P_{\mathrm{1\text{-loop}}}^{\mathrm{UV}}+P_{\mathrm{2\text{-loop}}}^{\mathrm{UV}}\,.(145)

The SPT perturbative seriesdoes not convergein the UV.
The EFT counterterms must absorb this UV sensitivity order by order.
Note that every new SPT loop simply re-defines the countertermγ\gamma.
Indeed, thekk-dependence of all of them is exactlyk2​P11k^{2}P_{11}.
Since we already have this operator included at the one-loop order,
the new UV corrections are totally redundant – they do not require new
counterterms. In other words, these re-definitions are completely unobservable,
and hence we can simply choose a scheme in which these parts of higher loop
corrections are identically set to zero. One says that the
redundant parts are subtracted from the loop integrals.
What remains calculable after this subtractions
are the IR-dominated parts of the loops,
which, as we will see in the next chapter, are suppressed by powers
ofk/kNL≪1k/k_{\mathrm{NL}}\ll 1. Thus, the strong UV sensitivity of SPT
loops is an artifact of the perfect fluid description.
After renormalization the loops organize a well behaved
parametric series. This agrees well with
simulation-based results that found that the actual
power spectrum
is very weakly sensitive to small scale dynamics[43,44], in full agreement with the EFT-based expectations.

EFT fitting in practice.The one-loop EFT power spectrum in theΛ→∞\Lambda\to\inftyscheme is:PEFT1​-loop​(k)=P11​(k)+P22SPT​(k)+2​P13SPT​(k)−2​γ​k2​P11​(k),P_{\mathrm{EFT}}^{1\text{-loop}}(k)=P_{11}(k)+P_{22}^{\mathrm{SPT}}(k)+2P_{13}^{\mathrm{SPT}}(k)-2\gamma\,k^{2}\,P_{11}(k)\,,(146)

whereγ\gammais a free parameter (the time-averaged effective sound
speed squared, in units of(Mpc/h)2(\mathrm{Mpc}/h)^{2}).
The counterterm−2​γ​k2​P11-2\gamma\,k^{2}\,P_{11}cancels theΛ\Lambda-dependent part ofP13P_{13}.
Fitting to Horizon RunNN-body simulation[16,20,31]atz=0z=0givesγ≈1.8​(Mpc/h)2\gamma\approx 1.8~\,(\mathrm{Mpc}/h)^{2}, see the right panel of12, where we show
theestimatorforγ\gammafor individualkkbins. We see
that the estimator is scale-independent up tok∼0.15​h/Mpck\sim 0.15\,h/\mathrm{Mpc},
after which it picks up a scale dependence due to the two-loop
corrections omitted in the theory prediction[45]. Nevertheless, the
consistency ofγ\gammaacross independent bins is an important
test of EFT: once the scheme is fixed, this parameter
should take a well determined constant value.

Figure13summarizes this section.
The left panel shows the failure of SPT to fit the N-body data
on all scales: the one-loop SPT prediction overshoots the data,
while the two-loop one undershoots. The right panel displays
the EFT results: the power spectrum prediction isΛ\Lambda-independent
and agrees with simulations up tok≃0.15​h/Mpck\simeq 0.15\,h/\mathrm{Mpc}.
Once upgraded to two-loops[23,45,46,47,48,49], EFT fits the data up tokmax≃0.25​h​Mpc−1k_{\rm max}\simeq 0.25~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}.Figure 13:Dark-matter power spectrum atz=0z=0, divided by the linear
power spectrumPlin​(k)P_{\rm lin}(k).Left:Standard Perturbation Theory (SPT). The one-loop
correction (light blue) overshoots the N-body data (black points)
already atk≳0.05​h​Mpc−1k\gtrsim 0.05\,h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}, and the
two-loop result (red) develops unphysical oscillations without
converging toward the data.Right:The one-loop EFT result, which includes the
counterterm−2​γ​k2​Plin​(k)-2\gamma\,k^{2}P_{\rm lin}(k)withγ\gammafit to simulations, agrees with the N-body data to sub-percent
accuracy up tok≃0.15​h​Mpc−1k\simeq 0.15\,h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}. The
two-loop EFT result extends the agreement tok≃0.25​h​Mpc−1k\simeq 0.25\,h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}. The failure of SPT at
highkkis a direct consequence of the missing UV counterterms:
the loop integrals receive uncontrolled contributions from
short-scale modes that are absorbed into the EFT parameters.

Baryonic effects.It is worth mentioning that
baryonic feedback on dark matter (i.e. backreaction due
to star formation etc.) can also be included
in EFT by considering baryons and dark matter as two separate fluids
with different stress tensors[50].
The net effect is simply a shift of the dark matter countertermγ→γ+γb\gamma\to\gamma+\gamma_{b}, whereγb\gamma_{b}is the baryonic
contribution. Intuitively, this can
be understood as coming from a simple Taylor expansion of the
Jeans suppression in linear theoryδ1​e−k2kJ2≈δ1​(1−k2kJ2).\delta_{1}e^{-\frac{k^{2}}{k_{J}^{2}}}\approx\delta_{1}\left(1-\frac{k^{2}}{k_{J}^{2}}\right)\,.

## A historic remark.

At the beginning, the presence of fitting
parameters for EFT dark matter predictions was considered a tragedy
by many non-EFT researches. How come a theory can’t predict everything?
In the EFT framework it is quite OK: we acknowledge
that small-scales are difficult, but in the large-scale limit
all of their complexity can be
captured by a few parameters only.
For pure N-body dynamics one can develop
more sophisticated UV models that allow one to estimate
the sound speed fairly accurately[51,52,53].
The point, however, is that for more complicated situations,
i.e. dynamics beyond N-body (e.g. ultralight axion dark matter),
or in the presence of baryonic feedback,
such estimates are much harder
to make, so one has to resort to fitting EFT parameters from the data.
In addition, once we start looking at the galaxies, there will be even
more EFT parameters whose accurate
semi-analytic predictions are very challenging. Thus, fitting
them from the data is universally acknowledged as a normal practice.

## 4.4Stochastic Effects

In the previous chapter we renormalized thek2​P11​(k)k^{2}P_{11}(k)UV
sensitivity of the one-loop power spectrum using the response of the
effective stress tensor to the long-wavelength fields. This, however,
cannot be the whole story: the UV limit ofP22P_{22},
Eq. (115), produced ak4k^{4}contribution completely
independent ofP11​(k)P_{11}(k), for which no counterterm has appeared so
far. The missing ingredient is that the symmetries allowτi​j\tau_{ij}to contain pieces that are not determined by the long-wavelength
cosmological fields at all:τi​jρ¯⊃ε​δi​j+εi​j,⟨ε​δ1⟩=0,⟨εi​j​δ1⟩=0.\frac{\tau_{ij}}{\bar{\rho}}\supset\varepsilon\,\delta_{ij}+\varepsilon_{ij}\,,\qquad\langle\varepsilon\,\delta_{1}\rangle=0\,,\quad\langle\varepsilon_{ij}\,\delta_{1}\rangle=0\,.(147)

Thesestochasticfields represent the contributions of the
fully non-linear, virialized modes, which have decoupled from the
cosmological fields: their evolution has erased the memory of the
initial conditions, so from the point of view of the long modes they
behave as pure noise, uncorrelated withδ1\delta_{1}. For the one-loop
power spectrum the scalar and tensor components in
Eq. (147) are degenerate, so for convenience we keep
only the scalar piece,τi​j=ρ¯​ε​δi​j\tau_{ij}=\bar{\rho}\,\varepsilon\,\delta_{ij},
for which1ρ¯​∂i∂jτi​j=Δ​ε.\frac{1}{\bar{\rho}}\,\partial^{i}\partial^{j}\tau_{ij}=\Delta\varepsilon\,.(148)

The equation of motion then reads∂ηΘ𝒌−32​δ𝒌+12​Θ𝒌=∫β​Θ𝒒1​Θ𝒒2−k2​cs,eff2ℋ2​δ𝒌−k2ℋ2​ε𝒌,\partial_{\eta}\Theta_{{\bm{k}}}-\frac{3}{2}\,\delta_{{\bm{k}}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\Theta_{{\bm{k}}}=\int\beta\,\Theta_{{\bm{q}}_{1}}\Theta_{{\bm{q}}_{2}}-\frac{k^{2}c_{s,\mathrm{eff}}^{2}}{\mathcal{H}^{2}}\,\delta_{{\bm{k}}}-\frac{k^{2}}{\mathcal{H}^{2}}\,\varepsilon_{{\bm{k}}}\,,(149)

and solving it perturbatively with the same Green’s function as in
the previous chapter, we obtainΔ​δ𝒌(τ)=−k2​γ​δ1​(𝒌)−k2​ε​(𝒌),\Delta\delta^{(\tau)}_{{\bm{k}}}=-k^{2}\,\gamma\,\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})-k^{2}\,\varepsilon({\bm{k}})\,,(150)

where, with a slight abuse of notation,ε​(𝒌)\varepsilon({\bm{k}})in the
last expression denotes the time integral of the stochastic source
against the Green’s function. Since⟨ε​δ1⟩=0\langle\varepsilon\,\delta_{1}\rangle=0,
all cross terms vanish, and the power spectrum receives a new,
purely additive contribution:⟨|δSPT+Δ​δ(τ)|2⟩′=P11+P22+2​P13−2​k2​γ​P11​(k)+k4​⟨|ε​(𝒌)|2⟩′.\langle|\delta_{\mathrm{SPT}}+\Delta\delta^{(\tau)}|^{2}\rangle^{\prime}=P_{11}+P_{22}+2P_{13}-2k^{2}\gamma\,P_{11}(k)+k^{4}\,\langle|\varepsilon({\bm{k}})|^{2}\rangle^{\prime}\,.(151)

k4​⟨|ε​(𝒌)|2⟩′k^{4}\langle|\varepsilon({\bm{k}})|^{2}\rangle^{\prime}above is the
stochastic power spectrum of the matter
fieldPstochP_{\rm stoch}.
What can we say about the underlying power spectrum⟨|ε​(𝒌)|2⟩′\langle|\varepsilon({\bm{k}})|^{2}\rangle^{\prime}? Symmetries and
dimensional analysis are enough. The fieldε\varepsilonis generated
by the small-scale dynamics, with support atq≳kNLq\gtrsim k_{\mathrm{NL}}, so its
correlations extend only over distances∼1/kNL\sim 1/k_{\mathrm{NL}}. Fork≪kNLk\ll k_{\mathrm{NL}}its power spectrum must therefore be analytic inkk, and
rotational invariance forbids odd powers, yielding:⟨|ε​(𝒌)|2⟩′=A0+A1​k2+⋯\langle|\varepsilon({\bm{k}})|^{2}\rangle^{\prime}=A_{0}+A_{1}\,k^{2}+\cdots(152)

Combining everything, we arrive at thefinal one-loop EFT power spectrum:P1​-loopEFT=P11+P22+2​P13−2​k2​γ​P11​(k)+A0​k4+⋯\boxed{P_{1\text{-loop}}^{\mathrm{EFT}}=P_{11}+P_{22}+2P_{13}-2k^{2}\gamma\,P_{11}(k)+A_{0}\,k^{4}+\cdots}(153)

Renormalization.Let us verify that the new parameter completes the renormalization
program of the previous chapter. Isolating theΛ\Lambda-dependent
UV parts of the loops with the help of the asymptotics of §3.4,i22≡9196​π2​∫kNLΛd​qq2​P112​(q),i13≡611260​π2​∫kNLΛ𝑑q​P11​(q),i_{22}\equiv\frac{9}{196\pi^{2}}\int_{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}^{\Lambda}\frac{dq}{q^{2}}\;P_{11}^{2}(q)\,,\qquad i_{13}\equiv\frac{61}{1260\pi^{2}}\int_{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}^{\Lambda}dq\;P_{11}(q)\,,(154)

we can writeP1​-loopEFT=[P11+P22+2​P13]0kNL+k4​(A0+i22)−2​k2​(γ+i13)​P11​(k).P_{1\text{-loop}}^{\mathrm{EFT}}=\Big[P_{11}+P_{22}+2P_{13}\Big]_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}+k^{4}\left(A_{0}+i_{22}\right)-2k^{2}\left(\gamma+i_{13}\right)P_{11}(k)\,.(155)

The UV parts ofbothloop integrals now sit next to an EFT
parameter with exactly the samekk-dependence. SplittingA0=A0inf​(Λ)+A0finA_{0}=A_{0}^{\mathrm{inf}}(\Lambda)+A_{0}^{\mathrm{fin}}andγ=γinf​(Λ)+γfin\gamma=\gamma^{\mathrm{inf}}(\Lambda)+\gamma^{\mathrm{fin}}as
before, the cutoff dependence is completely removed — this is
renormalization at work. The finite part of the stochastic
contribution scales asPstoch​(k)=A0fin​k4.P_{\mathrm{stoch}}(k)=A_{0}^{\mathrm{fin}}\,k^{4}\,.(156)

It is instructive to compare this with the popular halo model, whose
one-halo term (the analog ofPstochP_{\mathrm{stoch}}) approaches aconstantat smallkk[54,55].
If you are reading this notes, you probably already know
which prediction is the correct one.
Precision measurements ofPstochP_{\mathrm{stoch}}from N-body
simulations
support thek4k^{4}scaling in thek→0k\to 0limit
predicted by the EFT,
see the
right panel of
Fig.12(more detail in[56,57,58]). This is not a coincidence.
As we now
show, the constantPstochP_{\mathrm{stoch}}is in conflict with basic conservation laws.

Peebles’ argument.Thek4k^{4}scaling of the stochastic power is not an accident of
perturbation theory — it is fixed by mass and momentum
conservation. Following Peebles[59](see also[42]), let’s model the small-scale structure as
a collection of particles of massesmnm_{n}at positions𝒙n{\bm{x}}_{n}, confined to a region of sizeR≪1/kR\ll 1/k.
These particles can be thought of as dark
matter particles inside a halo.
The small-scale non-perturbative
dynamics rearranges the particles,𝒙n→𝒙n+Δ​𝒙n{\bm{x}}_{n}\to{\bm{x}}_{n}+\Delta{\bm{x}}_{n},
generating a density perturbationδ​ρ​(𝒌)|stoch=∑nmn​ei​𝒌⋅(𝒙n+Δ​𝒙n)−∑nmn​ei​𝒌⋅𝒙n.\delta\rho({\bm{k}})\Big|_{\mathrm{stoch}}=\sum_{n}m_{n}\,e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot({\bm{x}}_{n}+\Delta{\bm{x}}_{n})}-\sum_{n}m_{n}\,e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}_{n}}\,.(157)

Expanding in𝒌⋅Δ​𝒙n≪1{\bm{k}}\cdot\Delta{\bm{x}}_{n}\ll 1,δ​ρ​(𝒌)|stoch=∑ni​𝒌⋅Δ​𝒙n​mn​ei​𝒌⋅𝒙n−12​∑n(𝒌⋅Δ​𝒙n)2​mn​ei​𝒌⋅𝒙n+⋯\delta\rho({\bm{k}})\Big|_{\mathrm{stoch}}=\sum_{n}i\,{\bm{k}}\cdot\Delta{\bm{x}}_{n}\;m_{n}\,e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}_{n}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n}\left({\bm{k}}\cdot\Delta{\bm{x}}_{n}\right)^{2}m_{n}\,e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}_{n}}+\cdots(158)

In the long-wavelength limit we can setei​𝒌⋅𝒙n→1e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}_{n}}\to 1inside the region. There is noO​(k0)O(k^{0})term because the total mass
of the region is conserved by the internal dynamics. TheO​(k)O(k)(dipole) term is proportional to∑nmn​Δ​𝒙n\sum_{n}m_{n}\,\Delta{\bm{x}}_{n}, i.e. to
the displacement of the center of mass — and this vanishes as
well, because internal gravitational forces cannot generate a net
momentum. The leading surviving contribution is the quadrupole:δ​ρρ|stoch∝k2⟹⟨|δ​ρρ|2⟩stoch∝k4.\frac{\delta\rho}{\rho}\Big|_{\mathrm{stoch}}\propto k^{2}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\langle\Big|\frac{\delta\rho}{\rho}\Big|^{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{stoch}}\propto k^{4}\,.(159)

The EFT recovers this symmetry-based result automatically: the
stochastic source enters the equations of motion only through∂i∂jτi​j\partial^{i}\partial^{j}\tau_{ij}, which supplies the two powers ofkkat the level of the field.

Before closing this Section let us mention
that if a small-scale reshuffling is correlated with a
long-wavelength density fieldδ1​(𝒌)\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}), it will
produce an effective sound speed contribution∼k2​P11​(k)\sim k^{2}P_{11}(k).
In fact, this correlation must be present since the reshuffling
happens inside the halos, whose distribution
is modulated by the linear density fluctuationsδ1\delta_{1}via the peak-background split arguments which we will use in the next Chapter to motivate galaxy bias.
Therefore, the effective sound speed could have been discovered
by Peebles already in the 1980’s!

## 4.5Power Counting in a Scaling Universe

Having collected all the ingredients of the EFT at one loop, let us
estimate their relative sizes[60]. For this purpose it is convenient to
use the power-law universe of Chapter 2,P11PLU​(k)=2​π2kNL3​(kkNL)n,P_{11}^{\mathrm{PLU}}(k)=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}^{3}}\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{n}\,,(160)

withn=−1.5n=-1.5, which mimics the slope of theΛ\LambdaCDM spectrum
around the non-linear scale. In this universe the linear field
scales asδ1∼(k/kNL)(3+n)/2=(k/kNL)0.75\delta_{1}\sim(k/k_{\mathrm{NL}})^{(3+n)/2}=(k/k_{\mathrm{NL}})^{0.75},
and each loop brings an extra factor of the dimensionless power:P22,P13\displaystyle P_{22},\;P_{13}∼∫d3​q​P11​(q)​P11​(k)∼(kkNL)3+n​P11​(k),\displaystyle\sim\int d^{3}q\,P_{11}(q)\,P_{11}(k)\sim\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{3+n}P_{11}(k)\,,(161)P2​-loop\displaystyle P_{2\text{-loop}}∼(kkNL)2​(3+n)​P11​(k).\displaystyle\sim\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{2(3+n)}P_{11}(k)\,.(162)

Collecting all contributions to the dimensionless power spectrumΔ2​(k)∼k3​P​(k)\Delta^{2}(k)\sim k^{3}P(k):Δ2​(k)∼(kkNL)n+3⏟tree+(kkNL)2​(3+n)⏟1​-loop+(kkNL)3​(3+n)⏟2​-loop+(kkNL)n+5⏟k2​P11​ctr+(kkNL)7⏟k4​stoch+⋯\Delta^{2}(k)\sim\underbrace{\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{n+3}}_{\text{tree}}+\underbrace{\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{2(3+n)}}_{1\text{-loop}}+\underbrace{\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{3(3+n)}}_{2\text{-loop}}+\underbrace{\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{n+5}}_{k^{2}P_{11}\text{ ctr}}+\underbrace{\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{7}}_{k^{4}\text{ stoch}}+\cdots(163)

Forn=−1.5n=-1.5the exponents evaluate toΔ2​(k)∼(kkNL)1.5+(kkNL)3+(kkNL)4.5+(kkNL)3.5+(kkNL)7+⋯\Delta^{2}(k)\sim\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{1.5}+\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{3}+\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{4.5}+\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{3.5}+\left(\frac{k}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}\right)^{7}+\cdots(164)

The hierarchy is now manifest. The counterterm is only slightly
smaller than the one-loop correction (the difference of the
exponents,0.50.5, reflects the mildk2/kNLn+2k^{2}/k_{\mathrm{NL}}^{n+2}suppression),
so the two must always be included together. The stochastic term,
by contrast, is strongly suppressed by itsk4k^{4}scaling:Ptree≫P1​-loop≃Pctr≫P2​-loop≳Pstoch.\boxed{P_{\mathrm{tree}}\gg P_{1\text{-loop}}\simeq P_{\mathrm{ctr}}\gg P_{2\text{-loop}}\gtrsim P_{\mathrm{stoch}}\,.}(165)

The scaling Universe estimates apply to the
physical,renormalizedquantities.
Note that the scaling arguments can be used to estimate the time-dependence
of the finite parts of the counterterms. Indeed, from the linear scalingΔ2​(k)∝D+2​(z)\Delta^{2}(k)\propto D_{+}^{2}(z)we extractkNL−(3+n)∝D+2​(z)⇒kNL​(z)kNL​(z=0)=D+−2n+3​(z)∼D+−43​(z).k_{\mathrm{NL}}^{-(3+n)}\propto D^{2}_{+}(z)\quad\quad\Rightarrow\quad\quad\frac{k_{\mathrm{NL}}(z)}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}(z=0)}=D^{-\frac{2}{n+3}}_{+}(z)\sim D^{-\frac{4}{3}}_{+}(z)~\,.

This impliesγfin∼1/(kNL)2∝D+83\gamma_{\rm fin}\sim 1/(k_{\mathrm{NL}})^{2}\propto D^{\frac{8}{3}}_{+},
which matches simulations quite well[61,62,49].

## 5Chapter 5: Galaxy Bias

So far we have been describing the clustering of dark matter. What
we actually observe, however, are galaxies: highly non-linear
objects that form at special locations of the matter field — in
the peaks of the density, inside virialized halos.
Thanks to this formation mechanism,
on large scales galaxies trace the distribution of matter
in the Universe. This effect is known asgalaxy bias.
Remarkably, even
though galaxy formation itself is hopelessly complicated, thelarge-scale distributionof galaxies can be described
analytically by the same EFT logic: all the complications are
absorbed into a finite set of bias coefficients.

Before going for a full-blown EFT treatment of the problem, let
us consider phenomenological models of halo formation
and their correlations with the cosmological initial
conditions. These models are not as accurate as the full EFT,
but they provide some nice intuition behindhalo bias,
a relationship between
the matter halos and the underlying matter field.

## 5.1Phenomeological Models for Halo Bias

The first relevant ingredient isspherical collapse.
Consider, in an EdS background, a spherical top-hat region of
initial radiusRiR_{i}and overdensityδ¯i>0\bar{\delta}_{i}>0. By the
Newtonian shell theorem the sphere evolves as a closed Friedmann
universe, independently of its environment: its radius obeysR¨=−G​MR2,M=4​π3​ρ¯m,i​(1+δ¯i)​Ri3=const.\ddot{R}=-\frac{GM}{R^{2}}\,,\qquad M=\frac{4\pi}{3}\,\bar{\rho}_{m,i}\left(1+\bar{\delta}_{i}\right)R_{i}^{3}=\mathrm{const}\,.(166)

For a bound (overdense) region the solution is the famous cycloid,R=A​(1−cos⁡θ),t=B​(θ−sin⁡θ),A3=G​M​B2,R=A\,(1-\cos\theta)\,,\qquad t=B\,(\theta-\sin\theta)\,,\qquad A^{3}=GMB^{2}\,,(167)

while the background density in EdS isρ¯m=1/(6​π​G​t2)\bar{\rho}_{m}=1/(6\pi Gt^{2}). The overdensity of the sphere is
therefore known exactly at all times:1+δ=3​M4​π​R3⋅1ρ¯m=92​(θ−sin⁡θ)2(1−cos⁡θ)3.1+\delta=\frac{3M}{4\pi R^{3}}\cdot\frac{1}{\bar{\rho}_{m}}=\frac{9}{2}\,\frac{(\theta-\sin\theta)^{2}}{(1-\cos\theta)^{3}}\,.(168)

The milestones of the evolution: atturnaround,θ=π\theta=\pi, the sphere decouples from the expansion and reaches
its maximal radiusRta=2​AR_{\mathrm{ta}}=2A, with1+δta=9​π2/16≃5.551+\delta_{\mathrm{ta}}=9\pi^{2}/16\simeq 5.55; atθ=2​π\theta=2\pithe sphere formallycollapsesto a point, at the timetcol=2​π​Bt_{\mathrm{col}}=2\pi B.

Now compare with linear theory. Expanding
Eq. (168) at smallθ\theta,δ≃320​θ2=320​(6​tB)2/3∝t2/3∝a,\delta\simeq\frac{3}{20}\,\theta^{2}=\frac{3}{20}\left(\frac{6t}{B}\right)^{2/3}\propto t^{2/3}\propto a\,,(169)

i.e. we recover the linear growing mode, as we must. The key trick
is to ask: what value does theformal linear extrapolationof
the initial overdensity reach at the moment when the true non-linear
solution collapses? Settingt=tcol=2​π​Bt=t_{\mathrm{col}}=2\pi B,δc=320(12π)2/3≃1.686.\boxed{\;\delta_{c}=\frac{3}{20}\,(12\pi)^{2/3}\simeq 1.686\,.\;}(170)

This number is the bridge between the (Gaussian, calculable) initial
conditions and the (non-perturbative) formation of halos: a region
collapses by timeτ0\tau_{0}if itslinearly
evolvedsmoothed density exceedsδc\delta_{c}. In EdS the result is
exact and independent of the halo mass; inΛ\LambdaCDMδc\delta_{c}acquires only a weak cosmology dependence. In reality the collapse
to a point is halted by shell crossing and violent relaxation: the
object settles into virial equilibrium at roughly half the
turnaround radius, with a characteristic density contrast1+δvir≃18​π2≃1781+\delta_{\mathrm{vir}}\simeq 18\pi^{2}\simeq 178— the origin of
the familiarΔ≃200\Delta\simeq 200conventions used to define the halo
mass and radius.

Homework:using energy conservation and the virial theorem,
show that virialization at half the turnaround radius implies1+δvir=18​π21+\delta_{\mathrm{vir}}=18\pi^{2}if the collapse time is taken as2​π​B2\pi B.

## The Press–Schechter Halo Mass Function.

The end result of the non-linear collapse of matter is the
formation of halos. Let us study how the halos are distributed with
respect to their masses. This distribution is called thehalo mass function(HMF),d​n¯/d​ln⁡Md\bar{n}/d\ln M, wheren¯\bar{n}is the halo number density in units of(Mpc/h)−3(\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h)^{-3}. The
Press–Schechter approach[63]provides a simple analytic model for it.
We have just seen that in the spherical collapse model a region of
initial comoving radiusRRcollapses when its linearly extrapolated
overdensity reachesδc=1.686\delta_{c}=1.686. Following Press and Schechter,
let us assume that the same criterion applies to different patches of our Universe:
a spherical region collapses into a halo of massM​(R)=4​π3​ρ¯m​R3M(R)=\frac{4\pi}{3}\bar{\rho}_{m}R^{3}within the lifetime of the
Universe if its linear overdensity, extrapolated to today, satisfiesδ¯1​(R)≥δc\bar{\delta}_{1}(R)\geq\delta_{c}; regions withδ¯1​(R)<δc\bar{\delta}_{1}(R)<\delta_{c}do not make it. Whether a region
collapses or not is thus determined entirely by the initial
conditions, i.e. by the linear power spectrumP11​(k)P_{11}(k).

To compute the probability of collapse we first need the one-point
PDF of the smoothed linear densityδR\delta_{R}(as in Chapter 2, we
work with linear fields extrapolated to redshift zero).
This is best to do using the functional integral techniques, introduced
in AppendixA.
Since the
initial field is Gaussian, the PDF is given by the (normalized)
Gaussian functional integralP​(δR)=𝒩​∫𝒟​δ1​exp⁡{−∫𝒌δ1​(𝒌)​δ1​(−𝒌)2​P11​(k)}​δD(1)​(δR−∫d3​x​WR​(𝒙)​δ1​(𝒙)),P(\delta_{R})=\mathcal{N}\int\mathcal{D}\delta_{1}\;\exp\{-\int_{\bm{k}}\frac{\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\delta_{1}(-{\bm{k}})}{2P_{11}(k)}\Bigg\}\;\delta_{D}^{(1)}\!\left(\delta_{R}-\int d^{3}x\;W_{R}({\bm{x}})\,\delta_{1}({\bm{x}})\right)\,,(171)

where the delta function selects the field configurations with the
prescribed smoothed density, and in Fourier space the constraint
reads∫𝒌δ1​(𝒌)​W~R​(k​R)\int_{\bm{k}}\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\tilde{W}_{R}(kR)with the top-hat
window of Chapter 2. Using the integral representation of the delta
function,P​(δR)=𝒩​∫−i​∞i​∞d​λ2​π​e−λ​δR​∫𝒟​δ1​exp⁡{−∫𝒌δ1​(𝒌)​δ1​(−𝒌)2​P11​(k)+λ​∫𝒌δ1​(𝒌)​W~R​(k​R)},P(\delta_{R})=\mathcal{N}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\lambda}{2\pi}\,e^{-\lambda\delta_{R}}\int\mathcal{D}\delta_{1}\,\exp\{-\int_{\bm{k}}\frac{\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\delta_{1}(-{\bm{k}})}{2P_{11}(k)}+\lambda\int_{\bm{k}}\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\tilde{W}_{R}(kR)\Bigg\}\,,(172)

the functional integral is Gaussian and is computed by evaluating
the exponent on the saddle point,δ1​(−𝒌)=λ​P11​(k)​W~R​(k​R)\delta_{1}(-{\bm{k}})=\lambda\,P_{11}(k)\tilde{W}_{R}(kR), which leaves
an ordinary Gaussian integral overλ\lambda:P​(δR)=𝒩′​∫−i​∞i​∞d​λ2​π​exp⁡{−λ​δR+λ22​∫𝒌P11​(k)​|W~R​(k​R)|2}.P(\delta_{R})=\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\lambda}{2\pi}\,\exp\{-\lambda\delta_{R}+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\int_{\bm{k}}P_{11}(k)\,|\tilde{W}_{R}(kR)|^{2}\Bigg\}\,.(173)

The coefficient ofλ2\lambda^{2}is nothing but the smoothed mass
varianceσ2​(R)\sigma^{2}(R)of Eq. (35). Performing the
last integral, we getP​(δR)=12​π​σ2​(R)​exp⁡{−δR22​σ2​(R)}.P(\delta_{R})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}(R)}}\,\exp\{-\frac{\delta_{R}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}(R)}\Bigg\}\,.(174)

The probability for a region of radiusRRto collapse is then𝒫​(δR≥δc)=∫δc∞𝑑δR​P​(δR)=12​erfc​(ν2),ν≡δcσ​(R).\mathcal{P}(\delta_{R}\geq\delta_{c})=\int_{\delta_{c}}^{\infty}d\delta_{R}\;P(\delta_{R})=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{erfc}\!\left(\frac{\nu}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\,,\qquad\nu\equiv\frac{\delta_{c}}{\sigma(R)}\,.(175)

In the limitR→0R\to 0the variance diverges and we expectallmatter to end up in halos of some (small) mass; yet
Eq. (175) saturates at1/21/2. The mismatch is corrected by
an ad hoc factor of two,𝒫→2​𝒫\mathcal{P}\to 2\mathcal{P}—
famously introduced by hand by Press and Schechter, and explained
rigorously in the excursion-set formulation, where it accounts for
the underdense regions embedded in larger collapsing ones (the
cloud-in-cloud problem). By definition, this probability equals the
fraction of the total mass contained in halos with masses aboveM​(R)M(R),2​𝒫​(δR≥δc)=1ρ¯m​∫M​(R)∞M′​d​n¯d​M′​𝑑M′.2\mathcal{P}(\delta_{R}\geq\delta_{c})=\frac{1}{\bar{\rho}_{m}}\int_{M(R)}^{\infty}M^{\prime}\,\frac{d\bar{n}}{dM^{\prime}}\,dM^{\prime}\,.(176)

Differentiating both sides with respect toMMwe obtain thePress–Schechter mass function:Md​n¯d​M=ρ¯mMfPS(ν)|d​ln⁡σ​(R​[M])d​ln⁡M|,fPS(ν)=2πνe−ν2/2.\boxed{\;M\frac{d\bar{n}}{dM}=\frac{\bar{\rho}_{m}}{M}\,f_{\mathrm{PS}}\!\left(\nu\right)\left|\frac{d\ln\sigma(R[M])}{d\ln M}\right|\,,\qquad f_{\mathrm{PS}}(\nu)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\;\nu\,e^{-\nu^{2}/2}\,.\;}(177)

Note the structure of this result: the mean halo abundance isnon-perturbativeinP11P_{11}— the variance appears in an
exponent. This is an example of a quantity that is beyond the reach
of perturbation theory, just as the mean free path or the size of
molecules can be estimated in quantum mechanics but cannot be
computed within fluid dynamics. In the EFT context, the HMF is the
kind of input we can use for order-of-magnitude estimates of the
non-perturbative EFT parameters, but never for controlled
predictions.

## Thresholding: Peak-Background Split and Halo Bias.

So far we have considered only the mean abundance of halos. In the
spherical collapse model every region evolves independently; in the
actual Universe the modes interact, and these interactions modulate
halo formation and induce inhomogeneities in the halo distribution.
Imagine a small region sitting in the background of a
long-wavelength modeδℓ\delta_{\ell}. By the equivalence principle
(cf. Chapter 3), the large-scale fluctuation acts as a tiny
separate universe with slightly more matter in it than average:
locally, the small-scale fluctuations grow a bit faster, and faster
growth means earlier collapse. The long mode thereforeassistshalo formation, see the cartoon in Fig.14. The most
naive way to account for this is to weaken the collapse criterion in
such a region:δR​(𝒒)≥δc−δℓ​(𝒒),\delta_{R}({\bm{q}})\geq\delta_{c}-\delta_{\ell}({\bm{q}})\,,(178)

where𝒒{\bm{q}}is the initial (Langrangian) position.
Plugging this into the HMF (177) and expanding inδℓ\delta_{\ell},M​d​n¯d​M|δℓ=ρ¯mM​fPS​(δc−δℓσ​(R))​|d​ln⁡σd​ln⁡M|=M​d​n¯d​M|0×(1−d​ln⁡fPSd​ν​δℓ​(𝒒)σ​(R)+O​(δℓ2)).\begin{split}M\frac{d\bar{n}}{dM}\bigg|_{\delta_{\ell}}&=\frac{\bar{\rho}_{m}}{M}\,f_{\mathrm{PS}}\!\left(\frac{\delta_{c}-\delta_{\ell}}{\sigma(R)}\right)\left|\frac{d\ln\sigma}{d\ln M}\right|=M\frac{d\bar{n}}{dM}\bigg|_{0}\times\left(1-\frac{d\ln f_{\mathrm{PS}}}{d\nu}\,\frac{\delta_{\ell}({\bm{q}})}{\sigma(R)}+O(\delta_{\ell}^{2})\right)\,.\end{split}(179)

The coefficient ofδℓ\delta_{\ell}is the (Lagrangian) linear bias:b1L=−1σ​(R)​d​ln⁡fPSd​ν|ν=δc/σ​(R)=δc2−σ2​(R)δc​σ2​(R),b_{1}^{L}=-\frac{1}{\sigma(R)}\,\frac{d\ln f_{\mathrm{PS}}}{d\nu}\bigg|_{\nu=\delta_{c}/\sigma(R)}=\frac{\delta_{c}^{2}-\sigma^{2}(R)}{\delta_{c}\,\sigma^{2}(R)}\,,(180)

so that the Lagrangian-space overdensity of would-be halos
(proto-halos) isδh​(𝒒)≡n¯​(𝒒)−n¯n¯=b1L​δ1​(𝒒).\delta_{h}({\bm{q}})\equiv\frac{\bar{n}({\bm{q}})-\bar{n}}{\bar{n}}=b_{1}^{L}\,\delta_{1}({\bm{q}})\,.(181)

This is the first appearance of the tracer–matter relation. Note
that this is precisely the Lagrangian bias coefficient of
Chapter 7: the observed (Eulerian) tracers are obtained by advecting
the proto-halos with the flow, whenceb1=1+b1Lb_{1}=1+b_{1}^{L},
Eq. (309). Thus the thresholding model gives us an estimate
ofb1Lb_{1}^{L}. High-mass (ν≫1\nu\gg 1) objects haveb1L≃δc/σ2≫1b_{1}^{L}\simeq\delta_{c}/\sigma^{2}\gg 1: rare peaks are strongly
biased.Figure 14:1D cartoon explaining the mechanism of correlating
proto-halo positions with long-wavelength fluctuations in the
thresholding model. Long-wavelength modes locally act as separate
universes that assist halo formation by pushing small-scale
fluctuations closer to (or farther from) the critical thresholdδc=1.686\delta_{c}=1.686.

This result can be derived more rigorously from the same
thresholding picture used for the mean HMF, by studying the
clustering of the regions above threshold. The two-point function of
these regions — our proxy for the halo two-point function —
measures the excess probability of findingtwospheres, at
Lagrangian separation𝒓{\bm{r}}, both above the threshold:p2​(δR​(𝒒+𝒓)>δc,δR​(𝒒)>δc)=p12​(1+ξhL​(r)),p_{2}\big(\delta_{R}({\bm{q}}+{\bm{r}})>\delta_{c},\;\delta_{R}({\bm{q}})>\delta_{c}\big)=p_{1}^{2}\left(1+\xi_{h}^{L}(r)\right)\,,(182)

where the superscriptLLreminds us that we work in Lagrangian
coordinates. We need the joint PDF of the smoothed field at two
