two Lagrange multipliers, and noting that the second constraint,δR2=∫d3​q​δ1​(𝒒)​WR2​(𝒒+𝒓)\delta_{R_{2}}=\int d^{3}q\,\delta_{1}({\bm{q}})W_{R_{2}}({\bm{q}}+{\bm{r}}), brings a
factore−i​𝒌⋅𝒓e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{r}}}in Fourier space, the Gaussian integrals
giveP​(δR1,δR2)=12​π​1σR12​σR22−σR1​R24​(𝒓)​exp⁡{−σR22​δR12+σR12​δR22−2​σR1​R22​(𝒓)​δR1​δR22​(σR12​σR22−σR1​R24​(𝒓))},P(\delta_{R_{1}},\delta_{R_{2}})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{R_{1}}^{2}\sigma_{R_{2}}^{2}-\sigma^{4}_{R_{1}R_{2}}({\bm{r}})}}\,\exp\{-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{R_{2}}\delta^{2}_{R_{1}}+\sigma^{2}_{R_{1}}\delta^{2}_{R_{2}}-2\,\sigma^{2}_{R_{1}R_{2}}({\bm{r}})\,\delta_{R_{1}}\delta_{R_{2}}}{2\left(\sigma_{R_{1}}^{2}\sigma^{2}_{R_{2}}-\sigma^{4}_{R_{1}R_{2}}({\bm{r}})\right)}\Bigg\}\,,(183)

with the smoothed cross-correlationσR1​R22​(𝒓)≡∫𝒌P11​(k)​e−i​𝒌⋅𝒓​W~R1​(k​R1)​W~R2​(k​R2).\sigma^{2}_{R_{1}R_{2}}({\bm{r}})\equiv\int_{\bm{k}}P_{11}(k)\,e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{r}}}\,\tilde{W}_{R_{1}}(kR_{1})\,\tilde{W}_{R_{2}}(kR_{2})\,.(184)

For equal radii,σR​R2​(𝒓)=ξR​(r)\sigma^{2}_{RR}({\bm{r}})=\xi_{R}(r)is simply the
filtered linear correlation function of matter. Integrating the
joint PDF over both densities above threshold and dividing byp12p_{1}^{2}, withp1p_{1}from Eq. (175), we arrive at1+ξhL​(r)=2π​1[erfc​(ν/2)]2​∫ν∞𝑑ν′​e−ν′⁣2/2​erfc​[ν−ν′​ξ^R​(r)2​(1−ξ^R2​(r))],ξ^R​(r)≡ξR​(r)σ2​(R).1+\xi_{h}^{L}(r)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,\frac{1}{\left[\mathrm{erfc}(\nu/\sqrt{2})\right]^{2}}\int_{\nu}^{\infty}d\nu^{\prime}\;e^{-\nu^{\prime 2}/2}\,\mathrm{erfc}\!\left[\frac{\nu-\nu^{\prime}\hat{\xi}_{R}(r)}{\sqrt{2\left(1-\hat{\xi}^{2}_{R}(r)\right)}}\right]\,,\qquad\hat{\xi}_{R}(r)\equiv\frac{\xi_{R}(r)}{\sigma^{2}(R)}\,.(185)

A formal Taylor expansion inξR​(r)\xi_{R}(r)organizes this result as a
series of tracer–matter relations,ξhL​(r)=∑N=1∞1N!​(bNL)2​[ξR​(r)]N,bNL=2π​1erfc​(ν/2)​e−ν2/2σN​(R)​HeN−1​(ν),\xi_{h}^{L}(r)=\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{N!}\,(b_{N}^{L})^{2}\,\left[\xi_{R}(r)\right]^{N}\,,\qquad b_{N}^{L}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,\frac{1}{\mathrm{erfc}(\nu/\sqrt{2})}\,\frac{e^{-\nu^{2}/2}}{\sigma^{N}(R)}\,\mathrm{He}_{N-1}(\nu)\,,(186)

whereHeN​(x)\mathrm{He}_{N}(x)are the Hermite
polynomials,He0=1\mathrm{He}_{0}=1,He1​(x)=x\mathrm{He}_{1}(x)=x,He2​(x)=x2−1\mathrm{He}_{2}(x)=x^{2}-1, etc. In the high-mass limitσ​(R)→0\sigma(R)\to 0the bias parameters simplify,bNL|ν≫1=δcNσ2​N​(R),b_{N}^{L}\Big|_{\nu\gg 1}=\frac{\delta_{c}^{N}}{\sigma^{2N}(R)}\,,(187)

which correctly reproduces the naive
estimate (180) in the same limit. Importantly, the above
result, while being statistical in nature, can be reproduced
by promoting the Lagrangian proto-halo overdensity fieldδg\delta_{g}to be a power-law series in the local dark matter overdensity fieldδ1\delta_{1}[64]:δhL=f​(δm=δ1)=b1​δ1+b22​δ12+⋯.\delta_{h}^{L}=f(\delta_{m}=\delta_{1})=b_{1}\,\delta_{1}+\frac{b_{2}}{2}\,\delta_{1}^{2}+\cdots\,.(188)

This forms a basis for the deterministic galaxy bias.

Let us finish with a historic remark. Simple as it is, the
thresholding model provided the first mechanism correlating
small-scale structure — halos and galaxies — with the
long-wavelength cosmological fluctuations. This was a breakthrough:
it explained for the first time why galaxy
clustering should depend on cosmology[65,66].
At the same time the model is very naive. Tidal fields
should participate as well — they act as ananisotropicseparate universe modulating proto-halo formation — so the halo
density must respond to the tidal tensor too. A fixed threshold and
a specific window function are further simplifications that are hard
to justify. On large scales, the lesson we retain is the structure
of the answer: the tracer density is a function of the long-wavelength
environment,δg=f​(δm)=b1​δm+b22​δm2+⋯,Pg​gtree​(k)=b12​P11​(k),\delta_{g}=f(\delta_{m})=b_{1}\,\delta_{m}+\frac{b_{2}}{2}\,\delta_{m}^{2}+\cdots\,,\qquad P_{gg}^{\mathrm{tree}}(k)=b_{1}^{2}\,P_{11}(k)\,,(189)

with a single parameter capturinganygalaxy selection at
linear order. This is the simplification principle
of EFT at play again: just like the potential of any
charge distribution on large scales is characterized only by a single parameter,
its charge,
the galaxy overdensity is charachterized bylinear biasb1b_{1}.
In what follows we promote this observation to
systematic, symmetry-based EFT expansion[67,68,69,70](see[71]for an excellent review).
The galaxy overdensity
is a function of the underlying degrees of freedom sourced by
the matter field.
EFT writes down a symmetry-based Taylor approximation
for this function.

## 5.2The General EFT Bias Expansion

The local PBS ansatzδg=f​(δm)\delta_{g}=f(\delta_{m})cannot be the whole
story. Guided by the symmetries, let us ask what the galaxy density
is actually allowed to depend on. By the equivalence principle, a
uniform potential or a uniform gravitational acceleration is
locally unobservable, soΦ\Phiand∂iΦ\partial_{i}\Phicannot appear: the allowed building blocks are the second
derivatives, i.e.∂i∂jΦ\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\Phi(the tidal tensor), the
velocity gradients∂ivj\partial_{i}v^{j}, and the stochastic fields.
Moreover, galaxy formation takes a Hubble time, so, exactly as for
the effective stress tensor of Chapter 4, the dependence is
non-local in time and must be evaluated along the past fluid
trajectory. The full EFT expression is a functionalδg​(𝒙,τ)=∫τ𝑑τ′​F​[∂i∂jΦ​(𝒙fl,τ′),∂ivj​(𝒙fl,τ′),ε​(𝒙fl,τ′);τ,τ′].\delta_{g}({\bm{x}},\tau)=\int^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\;F\!\left[\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\Phi({\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}},\tau^{\prime}),\;\partial_{i}v^{j}({\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}},\tau^{\prime}),\;\varepsilon({\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}},\tau^{\prime});\,\tau,\tau^{\prime}\right]\,.(190)

ε\varepsilonnow is the stochastic field of galaxies which
is different from that of matter.
In perturbation theory this expression collapses, order by order,
to alocal-in-timeexpansion — the same mechanism we saw in
§4: eachδ(n)\delta^{(n)}carries a fixed time dependence∝D+n\propto D_{+}^{n}, so all the memory integrals reduce to constant
coefficients. At the one-loop order, we can writeδg=f​(∂i∂jΦ^,∂i∂jΦ^v,ε)​(𝒙,τ)\delta_{g}=f\big(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\hat{\Phi},\,\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\hat{\Phi}_{v},\varepsilon\big)({\bm{x}},\tau),
whereΦ^\hat{\Phi}andΦ^v\hat{\Phi}_{v}are the rescaled gravitational and
velocity potentials (Δ​Φ^=δ\Delta\hat{\Phi}=\delta,Δ​Φ^v=Θ\Delta\hat{\Phi}_{v}=\Theta) — the bias
expansion iseffectively local in time.

Organizing the expansion by the symmetries, up to third order (plus
the leading higher-derivative term) we getδg=b1​δ+b22​(δ2−⟨δ2⟩)+b𝒢2​𝒢2+b33!​δ3+b𝒢2​δ​𝒢2​δ+b𝒢3​𝒢3+bΓ3​Γ3+b∇2δ​∇2δ+ε+⋯\boxed{\delta_{g}=b_{1}\,\delta+\frac{b_{2}}{2}\,\left(\delta^{2}-\langle\delta^{2}\rangle\right)+b_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\,\mathcal{G}_{2}+\frac{b_{3}}{3!}\,\delta^{3}+b_{\mathcal{G}_{2}\delta}\,\mathcal{G}_{2}\,\delta+b_{\mathcal{G}_{3}}\,\mathcal{G}_{3}+b_{\Gamma_{3}}\,\Gamma_{3}+b_{\nabla^{2}\delta}\,\nabla^{2}\delta+\varepsilon+\cdots}(191)

with the tidal operators defined as𝒢2​(Φ^)≡(∂i∂jΦ^)2−(Δ​Φ^)2,Γ3≡𝒢2​(Φ^)−𝒢2​(Φ^v),\mathcal{G}_{2}(\hat{\Phi})\equiv\left(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\hat{\Phi}\right)^{2}-\left(\Delta\hat{\Phi}\right)^{2}\,,\qquad\Gamma_{3}\equiv\mathcal{G}_{2}(\hat{\Phi})-\mathcal{G}_{2}(\hat{\Phi}_{v})\,,(192)

and the cubic Galileon operator, built from the Hessian matrix(ℋΦ^)i​j≡∂i∂jΦ^(\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\Phi}})_{ij}\equiv\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\hat{\Phi}:𝒢3​(Φ^)≡(Δ​Φ^)3−3​Δ​Φ^​(∂i∂jΦ^)2+2​∂i∂jΦ^​∂j∂kΦ^​∂k∂iΦ^=6​det⁡(∂i∂jΦ^).\mathcal{G}_{3}(\hat{\Phi})\equiv\left(\Delta\hat{\Phi}\right)^{3}-3\,\Delta\hat{\Phi}\,\left(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\hat{\Phi}\right)^{2}+2\,\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\hat{\Phi}\;\partial^{j}\partial^{k}\hat{\Phi}\;\partial_{k}\partial^{i}\hat{\Phi}=6\,\det\left(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\hat{\Phi}\right)\,.(193)

The last equality follows from Newton’s identitydet⁡M=16​[(tr⁡M)3−3​tr⁡M​tr⁡M2+2​tr⁡M3]\det M=\frac{1}{6}\big[(\tr M)^{3}-3\tr M\,\tr M^{2}+2\tr M^{3}\big]for a3×33\times 3matrix. Together withδ3=(Δ​Φ^)3\delta^{3}=(\Delta\hat{\Phi})^{3}and𝒢2​δ\mathcal{G}_{2}\,\delta, the operator𝒢3\mathcal{G}_{3}completes the basis of cubic
rotational invariants of the tidal tensor: at this order one can
build onlytr⁡ℋΦ^3\tr\mathcal{H}^{3}_{\hat{\Phi}},tr⁡ℋΦ^2​tr⁡ℋΦ^\tr\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\hat{\Phi}}\tr\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\Phi}}and(tr⁡ℋΦ^)3(\tr\mathcal{H}_{\hat{\Phi}})^{3}, and{δ3,𝒢2​δ,𝒢3}\{\delta^{3},\mathcal{G}_{2}\delta,\mathcal{G}_{3}\}is a convenient repackaging of these three structures.
Several comments are in order:
- •

Thetidal biasb𝒢2b_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}is the first qualitatively
new ingredient beyond PBS: the equivalence principle allows the
galaxy field to respond to the full tidal tensor∂i∂jΦ^\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\hat{\Phi}, not just to its traceδ\delta.𝒢2\mathcal{G}_{2}starts at second order in perturbations.
- •

Γ3\Gamma_{3}starts atthirdorder, sinceΦ^=Φ^v\hat{\Phi}=\hat{\Phi}_{v}at linear order; it is the operator through
which the velocity potential first enters.
- •

b∇2δ​∇2δb_{\nabla^{2}\delta}\,\nabla^{2}\deltais the leadinghigher-derivativebias: the coefficient scales as the
square of the spatial scale of galaxy formation (e.g. the halo radius),b∇2δ∼R∗2b_{\nabla^{2}\delta}\sim R_{*}^{2}.
- •

ε\varepsilonis thestochasticfield — the part of
the galaxy field uncorrelated with the long-wavelength
cosmological fields (see §5.4).
- •

The above expression is a nested expansion
inδ1\delta_{1}. The bias operators are functions of the non-linear density
fieldδ\deltaand its tidal potential, which themselves need to be expanded
inδ1\delta_{1}using the matter EFT. This is a Eulerian bias expansion
formulated in terms of the finite-time matter field.

## 5.3Galaxy Kernels, One-Loop Power Spectrum, and Redundant Operators

Plugging the perturbative solutions forδ\deltaandΘ\Thetainto
the bias expansion defines thegalaxy kernelsKnK_{n},
analogous to the SPT kernelsFnF_{n}:δgdet​(𝒌)\displaystyle\delta_{g}^{\mathrm{det}}({\bm{k}})=b1​δ1​(𝒌)+∫𝒒K2​(𝒌−𝒒,𝒒)​δ1​(𝒌−𝒒)​δ1​(𝒒)\displaystyle=b_{1}\,\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})+\int_{\bm{q}}K_{2}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}})\,\delta_{1}({\bm{q}})+∫𝒒1,𝒒2,𝒒3(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌−𝒒123)​K3​(𝒒1,𝒒2,𝒒3)​δ1​(𝒒1)​δ1​(𝒒2)​δ1​(𝒒3)+⋯\displaystyle\quad+\int_{{\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2},{\bm{q}}_{3}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}_{123})\;K_{3}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2},{\bm{q}}_{3})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}_{1})\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}_{2})\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}_{3})+\cdots(194)

At second order,K2​(𝒌1,𝒌2)=b1​F2​(𝒌1,𝒌2)+b22+b𝒢2​[(𝒌1⋅𝒌2)2k12​k22−1],K_{2}({\bm{k}}_{1},{\bm{k}}_{2})=b_{1}\,F_{2}({\bm{k}}_{1},{\bm{k}}_{2})+\frac{b_{2}}{2}+b_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\left[\frac{({\bm{k}}_{1}\cdot{\bm{k}}_{2})^{2}}{k_{1}^{2}\,k_{2}^{2}}-1\right]\,,(195)

whileK3K_{3}combinesb1​F3b_{1}F_{3}with the SPT-evolved quadratic and cubic bias operators.
Repeating the steps of §3.3, the deterministic part of theone-loop galaxy power spectrumisPg​g1​-loop(k)|det=b12P11(k)+2∫𝒒[K2(𝒌−𝒒,𝒒)]2P11(|𝒌−𝒒|)P11(q)+6∫𝒒K3(𝒌,𝒒,−𝒒)P11(k)P11(q).\boxed{P_{gg}^{1\text{-loop}}(k)\Big|_{\rm det}=b_{1}^{2}\,P_{11}(k)+2\int_{\bm{q}}\left[K_{2}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}})\right]^{2}P_{11}(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)\,P_{11}(q)+6\int_{\bm{q}}K_{3}({\bm{k}},{\bm{q}},-{\bm{q}})\;P_{11}(k)\,P_{11}(q)\,.}(196)

Redundant operators.An important simplification takes place in
Eq. (196): the cubic operatorsδ3\delta^{3},𝒢3\mathcal{G}_{3}andG2​δG_{2}\deltado not contributeto the one-loop power spectrum.
To see why, consider theP13P_{13}-type contribution of theδ3\delta^{3}term:2​⟨b1​δ1,b33!​δ13⟩′=b1​b3​σΛ2​P11​(k),σΛ2≡∫Λd3​q​P11​(q),2\,\langle b_{1}\delta_{1}\,,\;\tfrac{b_{3}}{3!}\,\delta_{1}^{3}\rangle^{\prime}=b_{1}b_{3}\;\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\;P_{11}(k)\,,\qquad\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\equiv\int^{\Lambda}d^{3}q\;P_{11}(q)\,,(197)

which diverges asΛ→∞\Lambda\to\infty. Crucially, itskk-dependence
isexactlythat of the linear bias term, so it is completely
absorbed by the renormalization. Indeed, combining thisP13P_{13}contribution with the linear one we getb12​P11+b1​b3​σΛ2​P11​(k)=(b1+b32​σΛ2)2​P11​(k)+O​(σΛ4​P11​(k)),b_{1}^{2}P_{11}+b_{1}b_{3}\;\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}\;P_{11}(k)=\left(b_{1}+\frac{b_{3}}{2}\sigma^{2}_{\Lambda}\right)^{2}P_{11}(k)+O(\sigma^{4}_{\Lambda}P_{11}(k))\,,(198)

whereO​(σΛ4)O(\sigma^{4}_{\Lambda})are two-loop corrections which we
can ignore at the one-loop order.
We see that the physical predictions depend on a combination
ofb1b_{1}andb3​σΛ2b_{3}\sigma^{2}_{\Lambda}. Henceb1b_{1}should be interpreted
as a “bare” parameter, while the relevant combination
is the physical renormalized linear bias:b1ren=b1+b32​σΛ2+⋯.b_{1}^{\mathrm{ren}}=b_{1}+\frac{b_{3}}{2}\,\sigma_{\Lambda}^{2}+\cdots\,.(199)

Similarly including𝒢2​δ\mathcal{G}_{2}\deltaand theSPT-evolvedb2​δ2b_{2}\delta^{2}(i.e. plugging the SPT formulaδ=δ1+∫F2​δ12\delta=\delta_{1}+\int F_{2}\delta_{1}^{2}intoδ2\delta^{2}),
we end up with the following net expression:b1ren=b1+(b32+34​b221−4​b𝒢2​δ3)​σΛ2.b_{1}^{\mathrm{ren}}=b_{1}+\left(\frac{b_{3}}{2}+\frac{34b_{2}}{21}-\frac{4b_{\mathcal{G}_{2}\delta}}{3}\right)\sigma^{2}_{\Lambda}\,.(200)

As far as the cubic Galileon𝒢3\mathcal{G}_{3}is concerned,
its one-loop power spectrum contribution vanishes identically.
The parameter measured from the data is always the renormalized one;δ3\delta^{3},𝒢3\mathcal{G}_{3},𝒢2​δ\mathcal{G}_{2}\deltaadd nothing new and areredundantat this order.
In practice, it is convenient to usedimensional regularizationscheme, in which all convergent integrals are evaluated atΛ→∞\Lambda\to\infty,
but all divergent integrals are
set to zero[60,72].777See[73,74]for an explicit cutoff scheme.In this schemeσΛ2=0\sigma^{2}_{\Lambda}=0,
sob1b_{1}measured from a linear bias fit is the same asb1b_{1}measured
at the one-loop order.

The only cubic operator that
survives in the one-loop power spectrum isΓ3\Gamma_{3}.
The
independent deterministic parameters at one loop are thereforeb1,b2,b𝒢2,bΓ3,b∇2δ,b_{1}\,,\quad b_{2}\,,\quad b_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\,,\quad b_{\Gamma_{3}}\,,\quad b_{\nabla^{2}\delta}\,,(201)

supplemented by the stochastic contributions to which we now turn.

## 5.4Galaxy Stochasticity and Shot Noise

Just like matter, the galaxy field contains a component that is not determined by the
long-wavelength fields at all:δg=δgdet+ε,⟨δgdet​ε⟩=0.\delta_{g}=\delta_{g}^{\mathrm{det}}+\varepsilon\,,\qquad\langle\delta_{g}^{\mathrm{det}}\,\varepsilon\rangle=0\,.(202)

This stochastic field is required both physically — galaxy
formation depends on the small-scale environment, which is
decoupled from the long modes — and by consistency of the EFT.
Indeed, consider theP22P_{22}-type contribution of the quadratic
bias:Pg​g​(k)⊃b224​Pδ2​δ2​(k)=b222​∫d3​q​P11​(q)​P11​(|𝒌−𝒒|).P_{gg}(k)\supset\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4}P_{\delta^{2}\delta^{2}}(k)=\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{2}\int d^{3}q\;P_{11}(q)\,P_{11}(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)\,.(203)

In the UV limitk→0k\to 0:Pδ2​δ2|k→0≈b222​[∫0kNLd3​q​P112​(q)+∫kNLΛd3​q​P112​(q)]=calculable constant+C0Λ.P_{\delta^{2}\delta^{2}}\Big|_{k\to 0}\approx\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{2}\left[\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}d^{3}q\;P_{11}^{2}(q)+\int_{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}^{\Lambda}d^{3}q\;P_{11}^{2}(q)\right]=\text{calculable constant}+C_{0}^{\Lambda}\,.(204)

The small scales decouple and produceconstant,kk-independent power — including a cutoff-dependent pieceC0ΛC_{0}^{\Lambda}that must be cancelled by a constant counterterm.
This is supplied by the stochastic field: sinceε\varepsilonis
generated atq≳kNLq\gtrsim k_{\mathrm{NL}}, its power spectrum is analytic fork≪kNLk\ll k_{\mathrm{NL}},⟨ε​ε⟩𝒌′=α0+α1​k2+⋯,\langle\varepsilon\,\varepsilon\rangle^{\prime}_{{\bm{k}}}=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}\,k^{2}+\cdots\,,(205)

and the total power⟨δg​δg⟩′=⟨δgdet​δgdet⟩′+⟨ε​ε⟩′\langle\delta_{g}\delta_{g}\rangle^{\prime}=\langle\delta_{g}^{\mathrm{det}}\delta_{g}^{\mathrm{det}}\rangle^{\prime}+\langle\varepsilon\varepsilon\rangle^{\prime}is rendered cutoff-independent by the splitα0=α0Λ+α0fin\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}^{\Lambda}+\alpha_{0}^{\mathrm{fin}}, withC0Λ+α0ΛC_{0}^{\Lambda}+\alpha_{0}^{\Lambda}finite. Note the contrast with the
matter field: there, mass and momentum conservation (Peebles’
argument of Chapter 5) forced the stochastic power to scale ask4k^{4}. The galaxynumberdensity obeys no such conservation
laws: the galaxies keep forming by collapsing matter
and being destroyed via merging throughout the cosmic history.
Thus a constantα0\alpha_{0}is allowed and is indeed present.

Shot noise.How large isα0\alpha_{0}? A useful estimate comes from a set ofNNrandomly placed points (Poisson sampling) with mean densityn¯=N/V\bar{n}=N/V. The density fluctuations thenδg​(𝒙)=1n¯​∑i=1NδD(3)​(𝒙−𝒙i)−1⟹δg​(𝒌)=1n¯​∑iei​𝒌⋅𝒙i,𝒌≠0.\delta_{g}({\bm{x}})=\frac{1}{\bar{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{x}}-{\bm{x}}_{i})-1\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\delta_{g}({\bm{k}})=\frac{1}{\bar{n}}\sum_{i}e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}_{i}}\,,\quad{\bm{k}}\neq 0\,.(206)

Noting thatδD(3)​(𝒌=0)=V\delta^{(3)}_{D}({\bm{k}}=0)=V, the power spectrum is thenP​(k)=⟨δg​(𝒌)​δg​(−𝒌)⟩V=1V​n¯2​∑i,j⟨ei​𝒌⋅(𝒙i−𝒙j)⟩=1V​n¯2​(∑i=j1⏟=N+∑i≠j⟨ei​𝒌⋅(𝒙i−𝒙j)⟩⏟=0​(no correlations))=1n¯.P(k)=\frac{\langle\delta_{g}({\bm{k}})\,\delta_{g}(-{\bm{k}})\rangle}{V}=\frac{1}{V\bar{n}^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\langle e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot({\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j})}\rangle=\frac{1}{V\bar{n}^{2}}\Big(\underbrace{\sum_{i=j}1}_{=\,N}+\underbrace{\sum_{i\neq j}\langle e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot({\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}_{j})}\rangle}_{=\,0\;\text{(no correlations)}}\Big)=\frac{1}{\bar{n}}\,.(207)

This is thePoisson shot noise. It sets the natural
normalization of the stochastic power,⟨ε​ε⟩𝒌′=1n¯​(α~0+α~1​k2kstoch2+⋯),\langle\varepsilon\,\varepsilon\rangle^{\prime}_{{\bm{k}}}=\frac{1}{\bar{n}}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{0}+\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\,\frac{k^{2}}{k_{\mathrm{stoch}}^{2}}+\cdots\right)\,,(208)

withα~0=1\tilde{\alpha}_{0}=1for a pure Poisson process. In practiceO​(1)O(1)deviations are expected, e.g. fromhalo exclusion[75,71](halos have finite size and cannot overlap) and from the non-linear
environment dependence of galaxy formation, soα~0\tilde{\alpha}_{0}must
be treated as a free parameter.

Finally, let us make a comment
on the calculable part of thePδ2​δ2P_{\delta^{2}\delta^{2}}integral in Eq. (204),Cδ2​δ2=b222​∫0kNLd3​q​P112​(q).C_{\delta^{2}\delta^{2}}=\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{NL}}}d^{3}q\;P_{11}^{2}(q)~\,.(209)

Based on naturalness, this part is physical, and hence should be
present in theory predictions. However, since it is fully degenerate with the shot noise, in practice it is often
subtracted from the deterministic power spectrum.
This does not change
the estimate (208) becauseCδ2​δ2C_{\delta^{2}\delta^{2}}in (209) is typically
smaller than the true shot noise of the realistic galaxy samples.888As a point of comparison, we can consider DESI DR1 LRG2 sample atz=0.7z=0.7,
which hasb2≈0.3b_{2}\approx 0.3,Cδ2​δ2≈42​[Mpc/h]3C_{\delta^{2}\delta^{2}}\approx 42~[\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h]^{3}andn¯−1≃4.7⋅103​[Mpc/h]3\bar{n}^{-1}\simeq 4.7\cdot 10^{3}~[\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h]^{3}[76].However, there are important exceptions: the neutral hydrogen (HI) field[77]and the Lyman-α\alphaforest[78,79].
These tracers have tiny
physical stochastic power spectrum, soCδ2​δ2≫⟨ε​ε⟩𝒌′C_{\delta^{2}\delta^{2}}\gg\langle\varepsilon\,\varepsilon\rangle^{\prime}_{{\bm{k}}}. AbsorbingCδ2​δ2C_{\delta^{2}\delta^{2}}into⟨ε​ε⟩𝒌′\langle\varepsilon\,\varepsilon\rangle^{\prime}_{{\bm{k}}}is a bad idea in this case because
this inflates the amplitude ofα~0\tilde{\alpha}_{0}that one has to allow in the fit. In such cases it is more practical
to keepCδ2​δ2C_{\delta^{2}\delta^{2}}as part of the deterministic power
spectrum.Figure 15:One-loop galaxy power spectrum basis shapes for the fiducial cosmology atz=0z=0.Figure 16:EFT fits to galaxy power spectrum and galaxy-matter
cross-spectrum of the mock luminous red galaxy sample from PT Challenge N-body simulation atz=0.61z=0.61.Left:One-loop predictions discussed in the main text.
The EFT fit is accurate up tokmax=0.29​h​Mpc−1k_{\rm max}=0.29~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}(depicted by the vertical dashed line).Right:Two-loop EFT predictions from Ref.[80], accurate up tokmax=0.85​h​Mpc−1k_{\rm max}=0.85~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}.

## 5.5Comparison with Simulation Data

The Final Theory Prediction.Collecting all the terms, the one-loop galaxy auto power spectrum readsPg​g​(k)=b12​(P11​(k)+Δ​P1​-​loop​(k))+b1​b2​ℐδ2​(k)+2​b1​b𝒢2​ℐ𝒢2​(k)+b1​(2​b𝒢2+45​bΓ3)​ℱ𝒢2​(k)+14​b22​ℐδ2​δ2​(k)+bG22​ℐ𝒢2​𝒢2​(k)+12​b2​b𝒢2​ℐδ2​𝒢2​(k)+P∇2δ​(k)+Pε​ε​(k),\begin{split}P_{gg}(k)=&~b^{2}_{1}\left(P_{11}(k)+\Delta P_{\rm 1\text{-}loop}(k)\right)+b_{1}b_{2}\,\mathcal{I}_{\delta^{2}}(k)+2\,b_{1}b_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\,\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(k)\\
&+b_{1}\left(2\,b_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}+\frac{4}{5}\,b_{\Gamma_{3}}\right)\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(k)+\frac{1}{4}\,b^{2}_{2}\,\mathcal{I}_{\delta^{2}\delta^{2}}(k)+b^{2}_{G_{2}}\,\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}\mathcal{G}_{2}}(k)\\
&+\frac{1}{2}b_{2}\,b_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\,\mathcal{I}_{\delta^{2}\mathcal{G}_{2}}(k)+P_{\nabla^{2}\delta}(k)+P_{\varepsilon\varepsilon}(k)\,,\end{split}(210)

where all spectra are evaluated at the same redshift (the time
dependence is left implicit),Pε​εP_{\varepsilon\varepsilon}is the
stochastic power spectrum of §5.4, and we abbreviated the Fourier-space
kernel of the tidal operator𝒢2\mathcal{G}_{2}(the same combination that appears
in the quadratic galaxy kernel (195)):𝒮​(𝒌1,𝒌2)≡(𝒌1⋅𝒌2)2k12​k22−1.\mathcal{S}({\bm{k}}_{1},{\bm{k}}_{2})\equiv\frac{({\bm{k}}_{1}\cdot{\bm{k}}_{2})^{2}}{k_{1}^{2}\,k_{2}^{2}}-1\,.(211)

The basis loop integrals are defined as999Note we have an extra factor of 2 inℐδ2​𝒢2\mathcal{I}_{\delta^{2}\mathcal{G}_{2}}compared to[72,15].ℐδ2​(k)≡2​∫𝒒F2​(𝒒,𝒌−𝒒)​P11​(|𝒌−𝒒|)​P11​(q),\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\delta^{2}}(k)\equiv 2\int_{\bm{q}}F_{2}({\bm{q}},{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}})\,P_{11}(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)\,P_{11}(q)\,,(212a)ℐ𝒢2​(k)≡2​∫𝒒𝒮​(𝒒,𝒌−𝒒)​F2​(𝒒,𝒌−𝒒)​P11​(|𝒌−𝒒|)​P11​(q),\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(k)\equiv 2\int_{\bm{q}}\mathcal{S}({\bm{q}},{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}})\,F_{2}({\bm{q}},{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}})\,P_{11}(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)\,P_{11}(q)\,,(212b)ℱ𝒢2​(k)≡4​P11​(k)​∫𝒒𝒮​(𝒒,𝒌−𝒒)​F2​(𝒌,−𝒒)​P11​(q),\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(k)\equiv 4\,P_{11}(k)\int_{\bm{q}}\mathcal{S}({\bm{q}},{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}})\,F_{2}({\bm{k}},-{\bm{q}})\,P_{11}(q)\,,(212c)ℐδ2​δ2​(k)≡2​∫𝒒P11​(|𝒌−𝒒|)​P11​(q)−2​∫𝒒P112​(q),\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\delta^{2}\delta^{2}}(k)\equiv 2\int_{\bm{q}}P_{11}(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)\,P_{11}(q)-2\int_{\bm{q}}P^{2}_{11}(q)\,,(212d)ℐ𝒢2​𝒢2​(k)≡2​∫𝒒𝒮2​(𝒒,𝒌−𝒒)​P11​(|𝒌−𝒒|)​P11​(q),\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}\mathcal{G}_{2}}(k)\equiv 2\int_{\bm{q}}\mathcal{S}^{2}({\bm{q}},{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}})\,P_{11}(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)\,P_{11}(q)\,,(212e)ℐδ2​𝒢2​(k)≡4​∫𝒒𝒮​(𝒒,𝒌−𝒒)​P11​(|𝒌−𝒒|)​P11​(q),\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\delta^{2}\mathcal{G}_{2}}(k)\equiv 4\int_{\bm{q}}\mathcal{S}({\bm{q}},{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}})\,P_{11}(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)\,P_{11}(q)\,,(212f)P∇2δ​(k)≡−2​b1​(b∇2δ+γ​b1)​k2​P11​(k),\displaystyle P_{\nabla^{2}\delta}(k)\equiv-2\,b_{1}\left(b_{\nabla^{2}\delta}+\gamma\,b_{1}\right)k^{2}\,P_{11}(k)\,,(212g)

whereγ\gammais the matter counterterm of Chapter 4. Note that the

The one-loop bias shapes for our fiducial cosmology
atz=0z=0are displayed in Fig.15.

The galaxy–matter
cross spectrum relevant for lensing surveys
is obtained in the same way:Pg​m​(k)=b1​(P11​(k)+Δ​P1​-​loop​(k))+12​b2​ℐδ2​(k)+b𝒢2​ℐ𝒢2​(k)+(b𝒢2+25​bΓ3)​ℱ𝒢2​(k)−(b∇2δ+2​γ​b1)​k2​P11​(k)+Pε​εm​(k).\begin{split}P_{gm}(k)=&~b_{1}\left(P_{11}(k)+\Delta P_{\rm 1\text{-}loop}(k)\right)+\frac{1}{2}\,b_{2}\,\mathcal{I}_{\delta^{2}}(k)+b_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}\,\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(k)\\
&+\left(b_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}+\frac{2}{5}\,b_{\Gamma_{3}}\right)\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}(k)-\left(b_{\nabla^{2}\delta}+2\,\gamma\,b_{1}\right)k^{2}\,P_{11}(k)+P_{\varepsilon\varepsilon_{m}}(k)\,.\end{split}(213)

An important point is that the stochastic cross-spectrumPε​εm​(k)P_{\varepsilon\varepsilon_{m}}(k)involves a matter field now, which scales ask2k^{2}atk→0k\to 0. Therefore,
its EFT expansion starts atO​(k2)O(k^{2}):Pε​εm=1n¯​α~1′​k2kstoch2+⋯.P_{\varepsilon\varepsilon_{m}}=\frac{1}{\bar{n}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}_{1}\,\frac{k^{2}}{k_{\mathrm{stoch}}^{2}}+\cdots\,.(214)

IR resummation.The bias expansion (191)
is built from operators that satisfy the equivalence principle, and thus
they do not involve locally unobservable displacements, which
violate the perturbative expansion for the BAO. All the displacements
in the Eulerian bias expansion,
which do not cancel for modesq≳koscq\gtrsim k_{\mathrm{osc}}and require resummation,
are produced by the SPT evolution. These can be resummed in much the same way
as the offending contributions in the matter case. The result is the same expression
(114) but applied now to the galaxy power spectrum.

Comparison with simulations.The EFT fits to the galaxy power spectrum
and galaxy-matter cross-spectra
from the PT Challenge simulation atz=0.61z=0.61[81]are shown in Figure16.
The left panel shows the one-loop fit, while the right panel
displays the
two-loop EFT model[82,80,74], which is currently the state-of-the-art.
Note that at the two-loop order the one-loop operatorsδ3,δ​𝒢2\delta^{3},\delta\mathcal{G}_{2}and𝒢3\mathcal{G}_{3}are not redundant anymore.

A comment on scales.The EFT of galaxies contains severala prioridifferent expansion
scales: the matter non-linear scalekNLk_{\mathrm{NL}}, the higher-derivative
bias scale (b∇2δ−1/2∼R∗−1b_{\nabla^{2}\delta}^{-1/2}\sim R_{*}^{-1}), and the
stochastic scalekstochk_{\mathrm{stoch}}controlling the
scale-dependence of the stochasticity. For a generic galaxy sample
these may beparametrically different, and which one fails
first depends on the sample.
For instance, massive halos and galaxies residing in them atz∼0.5z\sim 0.5have largeR∗≃5​[Mpc/h]2R_{*}\simeq 5~[\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h]^{2}, so the gradient expansion
breaks down atk≃0.4​h​Mpc−1k\simeq 0.4~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}for them[58,83].
At the same time the stochasticity
scale
is typically quite low even for massive halos,kstoch∼1​h​Mpc−1k_{\mathrm{stoch}}\sim 1~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}.

Power counting.As a result of having different scales in the expansion, the power counting
rules are somewhat different for galaxies. Whilst the galaxy bias coefficients
do not change the power counting parametrically, e.g.b1∼O​(1)b_{1}\sim O(1)for realistic samples, the counting of the stochasticity is quite different.
Galaxy samples used in real spectroscopic surveys satisfy101010See e.g. DESI DR1 samples[76,84].Pg​g​(k=0.2​h​Mpc−1)​n¯≃1P_{gg}(k=0.2~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1})\bar{n}\simeq 1, so that at the BAO scalesPϵ​ϵ∼n¯−1P_{\epsilon\epsilon}\sim\bar{n}^{-1}has the same counting as the linear power spectrum.
Then thek2k^{2}stochasticity scales as one-loop deterministic power
spectrum. We stress, however, that the counting is different for other
galaxy samples. For instance, photometric galaxies used in
imaging surveys havePg​g​(k=0.2​h​Mpc−1)​n¯≫1P_{gg}(k=0.2~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1})\bar{n}\gg 1[85,86]. On the opposite part of the spectrum we have
quasars, which live in the regimePg​g​(k=0.2​h​Mpc−1)​n¯≪1P_{gg}(k=0.2~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1})\bar{n}\ll 1[87].
The great advantage of EFT is that it provides the power counting rules,
which we can use to estimate what terms are important for any
galaxy sample in question.

## 6Chapter 6: Redshift-Space Distortions

There is one more distortion separating us from the observations.
A galaxy survey does not measure positions: it measures two angles
and aredshift,(α,δ,z)(\alpha,\delta,z), which are converted into
Cartesian coordinates assuming an FRW background. The observed
redshift, however, contains a Doppler contribution from the peculiar
velocity of the galaxy on top of the Hubble flow,zobs=H​rphys+vpec,∥≡H​sRSD,z_{\mathrm{obs}}=H\,r_{\mathrm{phys}}+v_{\mathrm{pec,\parallel}}\equiv H\,s_{\mathrm{RSD}}\,,(215)

so the inferred (“redshift-space”) distancesRSDs_{\mathrm{RSD}}differs from the true one. These are theredshift-space distortions(RSD).

## 6.1The Mapping from Real to Redshift Space

In comoving units and in the plane-parallel (distant-observer)
approximation, with the line of sight alongz^=(0,0,1)\hat{z}=(0,0,1), the
mapping reads𝒔=𝒙+vzℋz^,\boxed{{\bm{s}}={\bm{x}}+\frac{v_{z}}{\mathcal{H}}\;\hat{z}\,,}(216)

wherevz=𝒗⋅z^v_{z}={\bm{v}}\cdot\hat{z}is the line-of-sight peculiar
velocity andℋ\mathcal{H}is the conformal Hubble rate. The mapping
merelyreshufflesthe galaxies along the line of sight, so
their number is conserved:(1+δg(s)​(𝒔))​d3​s=(1+δg​(𝒙))​d3​x.\left(1+\delta_{g}^{(s)}({\bm{s}})\right)d^{3}s=\left(1+\delta_{g}({\bm{x}})\right)d^{3}x\,.(217)

Multiplying bye−i​𝒌⋅𝒔e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{s}}}and integrating, we obtain anexactexpression for the redshift-space density field in
Fourier space:δg(s)​(𝒌)=δg​(𝒌)+∫d3​x​e−i​𝒌⋅𝒙​(e−i​kz​vz​(𝒙)/ℋ−1)​(1+δg​(𝒙)).\delta_{g}^{(s)}({\bm{k}})=\delta_{g}({\bm{k}})+\int d^{3}x\;e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}}\left(e^{-ik_{z}v_{z}({\bm{x}})/\mathcal{H}}-1\right)\left(1+\delta_{g}({\bm{x}})\right)\,.(218)

Note that this expression is fully non-perturbative in the velocity
field — all the approximations will enter only when we start
expanding the exponential.

## 6.2Kaiser Formula (Linear Theory)

Let us first linearize Eq. (218). It is
convenient to introduce the rescaled velocityuz≡vz/(f​ℋ)u_{z}\equiv v_{z}/(f\mathcal{H}), in terms of which the expansion
parameter isf​kz​uzfk_{z}u_{z}. To linear order,δg(s)​(𝒌)=b1​δ𝒌+∫d3​x​e−i​𝒌⋅𝒙​(−i​f​kz​uz​(𝒙)).\delta_{g}^{(s)}({\bm{k}})=b_{1}\,\delta_{{\bm{k}}}+\int d^{3}x\;e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}}\left(-ifk_{z}\,u_{z}({\bm{x}})\right)\,.(219)

For the linear growing modeui=−∂iδ1/Δu^{i}=-\partial^{i}\delta_{1}/\Delta(cf. §3.2), so in Fourier spaceuz​(𝒌)=i​(kz/k2)​δ1​(𝒌)u_{z}({\bm{k}})=i\,(k_{z}/k^{2})\,\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})andδg(s)​(𝒌)=b1​δ1​(𝒌)+f​kz2k2​δ1​(𝒌).\delta_{g}^{(s)}({\bm{k}})=b_{1}\,\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})+f\,\frac{k_{z}^{2}}{k^{2}}\,\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\,.(220)

We arrive at theKaiser formula[88]:δg(s)(𝒌)=(b1+fμ2)δ1(𝒌),μ≡kzk,\boxed{\delta_{g}^{(s)}({\bm{k}})=\left(b_{1}+f\mu^{2}\right)\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\,,\qquad\mu\equiv\frac{k_{z}}{k}\,,}(221)

and the linear (tree-level) redshift-space power spectrum⟨δg(s)​δg(s)⟩lin′=(b1+f​μ2)2​P11​(k).\langle\delta_{g}^{(s)}\,\delta_{g}^{(s)}\rangle^{\prime}_{\mathrm{lin}}=\left(b_{1}+f\mu^{2}\right)^{2}P_{11}(k)\,.(222)Physical InterpretationSquashing effect:Coherent infall velocities towards overdensities enhance the apparent clustering along the line of sight. This is captured by the byf​μ2f\mu^{2}term in the Kaiser formula.
This term also breaks isotropy: the power spectrum depends onμ=cos⁡θk,z^\mu=\cos\theta_{k,\hat{z}}.

Fingers of God.Virialized motions within clusters create elongation of structures along the line of sight in redshift space[89].
This is anon-perturbativeeffect: the velocities inside virialized halos are large and random.
In the EFT framework, Fingers of God are absorbed into the counterterms and stochastic contributions, as we will see below.

## 6.3Multipole Expansion

The angular dependence is conveniently expanded in Legendre
polynomials,Pg(s)​(k,μ)=∑ℓPℓ​(k)​ℒℓ​(μ),Pℓ​(k)=2​ℓ+12​∫−11𝑑μ​Pg(s)​(k,μ)​ℒℓ​(μ).P_{g}^{(s)}(k,\mu)=\sum_{\ell}P_{\ell}(k)\,\mathcal{L}_{\ell}(\mu)\,,\qquad P_{\ell}(k)=\frac{2\ell+1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}d\mu\;P_{g}^{(s)}(k,\mu)\;\mathcal{L}_{\ell}(\mu)\,.(223)

Expanding the Kaiser result(b1+f​μ2)2=b12+2​b1​f​μ2+f2​μ4(b_{1}+f\mu^{2})^{2}=b_{1}^{2}+2b_{1}f\mu^{2}+f^{2}\mu^{4}with the help ofℒ2=(3​μ2−1)/2\mathcal{L}_{2}=(3\mu^{2}-1)/2andℒ4=(35​μ4−30​μ2+3)/8\mathcal{L}_{4}=(35\mu^{4}-30\mu^{2}+3)/8,
we find that onlyℓ=0,2,4\ell=0,2,4are non-zero at tree level:P0​(k)\displaystyle P_{0}(k)=(b12+23​b1​f+15​f2)​P11​(k),\displaystyle=\left(b_{1}^{2}+\frac{2}{3}\,b_{1}f+\frac{1}{5}\,f^{2}\right)P_{11}(k)\,,(224)P2​(k)\displaystyle P_{2}(k)=(43​b1​f+47​f2)​P11​(k),\displaystyle=\left(\frac{4}{3}\,b_{1}f+\frac{4}{7}\,f^{2}\right)P_{11}(k)\,,(225)P4​(k)\displaystyle P_{4}(k)=835​f2​P11​(k).\displaystyle=\frac{8}{35}\,f^{2}\,P_{11}(k)\,.(226)

Non-linear evolution generates higher multipoles at loop level, but
the bulk of the information resides inℓ=0,2,4\ell=0,2,4.
The reason this basis is convenient to use
is that the multipoles can be
easily estimated from the data[90].

## 6.4Beyond Linear Order — RSD Kernels

To go beyond linear theory, we expand the exponential in
Eq. (218) in powers off​kz​uzfk_{z}u_{z}. For the
one-loop power spectrum we need terms up to cubic order:δg(s)​(𝒌)=δg​(𝒌)−i​f​kz​[(1+δg)​uz]𝒌+(i​f​kz)22​[(1+δg)​uz2]𝒌+(i​f​kz)36​[(1+δg)​uz3]𝒌,\delta_{g}^{(s)}({\bm{k}})=\delta_{g}({\bm{k}})-ifk_{z}\left[(1+\delta_{g})\,u_{z}\right]_{{\bm{k}}}+\frac{(ifk_{z})^{2}}{2}\left[(1+\delta_{g})\,u_{z}^{2}\right]_{{\bm{k}}}+\frac{(ifk_{z})^{3}}{6}\left[(1+\delta_{g})\,u_{z}^{3}\right]_{{\bm{k}}}\,,(227)

where[f]𝒌≡∫d3​x​e−i​𝒌⋅𝒙​f​(𝒙)[f]_{{\bm{k}}}\equiv\int d^{3}x\;e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}}f({\bm{x}}).
Substituting the perturbative expansions ofδg\delta_{g}(bias
kernelsKnK_{n}) and of the velocity (SPT kernelsGnG_{n}) defines theredshift-space kernelsZnZ_{n}:δg(s)​(𝒌)=∑n=1∞(2​π)3​∫𝒒1​⋯​𝒒nδD(3)​(𝒌−𝒒1​⋯​n)​Zn​(𝒒1,…,𝒒n)​δ1​(𝒒1)​⋯​δ1​(𝒒n).\delta_{g}^{(s)}({\bm{k}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(2\pi)^{3}\int_{{\bm{q}}_{1}\cdots{\bm{q}}_{n}}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}_{1\cdots n})\;Z_{n}({\bm{q}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{q}}_{n})\;\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}_{1})\cdots\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}_{n})\,.(228)

The first two kernels areZ1​(𝒌)\displaystyle Z_{1}({\bm{k}})=b1+f​μ2,\displaystyle=b_{1}+f\mu^{2}\,,(229)Z2​(𝒒1,𝒒2)\displaystyle Z_{2}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})=K2​(𝒒1,𝒒2)+f​μ122​G2​(𝒒1,𝒒2)+f​μ12​k122​[μ1q1​(b1+f​μ22)+μ2q2​(b1+f​μ12)],\displaystyle=K_{2}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})+f\mu_{12}^{2}\,G_{2}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})+\frac{f\mu_{12}k_{12}}{2}\left[\frac{\mu_{1}}{q_{1}}\left(b_{1}+f\mu_{2}^{2}\right)+\frac{\mu_{2}}{q_{2}}\left(b_{1}+f\mu_{1}^{2}\right)\right]\,,(230)

whereμi=qi,z/qi\mu_{i}=q_{i,z}/q_{i}andμ12=(q1,z+q2,z)/|𝒒1+𝒒2|\mu_{12}=(q_{1,z}+q_{2,z})/|{\bm{q}}_{1}+{\bm{q}}_{2}|;
the expression forZ3Z_{3}is analogous but lengthy (see e.g.[91]).
The deterministic one-loop redshift-space power spectrum is then built exactly as
in real space,Pg​g,1​-loop(s)​(k,μ)=Z12​P11​(k)+2​∫𝒒Z22​(𝒌−𝒒,𝒒)​P11​(|𝒌−𝒒|)​P11​(q)+6​Z1​(𝒌)​∫𝒒Z3​(𝒌,𝒒,−𝒒)​P11​(k)​P11​(q).\begin{split}P_{gg,\,1\text{-loop}}^{(s)}(k,\mu)=&Z_{1}^{2}\,P_{11}(k)+2\int_{\bm{q}}Z_{2}^{2}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}},{\bm{q}})\,P_{11}(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}|)P_{11}(q)\\
&+6\,Z_{1}({\bm{k}})\int_{\bm{q}}Z_{3}({\bm{k}},{\bm{q}},-{\bm{q}})\,P_{11}(k)P_{11}(q)\,.\end{split}(231)

## 6.5Renormalization of Contact Operators

The building blocks of the RSD expansion (227)
arecontact operators: products of fields evaluated at the
same point, such as[δg​(𝒙)​ui​(𝒙)]𝒌\left[\delta_{g}({\bm{x}})\,u^{i}({\bm{x}})\right]_{{\bm{k}}}.
In Fourier space such a product is a convolution overallmomenta, so it is UV-sensitive: short modes of both fields
contribute to it even when the external momentumkkis small.
These operators must be renormalized[92,93,94].

The procedure follows the same logic as everywhere in this course.
Split every field into long- and short-wavelength parts,δg=δgℓ+δgs,ui=uiℓ+uis,\delta_{g}=\delta_{g}^{\ell}+\delta_{g}^{s}\,,\qquad u_{i}=u_{i}^{\ell}+u_{i}^{s}\,,(232)

and average over the short modes with the long modes held fixed;
we denote this average by⟨…⟩s.s.\langle\ldots\rangle_{\mathrm{s.s.}}. This is
exactly theBorn–Oppenheimerlogic of molecular physics:
the fast (short-scale) degrees of freedom are integrated out in the
background of the slow (long-wavelength) ones, and the result is an
effective operator built from the slow fields.

As a warm-up, consider the galaxy density itself:⟨δg⟩s.s.=δgℓ+⟨δgs⟩s.s..\langle\delta_{g}\rangle_{\mathrm{s.s.}}=\delta_{g}^{\ell}+\langle\delta_{g}^{s}\rangle_{\mathrm{s.s.}}\,.(233)

The short-mode average in the presence of the long fields is itself
a function of the long fields,⟨δgs⟩s.s.=∑O~zO~​O~=b1′​δℓ+b2′​δℓ2+⋯\langle\delta_{g}^{s}\rangle_{\mathrm{s.s.}}=\sum_{\tilde{O}}z_{\tilde{O}}\,\tilde{O}=b_{1}^{\prime}\,\delta_{\ell}+b_{2}^{\prime}\,\delta_{\ell}^{2}+\cdots,
so it merely reshuffles the bias coefficients:⟨δg⟩s.s.≡δgR\langle\delta_{g}\rangle_{\mathrm{s.s.}}\equiv\delta_{g}^{\mathrm{R}}. Nothing
new here. The novelty appears for the velocity-weighted operators:⟨(1+δg)​ui⟩s.s.=(1+δgℓ)​uiℓ+⟨δgs⟩s.s.​uiℓ+⟨(1+δgℓ+δgs)​uis⟩s.s.≡(1+δgR)​uiℓ+Oui,\langle(1+\delta_{g})\,u_{i}\rangle_{\mathrm{s.s.}}=(1+\delta_{g}^{\ell})\,u_{i}^{\ell}+\langle\delta_{g}^{s}\rangle_{\mathrm{s.s.}}\,u_{i}^{\ell}+\langle(1+\delta_{g}^{\ell}+\delta_{g}^{s})\,u_{i}^{s}\rangle_{\mathrm{s.s.}}\equiv\left(1+\delta_{g}^{\mathrm{R}}\right)u_{i}^{\ell}+O_{u}^{i}\,,(234)

where the last term defines a genuinely new operatorOuiO_{u}^{i}: the
response of the short-scale “atmosphere” to the long-wavelength
environment. Note thatOuiO_{u}^{i}is ascalar under Galilean
transformations: a boost is carried entirely byuiℓu_{i}^{\ell}, while
the short modes only know about locally observable quantities.
Analogously,⟨(1+δg)​ui​uj⟩s.s.\displaystyle\langle(1+\delta_{g})\,u_{i}u_{j}\rangle_{\mathrm{s.s.}}=(1+δgR)​uiℓ​ujℓ+uiℓ​Ouj+ujℓ​Oui+Ou2i​j,\displaystyle=\left(1+\delta_{g}^{\mathrm{R}}\right)u_{i}^{\ell}u_{j}^{\ell}+u_{i}^{\ell}\,O_{u}^{j}+u_{j}^{\ell}\,O_{u}^{i}+O_{u^{2}}^{ij}\,,(235)⟨(1+δg)​ui​uj​uk⟩s.s.\displaystyle\langle(1+\delta_{g})\,u_{i}u_{j}u_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{s.s.}}=(1+δgR)​uiℓ​ujℓ​ukℓ+(uiℓ​ujℓ​Ouk+2 perm.)+(uiℓ​Ou2j​k+2 perm.)+Ou3i​j​k.\displaystyle=(1+\delta^{R}_{g})u_{i}^{\ell}u_{j}^{\ell}u_{k}^{\ell}+(u^{\ell}_{i}u^{\ell}_{j}O_{u}^{k}+\text{2 perm.})+(u^{\ell}_{i}O_{u^{2}}^{jk}+\text{2 perm.})+O_{u^{3}}^{ijk}\,.(236)

The EFT now tells us what these operators can be. By symmetries
(Galilean invariance, the equivalence principle, rotational
invariance) and dimensional analysis, at leading order in
perturbations and derivatives:Oui=c1​∂iδ+⋯,Ou2i​j=c2​δi​j​δ+c3​∂i∂jΦ^+⋯O_{u}^{i}=c_{1}\,\partial^{i}\delta+\cdots\,,\qquad O_{u^{2}}^{ij}=c_{2}\,\delta^{ij}\,\delta+c_{3}\,\partial^{i}\partial^{j}\hat{\Phi}+\cdots(237)

A Galilean-invariant vector must be a gradient of a scalar, and a
symmetric tensor admits exactly the two structures shown.

Let us now insert the renormalized operators into the
mapping (227) and project onto the line of
sight. Using∂i∂jΦ^→(ki​kj/k2)​δ\partial^{i}\partial^{j}\hat{\Phi}\to(k^{i}k^{j}/k^{2})\,\deltain Fourier space, we find−i​f​kz​z^i​[⟨(1+δg)​ui⟩s.s.]𝒌\displaystyle-ifk_{z}\,\hat{z}_{i}\left[\langle(1+\delta_{g})u^{i}\rangle_{\mathrm{s.s.}}\right]_{{\bm{k}}}=−i​f​kz​z^i​[(1+δgR)​uℓi]𝒌+c1​f​kz2​δ1​(𝒌),\displaystyle=-ifk_{z}\,\hat{z}_{i}\left[(1+\delta_{g}^{\mathrm{R}})\,u^{i}_{\ell}\right]_{{\bm{k}}}+c_{1}f\,k_{z}^{2}\,\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\,,(238)(i​f​kz)22​z^i​z^j​[⟨(1+δg)​ui​uj⟩s.s.]𝒌\displaystyle\frac{(ifk_{z})^{2}}{2}\,\hat{z}_{i}\hat{z}_{j}\left[\langle(1+\delta_{g})u^{i}u^{j}\rangle_{\mathrm{s.s.}}\right]_{{\bm{k}}}=−f2​kz22​[(1+δgR)​uℓi​uℓj​z^i​z^j]𝒌−f2​kz22​(c2+c3​μ2)​δ1​(𝒌),\displaystyle=-\frac{f^{2}k_{z}^{2}}{2}\left[(1+\delta_{g}^{\mathrm{R}})\,u_{\ell}^{i}u_{\ell}^{j}\,\hat{z}_{i}\hat{z}_{j}\right]_{{\bm{k}}}-\frac{f^{2}k_{z}^{2}}{2}\left(c_{2}+c_{3}\,\mu^{2}\right)\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\,,(239)

where all higher-order terms inδ1\delta_{1}have been dropped (they
contribute beyond one loop). Collecting the new pieces together
with the real-space higher-derivative bias, the renormalized
redshift-space field takes the formδg(s)=δSPT(s)−b∇2δ​k2​δ1+kz2​(c1​f−12​f2​c2+12​f2​c3​μ2)​δ1,\delta_{g}^{(s)}=\delta^{(s)}_{\mathrm{SPT}}-b_{\nabla^{2}\delta}\,k^{2}\,\delta_{1}+k_{z}^{2}\left(c_{1}f-\frac{1}{2}\,f^{2}c_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,f^{2}c_{3}\,\mu^{2}\right)\delta_{1}\,,(240)

(the signs of the arbitrary constants are conventional). Usingkz2=k2​μ2k_{z}^{2}=k^{2}\mu^{2}, all counterterms combine intoΔδctr(s)(𝒌)=k2δ1(𝒌)[C0+C~1fμ2+C~2f2μ4],\boxed{\Delta\delta^{(s)}_{\mathrm{ctr}}({\bm{k}})=k^{2}\,\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})\left[C_{0}+\tilde{C}_{1}\,f\mu^{2}+\tilde{C}_{2}\,f^{2}\mu^{4}\right]\,,}(241)

whereC0=−b∇2δC_{0}=-b_{\nabla^{2}\delta}is inherited from real space, whileC~1\tilde{C}_{1}andC~2\tilde{C}_{2}are the two genuinely new RSD
counterterms. Correlating with the tree-level field, the
counterterm contribution to the power spectrum isPctr(s)=2​(b1+f​μ2)​[C0+C~1​f​μ2+C~2​f2​μ4]​k2​P11​(k)P^{(s)}_{\mathrm{ctr}}=2\left(b_{1}+f\mu^{2}\right)\left[C_{0}+\tilde{C}_{1}f\mu^{2}+\tilde{C}_{2}f^{2}\mu^{4}\right]k^{2}P_{11}(k).

Renormalization.This structure is enough to renormalize the
one-loop power spectrum in redshift space. Note that there isnoμ6​k2\mu^{6}k^{2}term at the level of the field: the symmetries
allow only the two tensor structures in
Eq. (237), so the angular dependence of the
counterterms truncates atμ4\mu^{4}. This is a non-trivial structural
prediction of the EFT — a purely phenomenological fit would have
no reason to stop atμ4\mu^{4}.

Non-renormalization of the growth factor.Importantly, the condition⟨δg(s)⟩=0\langle\delta_{g}^{(s)}\rangle=0and the linear bias renormalization Eq. (200)
dictate that RSD loopsdo notgenerate power spectrum
corrections∝μ2​P11​σΛ2,μ4​P11​σΛ2\propto\mu^{2}P_{11}\sigma^{2}_{\Lambda},\mu^{4}P_{11}\sigma^{2}_{\Lambda}. Therefore, the cosmology-dependent growth factor
in the Kaiser formula (222) is protected
from complications of the galaxy formation physics.111111A contamination,
however, may happen if the galaxy sample is selected in an orientation-dependent way[95]. However, a
chain of unlucky events need to happen for such “selection effects”
to get generated. First, galaxies must have a strong enough alignment with the ambient tidal field. And second,
they should be preferentially selected based on their orientation.
Selection effects are believed to be small for ongoing surveys
like DESI, though see[96,97]for BOSS.
Also see[98]for galaxy EFT in the presence
of selection effects.

## 6.6Stochasticity in Redshift Space

Exactly as in real space, the operators generated by the short-mode
averaging contain stochastic components, uncorrelated with the
long-wavelength fields:Oui⊃εi,Ou2i​j⊃εi​j.O_{u}^{i}\supset\varepsilon^{i}\,,\qquad O_{u^{2}}^{ij}\supset\varepsilon^{ij}\,.(242)

Retracing the steps of the previous section, the stochastic part of
the redshift-space galaxy field isδg,stoch(s)=ε−i​f​kz​z^i​εi−f2​kz22​z^i​z^j​εi​j.\delta^{(s)}_{g,\mathrm{stoch}}=\varepsilon-ifk_{z}\,\hat{z}_{i}\,\varepsilon^{i}-\frac{f^{2}k_{z}^{2}}{2}\,\hat{z}_{i}\hat{z}_{j}\,\varepsilon^{ij}\,.(243)

The correlators of the stochastic fields are fixed by rotational
invariance of the underlying dynamics (the line of sight is not a
preferred direction of thedynamics) and analyticity:⟨εi​εj⟩′=δi​j​B1+⋯,⟨εi​ε⟩′=i​ki​B2+⋯,⟨εi​j​ε⟩′=δi​j​B3+⋯\langle\varepsilon^{i}\,\varepsilon^{j}\rangle^{\prime}=\delta^{ij}\,B_{1}+\cdots\,,\qquad\langle\varepsilon^{i}\,\varepsilon\rangle^{\prime}=ik^{i}\,B_{2}+\cdots\,,\qquad\langle\varepsilon^{ij}\,\varepsilon\rangle^{\prime}=\delta^{ij}\,B_{3}+\cdots(244)

Assembling the stochastic power spectrum, every new term comes with
two powers ofkzk_{z}(one from each factor of the mapping or one
from the gradient in the cross-correlator), so⟨δg,stoch(s)​δg,stoch(s)⟩′=⟨ε​ε⟩′+A2​kz2=A0+A1​k2+A2​k2​μ2,\langle\delta^{(s)}_{g,\mathrm{stoch}}\,\delta^{(s)}_{g,\mathrm{stoch}}\rangle^{\prime}=\langle\varepsilon\varepsilon\rangle^{\prime}+A_{2}\,k_{z}^{2}=A_{0}+A_{1}\,k^{2}+A_{2}\,k^{2}\mu^{2}\,,(245)

whereA2A_{2}is a combination ofB1B_{1},B2B_{2},B3B_{3}(with the
appropriate powers offf). Normalizing to the Poisson shot noise
as in §5.4,Pstoch(s)​(k,μ)=1n¯​(α~0+α~1​k2kNL2+α~2​k2​μ2kRSD,stoch2).P^{(s)}_{\mathrm{stoch}}(k,\mu)=\frac{1}{\bar{n}}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{0}+\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\,\frac{k^{2}}{k_{\mathrm{NL}}^{2}}+\tilde{\alpha}_{2}\,\frac{k^{2}\mu^{2}}{k_{\mathrm{RSD,stoch}}^{2}}\right)\,.(246)

## 6.7The Alcock–Paczyński effect

Another important effect arises when we convert observed angles and
redshifts to distances, which is called theThe Alcock–Paczyński
(AP) distortions. The spatial part of the flat FRW
metric in comoving spherical coordinates(χ,θ,ϕ)(\chi,\theta,\phi)isd​ℓ2=d​χ2+χ2​d​Ω2,\mathrm{d}\ell^{2}=\mathrm{d}\chi^{2}+\chi^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}\,,(247)

whereχ\chiis comoving distance.
A small radial separation in redshift maps to a comoving distance viad​χ=c​d​z/H​(z)\mathrm{d}\chi=c\,\mathrm{d}z/H(z), while a small angular separationd​θ⟂\mathrm{d}\theta_{\perp}maps to a transverse distanced​ℓ⟂=DM​(z)​d​θ⟂\mathrm{d}\ell_{\perp}=D_{M}(z)\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\perp}, whereDM​(z)=(1+z)​DA​(z)D_{M}(z)=(1+z)\,D_{A}(z)is the comoving angular diameter distance.
Defining Cartesian-like comoving coordinates aligned with the line of
sight,d​x∥=cH​(z)​d​z,d​x⟂=DM​(z)​d​θ⟂,\mathrm{d}x_{\parallel}=\frac{c}{H(z)}\,\mathrm{d}z\,,\qquad\mathrm{d}x_{\perp}=D_{M}(z)\,\mathrm{d}\theta_{\perp}\,,(248)

the line element becomesd​ℓ2=d​x∥2+d​x⟂2\mathrm{d}\ell^{2}=\mathrm{d}x_{\parallel}^{2}+\mathrm{d}x_{\perp}^{2}, as it should in comoving space. Both conversion
factors depend on the cosmology.

## The AP distortion.

In practice one must adopt afiducialcosmology(Hfid,DMfid)(H^{\rm fid},\,D_{M}^{\rm fid})to turn(z,θ⟂)(z,\theta_{\perp})into
distances. If the true cosmology differs, the fiducial and true
coordinates are related byd​x∥fid=q~∥​d​x∥true,d​x⟂fid=q~⟂​d​x⟂true,\mathrm{d}x_{\parallel}^{\rm fid}=\tilde{q}_{\parallel}\,\mathrm{d}x_{\parallel}^{\rm true}\,,\qquad\mathrm{d}x_{\perp}^{\rm fid}=\tilde{q}_{\perp}\,\mathrm{d}x_{\perp}^{\rm true}\,,(249)

with the dilation parametersq~∥=Htrue​(z)Hfid​(z),q~⟂=DMfid​(z)DMtrue​(z).{\;\tilde{q}_{\parallel}=\frac{H^{\rm true}(z)}{H^{\rm fid}(z)}\,,\qquad\tilde{q}_{\perp}=\frac{D_{M}^{\rm fid}(z)}{D_{M}^{\rm true}(z)}\;}\,.(250)

A sphere of radiusrrin true comoving space maps to an ellipsoid
with semi-axesq~∥​r\tilde{q}_{\parallel}\,r(radial) andq~⟂​r\tilde{q}_{\perp}\,r(transverse). The anisotropy is governed by the AP ratioq~AP≡q~∥q~⟂=DMfid​(z)​Htrue​(z)DMtrue​(z)​Hfid​(z),\tilde{q}_{\rm AP}\equiv\frac{\tilde{q}_{\parallel}}{\tilde{q}_{\perp}}=\frac{D_{M}^{\rm fid}(z)\,H^{\rm true}(z)}{D_{M}^{\rm true}(z)\,H^{\rm fid}(z)}\,,(251)

while the isotropic dilation isq~iso=(q~⟂2​q~∥)1/3\tilde{q}_{\rm iso}=(\tilde{q}_{\perp}^{2}\,\tilde{q}_{\parallel})^{1/3}.

## Units and thehh-rescaling.

In practice, Boltzmann codes such asclassoutput
distances in Mpc andH​(z)H(z)in km s-1Mpc-1,
while the data analysis pipeline quotes wavenumbers inhfid/Mpch_{\rm fid}/\text{Mpc}and power spectra in[Mpc/hfid]3[\mathrm{Mpc}/h_{\rm fid}]^{3}, having converted angles and
redshifts using the fiducial Hubble constanthfidh_{\rm fid}.
The natural distance unit for the AP parameters is thereforeh−1​Mpch^{-1}\mathrm{Mpc}, in which thehh-dependence drops out
entirely.

The line-of-sight distance element inh−1​Mpch^{-1}\mathrm{Mpc}isd​x∥=c​d​z/(H/h)=c​d​z/(100​E​(z))\mathrm{d}x_{\parallel}=c\,\mathrm{d}z/(H/h)=c\,\mathrm{d}z/(100\,E(z)),
whereE​(z)≡H​(z)/H0E(z)\equiv H(z)/H_{0}is the dimensionless Hubble
parameter. SinceH/h=100​E​(z)​km​s−1​Mpc−1H/h=100\,E(z)\;\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}Mpc^{-1}}is independent ofhh, the line-of-sight dilation depends only onE​(z)E(z). Similarly, the comoving angular diameter distance inh−1​Mpch^{-1}\mathrm{Mpc}isDM[h]≡h​DM=(c/100)​∫0zdz′/E​(z′)D_{M}^{[h]}\equiv h\,D_{M}=(c/100)\int_{0}^{z}\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}/E(z^{\prime}),
which is alsohh-independent. The AP dilation parameters are
therefore defined asq∥=Etrue​(z)Efid​(z),q⟂=DMfid,[h]​(z)DMtrue,[h]​(z),\boxed{\;q_{\parallel}=\frac{E^{\rm true}(z)}{E^{\rm fid}(z)}\,,\qquad q_{\perp}=\frac{D_{M}^{{\rm fid},\,[h]}(z)}{D_{M}^{{\rm true},\,[h]}(z)}\;}\,,(252)

with the AP ratioqAP≡q∥q⟂=DMfid,[h]​(z)​Etrue​(z)DMtrue,[h]​(z)​Efid​(z),q_{\rm AP}\equiv\frac{q_{\parallel}}{q_{\perp}}=\frac{D_{M}^{{\rm fid},\,[h]}(z)\;E^{\rm true}(z)}{D_{M}^{{\rm true},\,[h]}(z)\;E^{\rm fid}(z)}\,,(253)

and the isotropic dilationqiso=(q⟂2​q∥)1/3q_{\rm iso}=(q_{\perp}^{2}\,q_{\parallel})^{1/3}.
Since bothE​(z)E(z)andDM[h]D_{M}^{[h]}arehh-independent, the
parametersq∥q_{\parallel},q⟂q_{\perp}, andqAPq_{\rm AP}depend only on
background quantities such asΩm\Omega_{m},ΩΛ\Omega_{\Lambda}, the dark energy equation of state.

## Remapping of wavevectors.

Since Fourier modes are conjugate to position, wavevectors
(inh​h/Mpch\,h/\mathrm{Mpc}) transform inversely to coordinates
(inh−1​Mpch^{-1}\mathrm{Mpc}):k∥true=q∥​k∥fid,k⟂true=q⟂​k⟂fid,k_{\parallel}^{\rm true}=q_{\parallel}\,k_{\parallel}^{\rm fid}\,,\qquad k_{\perp}^{\rm true}=q_{\perp}\,k_{\perp}^{\rm fid}\,,(254)

wherekfidk^{\rm fid}is inhfid/Mpch_{\rm fid}/\text{Mpc}andktruek^{\rm true}is
inhtrue/Mpch_{\rm true}/\text{Mpc}.
Writingk∥=k​μk_{\parallel}=k\muandk⟂=k​1−μ2k_{\perp}=k\sqrt{1-\mu^{2}}(withμ≡𝒌^⋅𝒏^\mu\equiv\hat{\bm{k}}\cdot\hat{{\bm{n}}}the cosine to the line of
sight), the true wavenumber and angle expressed in terms of fiducial
quantities arektrue=kfid​q⟂​F​(μfid),μtrue=qAP​μfidF​(μfid),k^{\rm true}=k^{\rm fid}\,q_{\perp}\,F(\mu^{\rm fid})\,,\quad\quad\quad\mu^{\rm true}=\frac{q_{\rm AP}\,\mu^{\rm fid}}{F(\mu^{\rm fid})}\,,(255)

whereF​(μ)≡1+(qAP2−1)​μ2.F(\mu)\equiv\sqrt{1+(q_{\rm AP}^{2}-1)\,\mu^{2}}\,.(256)

ForqAP=1q_{\rm AP}=1,F=1F=1andμtrue=μfid\mu^{\rm true}=\mu^{\rm fid}; the remapping reduces toktrue=q⟂​kfidk^{\rm true}=q_{\perp}\,k^{\rm fid}.

## Effect on the power spectrum.

The overdensityδ​(𝒙)\delta({\bm{x}})is a scalar: it does not change under
the coordinate remapping, only the volume element does. The
Fourier-space volume transforms asd3​kfid=(q∥​q⟂2)−1​d3​ktrue\mathrm{d}^{3}k^{\rm fid}=(q_{\parallel}\,q_{\perp}^{2})^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}^{3}k^{\rm true},
and the Dirac delta function picks up the inverse Jacobian.
The operational formula used in data analyses is thusPobs​(k,μ)=1q⟂2​q∥​Ptrue​(k​q⟂​F​(μ),qAP​μ/F​(μ)),\boxed{\;P^{\rm obs}(k,\mu)=\frac{1}{q_{\perp}^{2}\,q_{\parallel}}\;P^{\rm true}\!\bigl(k\,q_{\perp}\,F(\mu),\;q_{\rm AP}\,\mu/F(\mu)\bigr)\;}\,,(257)

Note that the power spectra on the two sides carry differenthh-units:PobsP^{\rm obs}is in[Mpc/hfid]3[{\rm Mpc}/h_{\rm fid}]^{3}(matchingkkinhfid/Mpch_{\rm fid}/{\rm Mpc}), whilePtrueP^{\rm true}fromclassis in[Mpc/htrue]3[{\rm Mpc}/h_{\rm true}]^{3}.
This unit difference is accounted for by the Jacobian.
The three physical effects encoded in this formula are:
remapping ofkk(ktrue=q⟂​F​kk^{\rm true}=q_{\perp}F\,k),
remapping ofμ\mu(μtrue=qAP​μ/F\mu^{\rm true}=q_{\rm AP}\mu/F), and the
volume prefactor (1/q⟂2​q∥1/q_{\perp}^{2}q_{\parallel}).

## Combining with redshift-space distortions.

In redshift space the true power spectrum is already anisotropic.
In the Kaiser approximation,Ptrue​(k,μ)=b12​(1+β​μ2)2​Plin​(k),β≡f/b1,P^{\rm true}(k,\mu)=b_{1}^{2}\bigl(1+\beta\,\mu^{2}\bigr)^{2}\,P_{\rm lin}(k)\,,\qquad\beta\equiv f/b_{1}\,,(258)

wheref=d​ln⁡D+/d​ln⁡af=\mathrm{d}\ln D_{+}/\mathrm{d}\ln ais the logarithmic growth rate andb1b_{1}the linear bias (with thehh-rescaling absorbed).
Substituting Eq. (255) into Eq. (258)
and applying Eq. (257):Pobs​(k,μ)=b12q⟂2​q∥​(1+β​qAP2​μ2F2​(μ))2​Plin​(k​q⟂​F​(μ)).P^{\rm obs}(k,\mu)=\frac{b_{1}^{2}}{q_{\perp}^{2}\,q_{\parallel}}\;\biggl(1+\beta\,\frac{q_{\rm AP}^{2}\,\mu^{2}}{F^{2}(\mu)}\biggr)^{\!2}\;P_{\rm lin}\!\bigl(k\,q_{\perp}\,F(\mu)\bigr)\,.(259)

The observed multipoles are obtained by projecting onto Legendre
polynomials:Pℓobs​(k)=2​ℓ+12​∫−11dμ​Pobs​(k,μ)​ℒℓ​(μ).P_{\ell}^{\rm obs}(k)=\frac{2\ell+1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\;P^{\rm obs}(k,\mu)\;\mathcal{L}_{\ell}(\mu)\,.(260)

Are we making a mistake?Finally, let is comment on a confusing point about the AP distortions.
Often in the literature it is presented as if we are intentionally making
a mistake by assuming some wrong fiducial cosmology, which then need to be iterated. The correct interpretation
in the EFT-based full-shape analyses
is that the AP distortions is just a convenient way to
measure distances in galaxy clustering by normalizing them to some
fiducial baseline. Using a fiducial cosmology we do not make a mistakeper se. The distance dependence is always fully forward-modeled,
and even if the fiducial cosmology happened to be different from the one
inferred from the data, this is totally fine as long as the
AP distortions are modeled consistently (e.g. without additional approximations like a Taylor expansion ofHfid/HtrueH_{\rm fid}/H_{\rm true}around unity).

## 6.8Summary and Discussion

IR resummation.The last effect we need to include is a generalization of IR resummation in the case of RSD.
Unlike the bias case, the RSD mapping explicitly depends on the
velocity of the tracer w.r.t the observer, which is not a
frame-invariant quantity. As such, it is not protected by the equivalence
principle. However, the correlation functions are, so one can still
derive the generalizations of the IR cancellation arguments
in the RSD case. Just like in the real space case, the cancellation
does not happen for
modesq∼koscq\sim k_{\mathrm{osc}}, which require IR resummation.
This is done
by promoting the damping factorΣ2\Sigma^{2}in Eq. (112)
to be direction-dependent:Σtot2​(z,μ)=(1+f​(z)​μ2​(2+f​(z)))​D+2​(z)​Σ2+f2​(z)​μ2​(μ2−1)​D+2​(z)​δ​Σ2δ​Σ2≡12​π2​∫0kIR𝑑q​P11​(q)​j2​(qkosc).\begin{split}\Sigma^{2}_{\rm tot}(z,\mu)&=(1+f(z)\mu^{2}(2+f(z)))D^{2}_{+}(z)\Sigma^{2}+f^{2}(z)\mu^{2}(\mu^{2}-1)D^{2}_{+}(z)\delta\Sigma^{2}\\
\delta\Sigma^{2}&\equiv\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{k_{\mathrm{IR}}}dq\,P_{11}(q)j_{2}\left(\frac{q}{k_{\mathrm{osc}}}\right)\,.\end{split}(261)

The direction dependence of the damping factor is a serious
practical issue, which can be resolved either by using further
approximations[32]or going into Lagrangian space[38].
The latter also provides some insight into the physical
origin of the damping factor, which will be discussed in the
Chapter 7.

The scales of RSD.The scaleskRSD,stochk_{\mathrm{RSD,stoch}}(and the analogouskRSD,detk_{\mathrm{RSD,det}}controlling the size ofC~1\tilde{C}_{1},C~2\tilde{C}_{2}) may beparametrically suppressedwith respect
tokNLk_{\mathrm{NL}}for some galaxy samples:kRSD≪kNL,k_{\mathrm{RSD}}\ll k_{\mathrm{NL}}\,,(262)

because of the small-scale velocity dispersion of virialized
objects, and the fact that the velocity field is, in general,
more non-linear than the density field. This is the EFT
manifestation of theFingers of God: their leading
deterministic effect is captured by thek2​μ2​P11k^{2}\mu^{2}P_{11}andk2​μ4​P11k^{2}\mu^{4}P_{11}counterterms, whilst the stochastic
effect
is captured by theμ2​k2\mu^{2}k^{2}stochastic term.
The corresponding coefficients may be
enhanced by(kNL/kRSD)2(k_{\mathrm{NL}}/k_{\mathrm{RSD}})^{2}, and
the derivative expansion in redshift space typically breaks down atk∼kRSDk\sim k_{\mathrm{RSD}}rather thankNLk_{\mathrm{NL}}[99,100].

If fingers-of-God are present,
the breakdown of EFT is manifest in large values
of the inferred countertermsC~1\tilde{C}_{1}andC~2\tilde{C}_{2}if one is using the normalizationkRSD=kNLk_{\mathrm{RSD}}=k_{\mathrm{NL}}.
For instance, luminous red galaxies havekRSD≃0.25​h​Mpc−1k_{\mathrm{RSD}}\simeq 0.25~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1},
implying that
atkmax=0.2​h​Mpc−1k_{\rm max}=0.2~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}the next-to-leading order counterterms∼k4​P11\sim k^{4}P_{11}are not negligible. A good policy is to include some of these counterterms
in the theory model even though formally they are higher order just because
their contribution is not negligible in power counting.
This practice is common in particle physics, see e.g.[101,102].
In this vein, Ref.[91]suggested to include the following higher-derivative contributionPRSD,∇z^4δ=c~​k4​μ4​f4​P11​(k).P_{\rm RSD,~\nabla^{4}_{\hat{z}}\delta}=\tilde{c}k^{4}\mu^{4}f^{4}P_{11}(k)\,.(263)

Once the cosmological constraints are marginalized over such extra counterterms,
the are more robust and conservative w.r.t. small-scale non-linearity. For theΛ\LambdaCDM power
spectrum it happened that∼k4​P11∼k2\sim k^{4}P_{11}\sim k^{2}atkmax≃0.2​h​Mpc−1k_{\rm max}\simeq 0.2~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1},
so that thek2​μ2k^{2}\mu^{2}stochastic contributions are degenerate
with the higher derivative corrections and can
effectively absorb them in the fit[103,58,76]. However,
the fit then returns incorrect values ofα~2\tilde{\alpha}_{2}, which are
inconsistent with precision measurements of these parameters from simulations
(discussed shortly).
In addition, one can propose a data-driven argument: as long
as the higher-order countertermc~\tilde{c}can be detected in data,
or if it modifies cosmological parameter inference in a significant way,
it should be included in the prediction. This is indeed the case
for luminous red galaxies of BOSS and DESI surveys[91,104,58,76].

PT Challenge simulation has a gigantic
volume of 566 [h−1h^{-1}Gpc]3, which is why the error bars
are invisible in the plot. Since the two-loop corrections are
greater than the error-bars in this case, the fit is performed at
more conservative scale cuts than the actual DESI data.

Comparison to simulations and data.The EFT prediction for galaxies in redshift space
matches both simulated and
observed data well up tokmax≃0.2​h​Mpc−1k_{\rm max}\simeq 0.2~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1},
see Fig.17.
Perfect recovery of cosmological parameters in various
masked data challenges such as[81,106]demonstrated
that this computation is not in the over-fitting regime.

An even stronger test of EFT is a comparison to simulations
at the field level[107,108,77,57,58,109,110].
To that end one applies the EFT prediction to the known initial
condition of the simulationδ1​(𝒌)\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})and use this prediction
as aforward modelto be compared to the actual simulated map.
The point it that the power spectrum is a relatively smooth function
ofkkthat can be overfit easily. In contrast, the field level comparison
requires matching not only the amplitudes of the modes, but also their phases,
i.e. we need to fit each pixel of the simulated galaxy map.
This comparison is nicely illustrated by the following
example. A forward modelδg​model(s)=Pg​g,N​L​(k)P11​(k)​δ1​(𝒌)\delta^{(s)}_{g~\rm model}=\sqrt{\frac{P_{gg,NL}(k)}{P_{11}(k)}}\delta_{1}({\bm{k}})(wherePg​g,N​LP_{gg,NL}is the non-linear galaxy power spectrum
from the simulation) by construction leads to a perfect match
to the galaxy power spectrum, but it fails completely at the field level because
it misses the phase contributions from higher order operators[111,56,107,108].
Thus, the field-level test allows one to address a key concern of the EFT
skeptics: is the success of EFT entirely due to the free parameters?
The answer is obviously “no.”
Even if one has infinitely many free parameters, one can’t fit every pixel in a
galaxy map without the correct bias model, i.e. operators and the displacement structure predicted by EFT.

The field level comparison allows one
to determine the stochastic power spectrum, see Fig.19,
which shows its measurement for DESI-like luminous red galaxies (LRG)
in bothMTNGhydrodynamical simulation
andAbacusN-body simulation
from[58,110](see[112,113]).Pstoch​n¯P_{\rm stoch}\bar{n}is white (orientation and scale-independent)
on large scales. The onset of scale andμ\mu-dependence in redshift
space is accurately captured by the EFT prediction (246).
The productPstoch​n¯∼α~0P_{\rm stoch}\bar{n}\sim\tilde{\alpha}_{0}is different from one on large scales due to the halo exclusion effect mentioned in the previous chapter.
The correct predictions for the stochastic power spectrum
is another important consistency test of EFT. In addition, let us
mention thatα~2>0\tilde{\alpha}_{2}>0for DESI-like LRGs, as clearly
seen in Fig.19. To recover this value from the
direct power spectrum fits it is crucial to include
the higher-order countertermPRSD,∇z^4δP_{\rm RSD,~\nabla^{4}_{\hat{z}}\delta},
in the theory prediction.

Note that just like in the case of the matter fields,PstochP_{\rm stoch}becomes shallower aroundk∼0.25​h​Mpc−1k\sim 0.25~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1},
which signals the breakdown of the
EFT expansion forPstochP_{\rm stoch}around this scale (for this galaxy sample).
In principle, EFT is really useful only for the deterministic
part of the power spectrum, whilst for the stochastic part it can’t offer anything other than the Taylor series ink2k^{2}. This implies that
once the stochastic power spectrum becomes non-perturbative,
no higher EFT order corrections can possibly improve the modeling
of the galaxy power spectrum in redshift space.121212This is particularly important now as some new “resummation”
techniques resurfaced in the literature.
In contrast to IR resummation, which resums
large-scale modes that are fully described by the EFT,
these new “resummation” approaches try to resumm the UV contributions,
paralleling the logic of the unsuccessful program to resum
SPT loop diagrams.
There are two problems with such “resummations” in general:
(a) we don’t have a parametric control over the UV contributions,
i.e. the “resummed” terms are of the same order as the unresummed ones;
and (b) we don’t know the UV theory. Even if these “resummation”
formulas can formally fit the galaxy power spectrum data atk∼0.3​h​Mpc−1k\sim 0.3~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1},
the field level EFT analyses imply that this is achieved only due
to a massive over-fitting.
No resummation can fix the non-trivial scaling ofPstochP_{\rm stoch}which all simulations consistently predict
atk∼0.3​h​Mpc−1k\sim 0.3~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}.
At the same time, simulations suggest thatPstochP_{\rm stoch}can be a factor of ten greater than the deterministic part
ofPggP_{\rm gg}on these scale, rendering any UV resummation
completely useless even if they were correct, see the lower panel of Fig.19.Figure 18:The galaxy density field of DESI-like luminous red galaxies
from theMTNGsimulation (left panel), the EFT forward model (middle panel), and the residuals between them (right panel).
Redshift-space distortions are implemented along thezzaxis.Figure 19:The stochastic power spectraPstochP_{\rm stoch}of DESI-like
Luminous Red Galaxies (LRG) from field-level EFT
fits toMTNGhydrodynamical simulation (upper left panel)
andAbacushalo occupation distribution (HOD) models (upper right panel).n¯\bar{n}is the number density of the simulated galaxies.Pstoch​n¯P_{\rm stoch}\bar{n}is different from unity on large scales due to halo exclusion effects. It is scale and orientation-independent (white) in thek→0k\to 0limit in agreement with EFT. The leading corrections beyond the white noise behavior match the EFT predictionα1​k2+α2​μ2​k2\alpha_{1}k^{2}+\alpha_{2}\mu^{2}k^{2}.PstochP_{\rm stoch}becomes shallower aroundk∼0.25​h​Mpc−1k\sim 0.25~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}, which sets the limit of the EFT reach in redshift space for these galaxy samples. No resummation of deterministic contributions
can improve the modeling of the stochastic power spectrum,
whose amplitude is comparable or exceeding the deterministic one atk∼0.3​h​Mpc−1k\sim 0.3~\,h\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}, see thelower panels.

All in all, while the regime of validity of EFT is somewhat
limited in redshift space, it is nevertheless a powerful tool
that can be used for precision measurements of cosmological
parameters in the quasi-linear regime. At the same time, simulations suggest
that small galaxies hosted by light halos, which are typically
identified by their strong emission lines,
have a higher cutoff
than
more massive galaxies[100,110,114].
In addition, Fingers-of-God can be suppressed
by a careful data-driven selection[115].
Therefore,
the perturbativity of the sample (i.e. the EFT cutoff),
is a highly sample- and selection-dependent property.
Finally, simulations suggest
that high-redshift data may be more
perturbative than the current samples[114],
suggesting a bright future for the EFT!

## 7Chapter 7: Lagrangian Perturbation Theory

In Chapter 3 we saw that the IR-enhanced effects of large-scale
flows on the BAO must be resummed, and we listed Lagrangian
perturbation theory (LPT) as one of the two ways to do it: a
formulation in which the displacement is never Taylor-expanded, so
that the boost symmetry and the associated IR structure are manifest
at every step[6,34,116,117,118,119,35,120,38,121]. In this chapter we develop LPT systematically: the
displacement field and the exact expression for the power spectrum,
the Zel’dovich approximation[122], the higher-order Lagrangian kernels
and their matching to SPT, and finally the position-space picture of
why bulk flows are harmless for the broadband power spectrum but
fatal for the perturbative treatment of the BAO peak.

A remark on notation: throughout this chapter𝒒{\bm{q}}denotes theLagrangian coordinate(the initial position of a fluid
element), not a loop momentum; momenta are denoted𝒌{\bm{k}},𝒑{\bm{p}},𝒍{\bm{l}}, and we abbreviate∫𝒑≡∫d3​p(2​π)3\int_{\bm{p}}\equiv\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}, as before.

## 7.1The Displacement Field

In the Lagrangian formulation of hydrodynamics one follows the
trajectories of the particles (fluid elements). A particle that
started at the initial position𝒒{\bm{q}}— equivalently,𝒒{\bm{q}}is
simply a label — is found at timeτ\tauat the comoving position𝒙​(𝒒,τ)=𝒒+𝚿​(𝒒,τ),{\bm{x}}({\bm{q}},\tau)={\bm{q}}+{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}},\tau)\,,(264)

which defines the fundamental dynamical variable of LPT, thedisplacement field𝚿​(𝒒,τ){\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}},\tau).

Let us derive its equation of motion directly from Newtonian
cosmology. A particle at physical position𝒓​(t){\bm{r}}(t)obeys Newton’s
law in the full gravitational potential,d2​𝒓d​t2=−∇𝒓Φ​(𝒓).\frac{d^{2}{\bm{r}}}{dt^{2}}=-\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{r}}}\Phi({\bm{r}})\,.(265)

We now repeat, for a single trajectory, the steps that led to the
fluid equations of Chapter 3: decouple the Hubble flow by writing𝒓=a​(τ)​𝒙{\bm{r}}=a(\tau)\,{\bm{x}}, and split the potential into background and
perturbation as in Eq. (54),Φ=−12​a¨​a​|𝒙|2+ϕ​(𝒙,τ)\Phi=-\frac{1}{2}\ddot{a}\,a\,|{\bm{x}}|^{2}+\phi({\bm{x}},\tau). Withd​t=a​d​τdt=a\,d\tau, the first time derivative of the trajectory is the
familiar Hubble-flow-plus-peculiar-velocity decomposition,𝒓˙=ℋ​𝒙+𝒙′\dot{\bm{r}}=\mathcal{H}\,{\bm{x}}+{\bm{x}}^{\prime}, and one more derivative gives the
left-hand side of Eq. (265),d2​𝒓d​t2=1a​dd​τ​(ℋ​𝒙+𝒙′)=1a​(𝒙′′+ℋ​𝒙′+ℋ′​𝒙).\frac{d^{2}{\bm{r}}}{dt^{2}}=\frac{1}{a}\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\mathcal{H}\,{\bm{x}}+{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\right)=\frac{1}{a}\left({\bm{x}}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}\,{\bm{x}}^{\prime}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\,{\bm{x}}\right)\,.(266)

For the right-hand side, use∇𝒓=a−1​∇𝒙\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{r}}}=a^{-1}\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{x}}}together witha¨​a=ℋ′\ddot{a}\,a=\mathcal{H}^{\prime}(which follows froma¨=1a​(a′/a)′\ddot{a}=\frac{1}{a}(a^{\prime}/a)^{\prime}), so that−∇𝒓Φ=1a​(ℋ′​𝒙−∇𝒙ϕ).-\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{r}}}\Phi=\frac{1}{a}\left(\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\,{\bm{x}}-\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{x}}}\phi\right)\,.(267)

The twoℋ′​𝒙\mathcal{H}^{\prime}{\bm{x}}terms cancelidentically— the
background potential is precisely what supports the pure Hubble flow𝒙=const{\bm{x}}=\mathrm{const}as a solution:131313Equivalently, on the matter-dominated background
one may substitute the Einstein–de Sitter value of the background
potential,Φ=14​ℋ2​|𝒙|2+ϕ\Phi=\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{H}^{2}|{\bm{x}}|^{2}+\phi. The equation
of motion then reads𝒙′′+ℋ​𝒙′+ℋ′​𝒙=−12​ℋ2​𝒙−∇𝒙ϕ{\bm{x}}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}{\bm{x}}^{\prime}+\mathcal{H}^{\prime}{\bm{x}}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}{\bm{x}}-\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{x}}}\phi, and the terms without time derivatives cancel by the
Friedmann equationℋ′=−12​ℋ2\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}of EdS.𝒙′′+ℋ​𝒙′=−∇𝒙ϕ​(𝒙).{\bm{x}}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}\,{\bm{x}}^{\prime}=-\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{x}}}\phi({\bm{x}})\,.(268)

Finally, evaluate this along the trajectory𝒙​(𝒒,τ)=𝒒+𝚿​(𝒒,τ){\bm{x}}({\bm{q}},\tau)={\bm{q}}+{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}},\tau): the label𝒒{\bm{q}}carries no time
dependence, so𝒙′=𝚿′{\bm{x}}^{\prime}={\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime}and𝒙′′=𝚿′′{\bm{x}}^{\prime\prime}={\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime\prime}, while the force is
evaluated at the displaced position,𝚿′′+ℋ𝚿′=−∇𝒙ϕ|𝒙=𝒒+𝚿,Δx​ϕ=32​ℋ2​Ωm​δ.\boxed{\;{\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}\,{\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime}=-\left.\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{x}}}\phi\right|_{{\bm{x}}={\bm{q}}+{\bm{\Psi}}}\,,}\qquad\Delta_{x}\phi=\frac{3}{2}\,\mathcal{H}^{2}\Omega_{m}\,\delta\,.(269)

Note that the force is evaluated at thedisplacedposition —
this is the only place where the non-linearity enters. Equation (269) is of course nothing
but the characteristic form of the Euler equation of Chapter 3 withτi​j=0\tau_{ij}=0: the peculiar velocity along the trajectory is𝒗=𝒙′=𝚿′{\bm{v}}={\bm{x}}^{\prime}={\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime}, and𝚿′′{\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime\prime}is its convective derivative(∂τ+𝒗⋅∇𝒙)​𝒗\left(\partial_{\tau}+{\bm{v}}\cdot\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{x}}}\right){\bm{v}}, since the partial
derivatives in Eq. (269) are taken at fixed𝒒{\bm{q}},
i.e. following the particle.

The density field is not an independent variable: it is fixed by
mass conservation. There are two ways to see this. First, counting particles:
the number of particles inside an Eulerian volume element centered
at𝒙{\bm{x}}is obtained by summing over all𝒒{\bm{q}}whose trajectories
end up there, so1+δ​(𝒙,τ)=∫d3​q​δD(3)​(𝒙−𝒒−𝚿​(𝒒,τ))=1det⁡(δi​j+∂Ψi∂qj)|𝒒:𝒙​(𝒒,τ)=𝒙.1+\delta({\bm{x}},\tau)=\int d^{3}q\;\delta_{D}^{(3)}\big({\bm{x}}-{\bm{q}}-{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}},\tau)\big)=\frac{1}{\det\left(\delta_{ij}+\dfrac{\partial\Psi^{i}}{\partial q^{j}}\right)}\Bigg|_{{\bm{q}}:\,{\bm{x}}({\bm{q}},\tau)={\bm{x}}}\,.(270)

Second, directly from mass conservation: the volume elements get
distorted but carry their mass with them,ρ¯​d3​q=ρ​(𝒙,τ)​d3​x\bar{\rho}\,d^{3}q=\rho({\bm{x}},\tau)\,d^{3}x, which gives the same Jacobian
formula. When the map𝒒→𝒙{\bm{q}}\to{\bm{x}}ceases to be single-valued
(which happens at shell crossing,det→0\det\to 0), the sum over streams in the first
expression must be kept — this is where the perturbative
description ultimately breaks down.

Homework:show that Eq. (270) solves the
continuity equation.

## 7.2The Exact Power Spectrum: Cumulant Expansion

The great advantage of the Lagrangian formulation is that the
density field can be written in closed form. Fourier transforming
Eq. (270) (from now on we suppress the time
argument and consider𝒌≠0{\bm{k}}\neq 0),δ​(𝒌)=∫d3​q​e−i​𝒌⋅𝒒​(e−i​𝒌⋅𝚿​(𝒒)−1):\delta({\bm{k}})=\int d^{3}q\;e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}\left(e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}})}-1\right)\,:(271)

the displacement enters only through the phasee−i​𝒌⋅𝚿e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}}— the same exponential structure that we
encountered in the toy model and in the boost-symmetry argument of
Chapter 3, except that here it is exact and fully non-linear. For
the two-point function, the terms involving a single expectation
value⟨e−i​𝒌⋅𝚿⟩\langle e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}}\rangleare proportional toδD(3)​(𝒌)\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}})and drop for𝒌≠0{\bm{k}}\neq 0; statistical
homogeneity then implies that the remaining average depends only on
the separation𝒒=𝒒1−𝒒2{\bm{q}}={\bm{q}}_{1}-{\bm{q}}_{2}, and we obtain theexactresultP(k)=∫d3qe−i​𝒌⋅𝒒(⟨e−i​𝒌⋅[𝚿​(𝒒)−𝚿​(0)]⟩−1).\boxed{\;P(k)=\int d^{3}q\;e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}\left(\langle e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot\left[{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}})-{\bm{\Psi}}(0)\right]}\rangle-1\right)\,.\;}(272)

Note that only therelativedisplacement of the two points
appears: a uniform shift of all particles cancels between the two
exponentials. This is the Lagrangian-space incarnation of the
equivalence-principle protection derived in Chapter 3, and it holds
here non-perturbatively.

To evaluate the average we use the cumulant expansion theorem,⟨e−i​X⟩=exp⁡(∑N=1∞(−i)NN!​⟨XN⟩c),\langle e^{-iX}\rangle=\exp\left(\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-i)^{N}}{N!}\,\langle X^{N}\rangle_{c}\right)\,,(273)

withX=𝒌⋅[𝚿​(𝒒)−𝚿​(0)]X={\bm{k}}\cdot[{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}})-{\bm{\Psi}}(0)]. Parity implies⟨𝚿⟩=0\langle{\bm{\Psi}}\rangle=0, so the expansion starts atN=2N=2. Separating the
zero-lag (one-point) cumulants from the𝒒{\bm{q}}-dependent ones with
the help of the binomial theorem, one can organize the result asP​(k)=∫d3​q​e−i​𝒌⋅𝒒​(e−2​∑n=1∞ki1​…​ki2​n(2​n)!​Ai1​…​i2​n(2​n)​e∑N=2∞ki1​…​kiNN!​Bi1​…​iN(N)​(𝒒)−1),P(k)=\int d^{3}q\;e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}\left(e^{-2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{k_{i_{1}}\dots k_{i_{2n}}}{(2n)!}\,A^{(2n)}_{i_{1}\dots i_{2n}}}\;e^{\sum_{N=2}^{\infty}\frac{k_{i_{1}}\dots k_{i_{N}}}{N!}\,B^{(N)}_{i_{1}\dots i_{N}}({\bm{q}})}-1\right)\,,(274)

withAi1​…​i2​n(2​n)=(−1)n−1​⟨Ψi1​(0)​⋯​Ψi2​n​(0)⟩c,Bi1​…​iN(N)​(𝒒)=iN​∑j=1N−1(Nj)​⟨Ψ(i1​(𝒒1)​⋯​Ψij​(𝒒1)​Ψij+1​(𝒒2)​⋯​ΨiN)​(𝒒2)⟩c,\begin{split}A^{(2n)}_{i_{1}\dots i_{2n}}&=(-1)^{n-1}\,\langle\Psi_{i_{1}}(0)\cdots\Psi_{i_{2n}}(0)\rangle_{c}\,,\\
B^{(N)}_{i_{1}\dots i_{N}}({\bm{q}})&=i^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}\binom{N}{j}\,\langle\Psi_{(i_{1}}({\bm{q}}_{1})\cdots\Psi_{i_{j}}({\bm{q}}_{1})\,\Psi_{i_{j+1}}({\bm{q}}_{2})\cdots\Psi_{i_{N})}({\bm{q}}_{2})\rangle_{c}\,,\end{split}(275)

where the indices are symmetrized and𝒒=𝒒1−𝒒2{\bm{q}}={\bm{q}}_{1}-{\bm{q}}_{2}. TheAA’s encode the (large) one-point displacement variances, theBB’s
the correlations between the two points. Equation (274) is
still exact — all of Eulerian perturbation theory is contained in
the low-order cumulants of𝚿{\bm{\Psi}}.

## 7.3The Zel’dovich Approximation

The Zel’dovich approximation (ZA) is thelinearsolution for
the displacement — which, via the non-linear
map (270), still generates a fully non-linear
density field. Linearizing Eq. (269) (so that∇𝒙≈∇q\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{x}}}\approx\bm{\nabla}_{q}) and taking the divergence, the variableψ≡∇q⋅𝚿\psi\equiv\bm{\nabla}_{q}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}obeysψ′′+ℋ​ψ′−32​ℋ2​Ωm​ψ=0,\psi^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}\,\psi^{\prime}-\frac{3}{2}\,\mathcal{H}^{2}\Omega_{m}\,\psi=0\,,(276)

i.e. exactly the linear growth equation (withδ1=−ψ\delta_{1}=-\psi, as
follows from expanding the determinant in
Eq. (270)). Selecting the growing mode and fixing
the initial conditions byδ0=−∇q⋅𝚿0\delta_{0}=-\bm{\nabla}_{q}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}_{0}, we find
in Fourier space𝚿ZA​(𝒌,τ)=i​𝒌k2​δ1​(𝒌,τ),{\bm{\Psi}}_{\mathrm{ZA}}({\bm{k}},\tau)=\frac{i{\bm{k}}}{k^{2}}\,\delta_{1}({\bm{k}},\tau)\,,(277)

which is precisely the linear displacement (38)
introduced in Chapter 2. Since𝚿ZA{\bm{\Psi}}_{\mathrm{ZA}}is linear in the
Gaussian fieldδ0\delta_{0}, all its cumulants beyond the second
vanish, and the second one is⟨ΨZAi​(𝒒1)​ΨZAj​(𝒒2)⟩=∫𝒍li​ljl4​ei​𝒍⋅𝒒​P11​(l),𝒒=𝒒1−𝒒2.\langle\Psi^{i}_{\mathrm{ZA}}({\bm{q}}_{1})\,\Psi^{j}_{\mathrm{ZA}}({\bm{q}}_{2})\rangle=\int_{{\bm{l}}}\;\frac{l_{i}l_{j}}{l^{4}}\;e^{i{\bm{l}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}\,P_{11}(l)\,,\qquad{\bm{q}}={\bm{q}}_{1}-{\bm{q}}_{2}\,.(278)

The average in Eq. (272) is then a Gaussian
integral,⟨e−i​𝒌⋅[𝚿​(𝒒)−𝚿​(0)]⟩=exp⁡{−ki​kj2​⟨[Ψi​(𝒒)−Ψi​(0)]​[Ψj​(𝒒)−Ψj​(0)]⟩},\langle e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot[{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}})-{\bm{\Psi}}(0)]}\rangle=\exp\left\{-\frac{k_{i}k_{j}}{2}\langle\left[\Psi^{i}({\bm{q}})-\Psi^{i}(0)\right]\left[\Psi^{j}({\bm{q}})-\Psi^{j}(0)\right]\rangle\right\}\,,(279)

and using Eq. (278) (the term odd in𝒍{\bm{l}}integrates to zero by isotropy) we obtain theZel’dovich power spectrumPZA(k)=∫d3qe−i​𝒌⋅𝒒(exp{−2kikj∫𝒍li​ljl4P11(l)sin2(𝒍⋅𝒒2)}−1).\boxed{\;P_{\mathrm{ZA}}(k)=\int d^{3}q\;e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}\left(\exp\left\{-2\,k_{i}k_{j}\int_{{\bm{l}}}\frac{l_{i}l_{j}}{l^{4}}\,P_{11}(l)\,\sin^{2}\!\left(\frac{{\bm{l}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}{2}\right)\right\}-1\right)\,.\;}(280)

Two features are worth noting. First, the combinationsin2⁡(𝒍⋅𝒒/2)=12​[1−cos⁡(𝒍⋅𝒒)]\sin^{2}({\bm{l}}\cdot{\bm{q}}/2)=\tfrac{1}{2}[1-\cos({\bm{l}}\cdot{\bm{q}})]vanishes asl2​q2l^{2}q^{2}forl​q→0lq\to 0: very long modes drop out of the
relative displacement — the same suppression that appeared in the
bracket of the BAO damping factor (112). Second, the
exponent is kept unexpanded: the ZA resumsallpowers of the
displacement, which is exactly what the IR resummation of Chapter 3
requires (indeed, evaluating Eq. (280) for the wiggly part
of the spectrum reproduces the dampinge−k2​Σ2e^{-k^{2}\Sigma^{2}}).

## 7.4Higher Orders: theLnL_{n}Kernels

To go beyond the ZA systematically, we need the equations of motion
in a form suitable for iteration. For𝒌≠0{\bm{k}}\neq 0the density and
the potential read, from Eq. (270),δ​(𝒌)=∫d3​q​e−i​𝒌⋅𝒙​(𝒒),ϕ​(𝒌)=−32​ℋ2​Ωmk2​∫d3​q​e−i​𝒌⋅𝒙​(𝒒),\delta({\bm{k}})=\int d^{3}q\;e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}({\bm{q}})}\,,\qquad\phi({\bm{k}})=-\frac{3}{2}\,\frac{\mathcal{H}^{2}\Omega_{m}}{k^{2}}\int d^{3}q\;e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}({\bm{q}})}\,,(281)

so the equation of motion (269) becomes𝚿′′​(𝒒)+ℋ​𝚿′​(𝒒)=32​ℋ2​Ωm​∫𝒌i​𝒌k2​∫d3​q′​ei​𝒌⋅(𝒙​(𝒒)−𝒙​(𝒒′)).{\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime\prime}({\bm{q}})+\mathcal{H}\,{\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime}({\bm{q}})=\frac{3}{2}\,\mathcal{H}^{2}\Omega_{m}\int_{\bm{k}}\frac{i{\bm{k}}}{k^{2}}\int d^{3}q^{\prime}\;e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot\left({\bm{x}}({\bm{q}})-{\bm{x}}({\bm{q}}^{\prime})\right)}\,.(282)

We now Fourier transform with respect to theLagrangiancoordinate,𝚿​(𝒒)=∫𝒑ei​𝒑⋅𝒒​𝚿𝒑{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}})=\int_{\bm{p}}e^{i{\bm{p}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}\,{\bm{\Psi}}_{\bm{p}}, and
pass to the time variableη=ln⁡D+\eta=\ln D_{+}in the EdS approximationΩm=f2\Omega_{m}=f^{2}(in exact EdS this is the same
asη=ln⁡a\eta=\ln a):∂η2𝚿𝒑+12​∂η𝚿𝒑=32​∫d3​q​d3​q′​e−i​𝒑⋅𝒒​∫𝒌i​𝒌k2​ei​𝒌⋅(𝒒−𝒒′)​exp⁡{i​kj​∫𝒑′Ψ𝒑′j​(ei​𝒑′⋅𝒒−ei​𝒑′⋅𝒒′)}.\partial^{2}_{\eta}{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{\bm{p}}+\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{\eta}{{\bm{\Psi}}}_{\bm{p}}=\frac{3}{2}\int d^{3}q\,d^{3}q^{\prime}\;e^{-i{\bm{p}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}\int_{\bm{k}}\frac{i{\bm{k}}}{k^{2}}\;e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot({\bm{q}}-{\bm{q}}^{\prime})}\;\exp\left\{ik_{j}\int_{{\bm{p}}^{\prime}}\Psi^{j}_{{\bm{p}}^{\prime}}\left(e^{i{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\cdot{\bm{q}}}-e^{i{\bm{p}}^{\prime}\cdot{\bm{q}}^{\prime}}\right)\right\}\,.(283)

which defines theLagrangian kernelsLnL_{n}— the analogs
