## 7.5Matching to SPT and the Curl Displacement

Expanding the exponential in Eq. (271) in
powers of𝒌⋅𝚿{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}and inserting the perturbative
displacement connects the two expansions. It is convenient to split
the displacement into its longitudinal and transverse (curl) parts,𝚿​(𝒒)=∫𝒑ei​𝒑⋅𝒒​(i​𝒑​S𝒑L+i​𝒑×𝑺𝒑T),{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}})=\int_{\bm{p}}e^{i{\bm{p}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}\left(i{\bm{p}}\,S^{L}_{\bm{p}}+i{\bm{p}}\times{\bm{S}}^{T}_{\bm{p}}\right)\,,(288)

with kernelsLnL_{n}and𝑻n{\bm{T}}_{n}defined as in
Eq. (284).

Zel’dovich kernels.Keeping the full series in𝒌⋅𝚿{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}but only the linear solution𝚿ZA{\bm{\Psi}}_{\mathrm{ZA}}, one findsδ𝒌ZA=∑n=1∞D+n​∫𝒑1⋯​∫𝒑n(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌−𝒑1​…​n)​1n!​(𝒌⋅𝒑1)p12​⋯​(𝒌⋅𝒑n)pn2​δ0​(𝒑1)​⋯​δ0​(𝒑n),\delta^{\mathrm{ZA}}_{\bm{k}}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}D_{+}^{n}\int_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\!\!\cdots\!\int_{{\bm{p}}_{n}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{p}}_{1\dots n})\;\frac{1}{n!}\,\frac{({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{p}}_{1})}{p_{1}^{2}}\cdots\frac{({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{p}}_{n})}{p_{n}^{2}}\;\delta_{0}({\bm{p}}_{1})\cdots\delta_{0}({\bm{p}}_{n})\,,(289)

i.e. the SPT kernels of the Zel’dovich approximation are simplyFnZA​(𝒑1,…,𝒑n)=1n!​(𝒌⋅𝒑1)p12​⋯​(𝒌⋅𝒑n)pn2,𝒌=𝒑1​…​n.F_{n}^{\mathrm{ZA}}({\bm{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{n})=\frac{1}{n!}\,\frac{({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{p}}_{1})}{p_{1}^{2}}\cdots\frac{({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{p}}_{n})}{p_{n}^{2}}\,,\qquad{\bm{k}}={\bm{p}}_{1\dots n}\,.(290)

Full kernels.In general, each way of distributing thennlinear fields among the factors of(−i​𝒌⋅𝚿(m))(-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}^{(m)})contributes, and one finds order by order (all expressions
symmetrized over the momenta):F2\displaystyle F_{2}=F2ZA+12!​𝒌⋅𝑳2​(𝒑1,𝒑2),\displaystyle=F_{2}^{\mathrm{ZA}}+\frac{1}{2!}\,{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{2}({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2})\,,(291)F3\displaystyle F_{3}=F3ZA+13!​𝒌⋅𝑳3+[(𝒌⋅𝒑1)p12​12!​𝒌⋅𝑳2​(𝒑2,𝒑3)]symm,\displaystyle=F_{3}^{\mathrm{ZA}}+\frac{1}{3!}\,{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{3}+\left[\frac{({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{p}}_{1})}{p_{1}^{2}}\,\frac{1}{2!}\,{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{2}({\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{p}}_{3})\right]_{\mathrm{symm}}\,,(292)

and at fourth orderF4=F4ZA+14!​𝒌⋅𝑳4+12​[𝒌⋅𝑳2​(𝒑1,𝒑2)2!​𝒌⋅𝑳2​(𝒑3,𝒑4)2!]symm+[(𝒌⋅𝒑1)p12​𝒌⋅𝑳3​(𝒑2,𝒑3,𝒑4)3!]symm+[(𝒌⋅𝒑1)p12​𝒌⋅(𝒑234×𝑻3​(𝒑2,𝒑3,𝒑4))3!]symm+12​[(𝒌⋅𝒑1)p12​(𝒌⋅𝒑2)p22​𝒌⋅𝑳2​(𝒑3,𝒑4)2!]symm.\begin{split}F_{4}=F_{4}^{\mathrm{ZA}}&+\frac{1}{4!}\,{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{4}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{2}({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2})}{2!}\,\frac{{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{2}({\bm{p}}_{3},{\bm{p}}_{4})}{2!}\right]_{\mathrm{symm}}+\left[\frac{({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{p}}_{1})}{p_{1}^{2}}\,\frac{{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{3}({\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{p}}_{3},{\bm{p}}_{4})}{3!}\right]_{\mathrm{symm}}\\
&+\left[\frac{({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{p}}_{1})}{p_{1}^{2}}\,\frac{{\bm{k}}\cdot\left({\bm{p}}_{234}\times{\bm{T}}_{3}({\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{p}}_{3},{\bm{p}}_{4})\right)}{3!}\right]_{\mathrm{symm}}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{p}}_{1})}{p_{1}^{2}}\,\frac{({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{p}}_{2})}{p_{2}^{2}}\,\frac{{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{2}({\bm{p}}_{3},{\bm{p}}_{4})}{2!}\right]_{\mathrm{symm}}\,.\end{split}(293)

As a consistency check, insertingL2L_{2}from Eq. (287) into
Eq. (291) givesF2=F2ZA+314​(1−μ122)F_{2}=F_{2}^{\mathrm{ZA}}+\frac{3}{14}(1-\mu_{12}^{2}), which
reproduces exactly the kernel (71).

Note the pattern of the transverse contributions: in the term linear
in𝚿(n){\bm{\Psi}}^{(n)}, the curl part drops out identically, because there
the Fourier argument of the displacement is thetotalmomentum,𝒌⋅(𝒌×𝑻n)=0{\bm{k}}\cdot({\bm{k}}\times{\bm{T}}_{n})=0. The curl displacement
first appears inF4F_{4}, through the cross term with𝚿(1){\bm{\Psi}}^{(1)}above, where it is evaluated at the partial momentum𝒑234≠𝒌{\bm{p}}_{234}\neq{\bm{k}}.

The curl part and the Cauchy invariant.The transverse
kernels are fixed by a beautiful exact statement. Contract the
equation of motion (269) with the Jacobian matrix∂xi/∂qj\partial x^{i}/\partial q^{j}and use∂xi∂qj​x′′⁣i=dd​τ​(∂xi∂qj​x′⁣i)−∂x′⁣i∂qj​x′⁣i\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial q^{j}}\,x^{\prime\prime\,i}=\frac{d}{d\tau}\!\left(\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial q^{j}}\,x^{\prime\,i}\right)-\frac{\partial x^{\prime\,i}}{\partial q^{j}}\,x^{\prime\,i}(primes denoted/d​τd/d\tauat fixed𝒒{\bm{q}}, as everywhere in these
notes) to bring it to the form(dd​τ+ℋ)​∂xi∂qj​x′⁣i=∂qj(|𝒙′|22−ϕ).\left(\frac{d}{d\tau}+\mathcal{H}\right)\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial q^{j}}\,x^{\prime\,i}=\partial_{q^{j}}\!\left(\frac{|{\bm{x}}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}-\phi\right)\,.(294)

Taking the Lagrangian curl kills the right-hand side:(dd​τ+ℋ)​ϵl​m​j​∂xi∂qj​∂x′⁣i∂qm=0,\left(\frac{d}{d\tau}+\mathcal{H}\right)\epsilon_{lmj}\,\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial q^{j}}\,\frac{\partial x^{\prime\,i}}{\partial q^{m}}=0\,,(295)

so the quantity in brackets (the cosmological version of theCauchy invariant) decays, and since it vanishes in the
initial conditions it vanishes at all times:ϵl​m​j​∂xi∂qj​∂x′⁣i∂qm=0.\epsilon_{lmj}\,\frac{\partial x^{i}}{\partial q^{j}}\,\frac{\partial x^{\prime\,i}}{\partial q^{m}}=0\,.(296)

This is the Lagrangian-space counterpart of the statement that no
Eulerian vorticity is generated in the single-stream regime —
note, however, that it doesnotmean that𝚿{\bm{\Psi}}is
curl-free: expanding𝒙=𝒒+𝚿{\bm{x}}={\bm{q}}+{\bm{\Psi}}in Eq. (296),ϵl​m​j​∂qmΨ′⁣j=−ϵl​m​j​∂qjΨi​∂qmΨ′⁣i,\epsilon_{lmj}\,\partial_{q_{m}}\Psi^{\prime\,j}=-\,\epsilon_{lmj}\,\partial_{q_{j}}\Psi^{i}\,\partial_{q_{m}}\Psi^{\prime\,i}\,,(297)

the curl of𝚿{\bm{\Psi}}is sourced at second order by the longitudinal
part. Inserting the EdS expansion𝚿=∑n𝚿n​(𝒒)​D+n{\bm{\Psi}}=\sum_{n}{\bm{\Psi}}_{n}({\bm{q}})\,D_{+}^{n}and symmetrizing the sum
(each term carries exactly one time derivative, so the overall
factor ofD+′D_{+}^{\prime}drops out and the resulting relation holds in any
time variable), one findsϵl​m​j​∂qmΨj(n)=12​n​∑l=1n−1(n−2​l)​ϵl​j​m​∂qjΨi(l)​∂qmΨi(n−l),\epsilon_{lmj}\,\partial_{q_{m}}\Psi^{(n)}_{j}=\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{l=1}^{n-1}(n-2l)\,\epsilon_{ljm}\,\partial_{q_{j}}\Psi^{(l)}_{i}\,\partial_{q_{m}}\Psi^{(n-l)}_{i}\,,(298)

which vanishes forn=2n=2(the two terms cancel): the first
non-trivial curl appears atthirdorder. In Fourier space,
using the decomposition (288),𝑻3​(𝒑)​p2=13​[(𝒑1×(𝒑2+𝒑3))​(𝒑1⋅(𝒑2+𝒑3))​L1​(𝒑1)​L2​(𝒑2,𝒑3)]symm,{\bm{T}}_{3}({\bm{p}})\,p^{2}=\frac{1}{3}\Big[\left({\bm{p}}_{1}\times({\bm{p}}_{2}+{\bm{p}}_{3})\right)\left({\bm{p}}_{1}\cdot({\bm{p}}_{2}+{\bm{p}}_{3})\right)L_{1}({\bm{p}}_{1})\,L_{2}({\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{p}}_{3})\Big]_{\mathrm{symm}}\,,(299)

whereL1L_{1},L2L_{2}denote the scalar parts of the kernels above.
This is the object that enters theF4F_{4}relation (293) — i.e. the transverse displacement
first affects the density power spectrum at two loops.

## 7.6Displacement Cumulants and Equivalence to SPT

To use the master formula (274) beyond the ZA we need the
low-order cumulants of the displacement. From the
expansion (284), the displacement power spectrum,⟨Ψ𝒑i​Ψ𝒑′j⟩=(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒑+𝒑′)​Ci​j​(𝒑)\langle\Psi^{i}_{{\bm{p}}}\Psi^{j}_{{\bm{p}}^{\prime}}\rangle=(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{p}}+{\bm{p}}^{\prime})\,C^{ij}({\bm{p}}),
receives through one-loop order the contributionsCi​j=C(11)i​j+C(22)i​j+C(13)i​j+C(31)i​jC^{ij}=C^{ij}_{(11)}+C^{ij}_{(22)}+C^{ij}_{(13)}+C^{ij}_{(31)},
withC(11)i​j​(𝒑)=pi​pjp4​P11​(p),C(22)i​j​(𝒑)=12​∫𝒑′L2i​(𝒑′,𝒑−𝒑′)​L2j​(𝒑′,𝒑−𝒑′)​P11​(p′)​P11​(|𝒑−𝒑′|),C(13)i​j​(𝒑)=C(31)j​i​(𝒑)=−12​L1i​(𝒑)​P11​(p)​∫𝒑′L3j​(−𝒑,𝒑′,−𝒑′)​P11​(p′),\begin{split}C^{ij}_{(11)}({\bm{p}})&=\frac{p^{i}p^{j}}{p^{4}}\,P_{11}(p)\,,\qquad C^{ij}_{(22)}({\bm{p}})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\bm{p}}^{\prime}}L^{i}_{2}({\bm{p}}^{\prime},{\bm{p}}-{\bm{p}}^{\prime})\,L^{j}_{2}({\bm{p}}^{\prime},{\bm{p}}-{\bm{p}}^{\prime})\,P_{11}(p^{\prime})\,P_{11}(|{\bm{p}}-{\bm{p}}^{\prime}|)\,,\\
C^{ij}_{(13)}({\bm{p}})&=C^{ji}_{(31)}({\bm{p}})=-\frac{1}{2}\,L^{i}_{1}({\bm{p}})\,P_{11}(p)\int_{{\bm{p}}^{\prime}}L^{j}_{3}(-{\bm{p}},{\bm{p}}^{\prime},-{\bm{p}}^{\prime})\,P_{11}(p^{\prime})\,,\end{split}(300)

while the leading (tree-level) third cumulant is⟨Ψ𝒑1i​Ψ𝒑2j​Ψ𝒑3l⟩c=i​(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒑123)​Ci​j​l​(𝒑1,𝒑2,𝒑3),\langle\Psi^{i}_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\Psi^{j}_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}\Psi^{l}_{{\bm{p}}_{3}}\rangle_{c}=i\,(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{p}}_{123})\,C^{ijl}({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{p}}_{3})\,,(301)Ci​j​l​(𝒑1,𝒑2,𝒑3)=L1i​(𝒑1)​L1j​(𝒑2)​L2l​(𝒑1,𝒑2)​P11​(p1)​P11​(p2)+2 cyclic perms.C^{ijl}({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2},{\bm{p}}_{3})=L_{1}^{i}({\bm{p}}_{1})\,L_{1}^{j}({\bm{p}}_{2})\,L_{2}^{l}({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2})\,P_{11}(p_{1})\,P_{11}(p_{2})+\text{2 cyclic perms}\,.(302)

(The overall factors ofiicome from theii’s in the
ansatz (284) together with the parity of the kernels,𝑳n​(−𝒑1,…,−𝒑n)=−𝑳n​(𝒑1,…,𝒑n){\bm{L}}_{n}(-{\bm{p}}_{1},\ldots,-{\bm{p}}_{n})=-{\bm{L}}_{n}({\bm{p}}_{1},\ldots,{\bm{p}}_{n}).)

Equivalence to SPT.If we nowexpandthe
exponentials in Eq. (274) in the cumulants — undoing the
Lagrangian resummation — we must recover the Eulerian loop
expansion: LPT and SPT solve the same equations, and differ only in
how the perturbative series is organized. At one loop this can be
verified explicitly; the bookkeeping is carried out in
AppendixC, with the resultP(k)=P11(k)+P22(k)+2P13(k).\boxed{\;P(k)=P_{11}(k)+P_{22}(k)+2P_{13}(k)\,.\;}(303)

So order by order inP11P_{11}, LPT and SPT are thesameperturbative expansion, as they must be — they solve the same
equations. The difference is entirely in what is kept resummed, and
here one must be careful: neither the zero-lag cumulantsA(2​n)A^{(2n)}nor the correlationsB(N)B^{(N)}are physical by themselves. Their
large IR parts cancel in the combination in which they enter
Eq. (274) — this is the1−cos⁡(𝒍⋅𝒒)1-\cos({\bm{l}}\cdot{\bm{q}})structure of Eq. (280) — leaving the variance of therelativedisplacement across the separation𝒒{\bm{q}}, as
dictated by the equivalence principle. It is this combination that
LPT keeps in the exponent and that SPT Taylor-expands. For the
smooth part of the spectrum the expansion is harmless; for the BAO
wiggles the surviving exponent is of order unity,k2​Σ2=O​(1)k^{2}\Sigma^{2}=O(1), and the expansion fails — as we saw in
momentum space in Chapter 3, and as we will shortly see in position space.

## 7.7EFT Corrections to LPT

Since LPT and SPT are the same expansion order by order, LPT
inherits all the UV diseases of Chapter 3: the loop integrals probe
modes beyondkNLk_{\mathrm{NL}}, where the perturbative displacement is
meaningless. The Lagrangian description also makes the physical
origin of the breakdown transparent: aftershell crossingthe
map𝒒→𝒙{\bm{q}}\to{\bm{x}}becomes multi-valued, and the true trajectory of a
mass element bears no resemblance to its perturbative extrapolation.
The remedy is the same as in the Eulerian theory: coarse-grain and
parameterize the effect of the short modes by effective terms in the
equation of motion. This is implemented in Lagrangian EFT[119,35]. The trajectory equation (269)
becomes𝚿′′+ℋ​𝚿′=−∇𝒙ϕ|𝒙​(𝒒)+𝑭eff​[∂i∂jϕ]​(𝒙fl;τ)+𝑭stoch,{\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}\,{\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime}=-\left.\bm{\nabla}_{\!{\bm{x}}}\phi\right|_{{\bm{x}}({\bm{q}})}+{\bm{F}}_{\mathrm{eff}}\big[\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\phi\big]({\bm{x}}_{\mathrm{fl}};\tau)+{\bm{F}}_{\mathrm{stoch}}\,,(304)

where the effective force𝑭eff{\bm{F}}_{\mathrm{eff}}is built, by the
equivalence principle, from second derivatives of the potential
(and their gradients), evaluated along the past trajectory — it is
non-local in time, exactly as the effective stress tensor of
Chapter 4, and becomes local order by order in perturbation theory
by the same mechanism. At leading order in fields and gradients,𝑭eff∝∇Δ​ϕ∝∇δ{\bm{F}}_{\mathrm{eff}}\propto\bm{\nabla}\,\Delta\phi\propto\bm{\nabla}\delta,
and solving with the Green’s function of
AppendixBproduces acounterterm
displacementwhich is a pure gradient,Δ​𝚿ctr​(𝒌,η)=−i​γ​(η)​𝒌​δ1​(𝒌,η),\Delta{\bm{\Psi}}_{\mathrm{ctr}}({\bm{k}},\eta)=-\,i\,\gamma(\eta)\,{\bm{k}}\;\delta_{1}({\bm{k}},\eta)\,,(305)

with the very same coefficientγ\gammaas in Chapter 4: indeed,−i​𝒌⋅Δ​𝚿ctr=−γ​k2​δ1-i{\bm{k}}\cdot\Delta{\bm{\Psi}}_{\mathrm{ctr}}=-\gamma k^{2}\delta_{1}reproduces the Eulerian counterterm (130), as it must
— the two descriptions are related by a change of variables, so
they share the same Wilson coefficients. Similarly, the stochastic
force generates astochastic displacement𝜺Ψ{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\Psi}; momentum conservation (Peebles’ argument of
Chapter 4) forces the short-scale dynamics to preserve the center
of mass of each patch, so𝜺Ψ​(𝒌)∝𝒌{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\Psi}({\bm{k}})\propto{\bm{k}}at
smallkk, its power spectrum starts atO​(k2)O(k^{2}), and the induced
density noise−i​𝒌⋅𝜺Ψ-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\Psi}again producesPstoch∝k4P_{\mathrm{stoch}}\propto k^{4}[111].

One practical remark. In evaluating the master
formula (274) one has a choice of what to keep
exponentiated. The standard scheme is: keep thelinear(Zel’dovich) displacement — more precisely, its
long-wavelength part — resummed in the exponent, and expand
everything else (higher-order cumulants, counterterms, stochastic
terms) to the desired loop order. The result is precisely the
IR-resummed one-loop EFT spectrum of Eq. (114): the
Lagrangian formulation delivers the IR resummation automatically,
while the EFT corrections restore the correct UV behavior. Expanding
the exponent completely would take us back to SPT; keepingallof it resummed would keep spurious UV pieces of the
displacement in the exponent, which makes the UV convergence
properties even worse.

## 7.8Lagrangian Bias

Galaxy bias also takes a particularly transparent form in Lagrangian
space. Galaxies form out of special initial patches, so it is
natural to weight the fluid elements by a functional of theinitialfields and then let gravity transport them:1+δg​(𝒙,τ)=∫d3​q​F​[δ1​(𝒒),…]​δD(3)​(𝒙−𝒒−𝚿​(𝒒,τ)),1+\delta_{g}({\bm{x}},\tau)=\int d^{3}q\;F\big[\delta_{1}({\bm{q}}),\ldots\big]\;\delta_{D}^{(3)}\big({\bm{x}}-{\bm{q}}-{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}},\tau)\big)\,,(306)

i.e. the tracers are painted on the initial conditions and thenadvectedby the same displacement field as the matter. By the
equivalence principle the weightFFcan depend on the initial
density, the initial tidal field, higher derivatives, and a
stochastic component:F=1+b1L​δ1​(𝒒)+b2L2​(δ12​(𝒒)−⟨δ12⟩)+b𝒢2L​𝒢2​(𝒒)+b∇2L​Δ​δ1​(𝒒)+ε​(𝒒)+⋯,F=1+b_{1}^{L}\,\delta_{1}({\bm{q}})+\frac{b_{2}^{L}}{2}\left(\delta_{1}^{2}({\bm{q}})-\langle\delta_{1}^{2}\rangle\right)+b_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}^{L}\mathcal{G}_{2}({\bm{q}})+b_{\nabla^{2}}^{L}\,\Delta\delta_{1}({\bm{q}})+\varepsilon({\bm{q}})+\cdots,(307)

where𝒢2​(𝒒)\mathcal{G}_{2}({\bm{q}})is the Galileon operator built from
the initial tidal field (δ1\delta_{1}here is the linear field at the
time of observation, so thebnLb_{n}^{L}are time-dependent numbers, as
usual). In Fourier space,δg​(𝒌)=∫d3​q​e−i​𝒌⋅𝒒​(F​(𝒒)​e−i​𝒌⋅𝚿​(𝒒)−1),\delta_{g}({\bm{k}})=\int d^{3}q\;e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}\left(F({\bm{q}})\,e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}})}-1\right)\,,(308)

and the galaxy spectra are given by the same cumulant machinery as
before, with the weightsF​(𝒒1)F({\bm{q}}_{1}),F​(𝒒2)F({\bm{q}}_{2})inserted under the
average.

Two structural lessons follow immediately. First, expanding to
linear order and using number conservation of the tracers,δg=δ+b1L​δ1\delta_{g}=\delta+b_{1}^{L}\,\delta_{1}, i.e.b1=1+b1L\boxed{\;b_{1}=1+b_{1}^{L}\;}(309)

— the Eulerian linear bias is the Lagrangian one plus the
contribution of the flow itself. Second, and more importantly, the
Lagrangian bias basis isnon-local in time by construction: all
the operators are evaluated on the initial slice, and the time
non-locality that plagued the Eulerian discussion of Chapter 5 is
entirely carried by the advection, i.e. by𝚿{\bm{\Psi}}. Expanding
Eq. (306) in perturbations, the advected initial
operators automatically generate the full Eulerian
basis (191), so the two expansions
are equivalent at the one-loop order. At the two-loop order,
however the Lagrangian expansion will generate more terms than
the naive Eulerian one due to the curl-displacement contributions[80].
However, these can be accounted for in a more systematic version
of the Eulerian bias[74].

## 7.9RSD in Lagrangian Space

Redshift-space distortions are arguably at their simplest in the
Lagrangian language: the mapping of Chapter 6 acts onpositions, and in LPT the position and the velocity are both
carried by the displacement,𝒗=𝚿′{\bm{v}}={\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime}. The redshift-space
trajectory is therefore𝒙s​(𝒒)=𝒒+𝚿​(𝒒)+z^⋅𝚿′​(𝒒)ℋ​z^≡𝒒+𝚿s​(𝒒).{\bm{x}}_{s}({\bm{q}})={\bm{q}}+{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}})+\frac{\hat{z}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime}({\bm{q}})}{\mathcal{H}}\,\hat{z}\equiv{\bm{q}}+{\bm{\Psi}}_{s}({\bm{q}})\,.(310)

For the EdS-approximated time dependence𝚿(n)∝D+n{\bm{\Psi}}^{(n)}\propto D_{+}^{n}we have𝚿′⁣(n)=n​f​ℋ​𝚿(n){\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime(n)}=nf\mathcal{H}\,{\bm{\Psi}}^{(n)}, so the
redshift-space displacement is obtained order by order by a simplelinear map,Ψs,i(n)=Ri​j(n)​Ψj(n),Ri​j(n)=δi​j+n​f​z^i​z^j.\Psi^{(n)}_{s,i}=R^{(n)}_{ij}\,\Psi^{(n)}_{j}\,,\qquad R^{(n)}_{ij}=\delta_{ij}+n\,f\,\hat{z}_{i}\hat{z}_{j}\,.(311)

All the formulas of this chapter then go through with𝚿→𝚿s{\bm{\Psi}}\to{\bm{\Psi}}_{s}(the bias weightsF​(𝒒)F({\bm{q}})are untouched — the
mapping only reshuffles positions):P(s)​(k,μ)=∫d3​q​e−i​𝒌⋅𝒒​(⟨F1​F2​e−i​𝒌⋅[𝚿s​(𝒒)−𝚿s​(0)]⟩−1).P^{(s)}(k,\mu)=\int d^{3}q\;e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}\left(\langle F_{1}F_{2}\,e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot[{\bm{\Psi}}_{s}({\bm{q}})-{\bm{\Psi}}_{s}(0)]}\rangle-1\right)\,.(312)

At linear order,−i​𝒌⋅Ψs(1)=−i​(ki+f​kz​z^i)​Ψi(1)=(1+f​μ2)​δ1-i{\bm{k}}\cdot\Psi^{(1)}_{s}=-i\,(k_{i}+fk_{z}\hat{z}_{i})\Psi^{(1)}_{i}=(1+f\mu^{2})\,\delta_{1}, and together withb1Lb_{1}^{L}from the weight
we recover the Kaiser formula (258).

The payoff comes in the infrared. The exponent in
Eq. (312) now involves theredshift-spacedisplacement, whose linear variance is anisotropic: contracting𝒌​R(1)=𝒌+f​kz​z^{\bm{k}}R^{(1)}={\bm{k}}+fk_{z}\hat{z}with the isotropic
correlator (278) gives|𝒌+f​kz​z^|2=k2​[1+f​(f+2)​μ2]|{\bm{k}}+fk_{z}\hat{z}|^{2}=k^{2}\left[1+f(f+2)\,\mu^{2}\right]for the
leading (isotropic) part. The BAO damping of Chapter 3 therefore
generalizes tok2​Σ2⟶k2​[1+f​(f+2)​μ2]​Σ2\boxed{\;k^{2}\Sigma^{2}\;\longrightarrow\;k^{2}\left[1+f(f+2)\,\mu^{2}\right]\Sigma^{2}\;}(313)

(plus subleading anisotropic corrections): the wiggles are damped
more strongly along the line of sight, because the same bulk flows
that displace the pairs also Doppler-shift them. This anisotropic
damping factor is what is used in the IR-resummed redshift-space
power spectrum in practical analyses.

Finally, the EFT corrections. The redshift-space
map (311) feeds thevelocitycounterterms
into the displacement: the corrections to𝚿′{\bm{\Psi}}^{\prime}(cf. the
velocity responseΔ​Θ(cs2)=(m+1)​Δ​δ(cs2)\Delta\Theta^{(c_{s}^{2})}=(m+1)\Delta\delta^{(c_{s}^{2})}of AppendixB) enter multiplied byz^i​z^j\hat{z}_{i}\hat{z}_{j}, so the counterterm
displacement (305) acquires line-of-sight components
with independent coefficients. Projecting onto the density, this
reproduces exactly theμ2\mu^{2}andμ4\mu^{4}counterterm structure of
Eq. (241), truncating atμ4\mu^{4}for the same
symmetry reasons; and since the short-scale velocity dispersion is
larger than the short-scale density response (Fingers of God, cf. Chapter 6), the line-of-sight coefficients are parametrically
enhanced. The Lagrangian and Eulerian EFTs in redshift space are,
once again, the same theory in different variables.

## 7.10Bulk Flows and the BAO in Position Space

We conclude with the position-space picture of the IR story of Chapter 3,
which in the Lagrangian language becomes almost trivial. Consider two
clumps of matter at separation𝒙1{\bm{x}}_{1}, with linear correlation functionξ​(x1)=⟨δ​(0)​δ​(𝒙1)⟩\xi(x_{1})=\langle\delta(0)\delta({\bm{x}}_{1})\rangle, and plunge them into a
long-wavelength flow. The flow is a linear-theory mode, statistically
independent of the short-wavelength modes that make up the clumps, so in
all correlators below the averages over the flow and over the clumps
factorize.

Suppose first that the flow iscoherentacross the pair: both
clumps are displaced by the same𝚿L{\bm{\Psi}}_{L}, and their trajectories are
parallel. Thenbotharguments of the correlator are shifted, and⟨δ​(𝚿L)​δ​(𝒙1+𝚿L)⟩=∫𝒌1∫𝒌2ei​𝒌2⋅𝒙1​⟨ei​(𝒌1+𝒌2)⋅𝚿L⟩​⟨δ𝒌1​δ𝒌2⟩=∫𝒌ei​𝒌⋅𝒙1​P​(k)=ξ​(x1),\langle\delta({\bm{\Psi}}_{L})\,\delta({\bm{x}}_{1}+{\bm{\Psi}}_{L})\rangle=\int_{{\bm{k}}_{1}}\!\int_{{\bm{k}}_{2}}e^{i{\bm{k}}_{2}\cdot{\bm{x}}_{1}}\,\langle e^{i({\bm{k}}_{1}+{\bm{k}}_{2})\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}_{L}}\rangle\,\langle\delta_{{\bm{k}}_{1}}\delta_{{\bm{k}}_{2}}\rangle=\int_{{\bm{k}}}e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}_{1}}\,P(k)=\xi(x_{1})\,,(314)

because⟨δ𝒌1​δ𝒌2⟩∝δD(3)​(𝒌1+𝒌2)\langle\delta_{{\bm{k}}_{1}}\delta_{{\bm{k}}_{2}}\rangle\propto\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}_{1}+{\bm{k}}_{2})forces𝒌2=−𝒌1{\bm{k}}_{2}=-{\bm{k}}_{1}, upon which the phaseei​(𝒌1+𝒌2)⋅𝚿Le^{i({\bm{k}}_{1}+{\bm{k}}_{2})\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}_{L}}equals unityidentically, before
any averaging. A coherent flow, however large, leaves the correlation
function untouched — the position-space version of the
equivalence-principle cancellation (97).

Whatisphysical is therelative displacementof the
pair,Δ​𝚿L≡𝚿L​(𝒙1)−𝚿L​(0)\Delta{\bm{\Psi}}_{L}\equiv{\bm{\Psi}}_{L}({\bm{x}}_{1})-{\bm{\Psi}}_{L}(0), generated by the
modes that vary across the separation. Shifting only the separation byΔ​𝚿L\Delta{\bm{\Psi}}_{L}and averaging over the (Gaussian, linear-theory) flow,⟨δ​(0)​δ​(𝒙1+Δ​𝚿L)⟩=∫𝒌ei​𝒌⋅𝒙1​⟨ei​𝒌⋅Δ​𝚿L⟩​P​(k)=e12​⟨Δ​ΨLi​Δ​ΨLj⟩​∂i∂j​ξ​(x1):\langle\delta(0)\,\delta({\bm{x}}_{1}+\Delta{\bm{\Psi}}_{L})\rangle=\int_{{\bm{k}}}e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}_{1}}\,\langle e^{i{\bm{k}}\cdot\Delta{\bm{\Psi}}_{L}}\rangle\,P(k)=e^{\frac{1}{2}\langle\Delta\Psi^{i}_{L}\,\Delta\Psi^{j}_{L}\rangle\,\partial_{i}\partial_{j}}\;\xi(x_{1})\,:(315)

the bulk flows act on the correlation function as a Gaussiansmearingoperator. Now recall the two components ofξ\xi: a
smooth, featureless broadband and the BAO peak,ξ​(r)=ξs​(r)+ξw​(r),ξs​(r)=(r0r)γ,ξw​(r)=Aw​e−(r−ℓBAO)22​σw2,\xi(r)=\xi_{s}(r)+\xi_{w}(r)\,,\qquad\xi_{s}(r)=\left(\frac{r_{0}}{r}\right)^{\gamma}\,,\qquad\xi_{w}(r)=A_{w}\,e^{-\frac{(r-\ell_{\mathrm{BAO}})^{2}}{2\sigma_{w}^{2}}}\,,(316)

withℓBAO=1/kosc≃110​Mpc/h\ell_{\mathrm{BAO}}=1/k_{\mathrm{osc}}\simeq 110\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h(cf. Eq. (103)) and a peak widthσw∼10​Mpc/h\sigma_{w}\sim 10\,\mathrm{Mpc}/hset by Silk damping. Since the displacement correlations decay acrossℓBAO\ell_{\mathrm{BAO}}, the relative displacement is of the order of the
one-point r.m.s. displacement estimated in Chapter 2,|Δ​𝚿L|∼10​Mpc/h|\Delta{\bm{\Psi}}_{L}|\sim 10\,\mathrm{Mpc}/h. For the smooth part the smearing is then
negligible — each derivative in Eq. (315) costs1/r1/r:⟨Δ​ΨL2⟩​∂2ξs∼⟨Δ​ΨL2⟩r2​ξs∼10−2​ξs.\langle\Delta\Psi_{L}^{2}\rangle\,\partial^{2}\xi_{s}\sim\frac{\langle\Delta\Psi_{L}^{2}\rangle}{r^{2}}\,\xi_{s}\sim 10^{-2}\,\xi_{s}\,.(317)

For the peak, however, the derivatives are enhanced by its width, not by
the separation:⟨Δ​ΨL2⟩​∂2ξw∼⟨Δ​ΨL2⟩σw2​ξw∼ξw.\langle\Delta\Psi_{L}^{2}\rangle\,\partial^{2}\xi_{w}\sim\frac{\langle\Delta\Psi_{L}^{2}\rangle}{\sigma_{w}^{2}}\,\xi_{w}\sim\xi_{w}\,.(318)

The width of the BAO peak is of the same order as the large-scale
relative displacements, so Taylor expanding the exponent in
Eq. (315) makes no sense. But this Taylor expansion is
exactly what SPT does: the Eulerian expansion is a power series inP11P_{11}, and the displacement variance is itself⟨Δ​ΨL2⟩∼∫𝒑P11​(p)/p2=O​(P11)\langle\Delta\Psi_{L}^{2}\rangle\sim\int_{\bm{p}}P_{11}(p)/p^{2}=O(P_{11}). This is
the position-space counterpart of the momentum-space breakdown of
Chapter 3 (k2​Σ2∼1k^{2}\Sigma^{2}\sim 1for the wiggly part), and it makes the
resolution self-evident: keep the displacements in the exponent, as LPT
does automatically.

The quantitative connection to the damping factor of Chapter 3 is
sharp. The Gaussian peak inξw\xi_{w}is aradialfeature at|𝒓|=ℓBAO|{\bm{r}}|=\ell_{\mathrm{BAO}}, so its smearing — and hence the damping of
the wiggles in Fourier space — is controlled by the component ofΔ​𝚿L\Delta{\bm{\Psi}}_{L}alongthe separation, evaluated at the BAO scale:Σ2=12​⟨(𝒒^⋅Δ​𝚿L​(𝒒))2⟩|q=ℓBAO,\Sigma^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\,\langle\big(\hat{{\bm{q}}}\cdot\Delta{\bm{\Psi}}_{L}({\bm{q}})\big)^{2}\rangle\Big|_{q\,=\,\ell_{\mathrm{BAO}}}\,,(319)

which reproducesexactlyEq. (112), including the
spherical-Bessel bracket (see the Homework below). The IR-resummed
spectrum (114) is thus nothing but the Fourier transform
of the smeared correlation function (315).

Homework:using⟨Δ​ΨLi​Δ​ΨLj⟩​(𝒒)=2​∫𝒑pi​pjp4​P11​(p)​[1−cos⁡(𝒑⋅𝒒)]\langle\Delta\Psi_{L}^{i}\,\Delta\Psi_{L}^{j}\rangle({\bm{q}})=2\int_{\bm{p}}\frac{p^{i}p^{j}}{p^{4}}\,P_{11}(p)\left[1-\cos({\bm{p}}\cdot{\bm{q}})\right]and the angular integral∫d​Ωp4​π​p^i​p^j​ei​𝒑⋅𝒒=j1​(p​q)p​q​δi​j−j2​(p​q)​q^i​q^j\int\frac{d\Omega_{p}}{4\pi}\,\hat{p}^{i}\hat{p}^{j}\,e^{i{\bm{p}}\cdot{\bm{q}}}=\frac{j_{1}(pq)}{pq}\,\delta^{ij}-j_{2}(pq)\,\hat{q}^{i}\hat{q}^{j},

## 8Chapter 8: Outlook

There are many important topics that are not covered in these notes. Let
us mention some of them.

## Bispectrum.

The bispectrum is the Fourier-space three-point function:⟨δ​(𝒌1)​δ​(𝒌2)​δ​(𝒌3)⟩=(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌1+𝒌2+𝒌3)​B​(k1,k2,k3).\langle\delta({\bm{k}}_{1})\,\delta({\bm{k}}_{2})\,\delta({\bm{k}}_{3})\rangle=(2\pi)^{3}\,\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}_{1}+{\bm{k}}_{2}+{\bm{k}}_{3})\;B(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\,.(321)

It vanishes for Gaussian fields, but
it is generated by non-linearity. In EFT
attree level(leading order):B112(k1,k2,k3)=2F2(𝒌1,𝒌2)P11(k1)P11(k2)+2 cyclic perms.\boxed{B_{112}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=2\,F_{2}({\bm{k}}_{1},{\bm{k}}_{2})\,P_{11}(k_{1})\,P_{11}(k_{2})+\text{2 cyclic perms}\,.}(322)

This uses the sameF2F_{2}kernel from eq. (71).
One can generalize it to one-loop order and also include
galaxy bias, redshift space distortions, IR resummation, etc.[41,123,124,94].
A conceptually interesting
novelty is the appearance ofmixed stochasticcontributions
likeδg⊃ε​δ\delta_{g}\supset\varepsilon\deltaetc., which
are fully redundant at the power spectrum level.

## Primordial Non-Gaussianity.

If the initial conditions in our Universe satisfy⟨δ1​δ1​δ1⟩≠0\langle\delta_{1}\delta_{1}\delta_{1}\rangle\neq 0, many
interesting modifications to EFT should happen[125,126,127,128,129,130,71,131,132,133,134,135,136,137,105].
For instance, the⟨δ(1)​δ(2)⟩\langle\delta^{(1)}\delta^{(2)}\ranglepower spectrum corrections are not zero anymore.
However, most importantly, there will be new terms
in the galaxy bias expansion. Their origin is similar
to that of bias itself: the modifications of statistics
of the initial distributions can be effectively captured by
adding new fields in the deterministic field expansion ofδg\delta_{g}.
In particular, in the
presence of thelocalprimordial non-Gaussianity
one can add a new operatorbϕ​ϕ​(𝒒)b_{\phi}\phi({\bm{q}})(ϕ​(𝒒)\phi({\bm{q}})is the gravitational Bardeen potential on the Lagrangian time slice), giving rise
toscale-dependent bias, whose measurement is
one of the main
targets of ongoing and upcoming galaxy surveys. Importantly,ϕ​(𝒒)\phi({\bm{q}})should be evaluated on the Lagrangian coordinates
in order to respect the equivalence principle!

## Time-sliced perturbation theory (TSPT).

This is a formulation of EFT in the language of
generating functionals
familiar to particle
physicists. The relevant objects in this framework are equal-timenn-point functions,
which are more natural statistical
observables than the cosmological fields used in other EFT formulations.
The advantage of this approach is
that one makes the symmetries related to the equivalence
principle manifest, which is beneficial for IR resummation.
This allows one to develop IR resummation for an arbitrarynn-point functions, which is very difficult in alternative
formulations, such as LPT.
TSPT also provides a natural framework to implement
Wilson-Polchinski renormalization group ideas
at the level of the effective action[138].

## Backreaction.

One of the original motivations to develop EFT for LSS is to show
the absence of backreacion of small scale non-linear
structure onto large scales.
In the Newtonian settings this is guaranteed by Peebles’ argument
we discussed in Chapter 4. EFT for LSS can be formulated
in a fully relativistic setting and used to upgrade
Peebles’ argument to the level of full general relativity[4].

## Lyman-α\alphaforest.

An interesting example of a tracer with a different symmetry
structure is the Lyman-α\alphaforest. The observed
Lyman-α\alphaforest
flux decrement can be modeled
as a non-linear transformation of a
conserved redshift-space tracer field,
whose normalization requires
line-of-sight operators. The corresponding bias expansion
only has an SO(2) symmetry of rotations around the line-of-sight, as opposed
to the full SO(3) rotation symmetry of galaxies.
As a result, at the linear level one has an extra bias parameterbηb_{\eta}in the Kaiser formulaδF=(b1−bη​f​μ2)​δ1\delta_{F}=(b_{1}-b_{\eta}f\mu^{2})\delta_{1}[139,140,78,141,142,143,79,144,145].

## Field-level EFT.

In these notes we focused
on the galaxy power spectrum calculation, but EFT is a theory for the entire
field. This has several implications. First, one can use EFT as a forward model to produce galaxy maps at the pixel level.
This is helpful in order to get precision measurements of
EFT parameters by virtue of cosmic variance cancellation[146,147,148,57,58,110].
Second, one can use EFT to measure cosmological
parameters from the whole density map,
which can avoid a potential loss of information
due to compression of the galaxy field intonn-point functions[149,150,151,152,153,154].
These topics are a subject of intense investigation at the moment.

## EFT for galaxy shapes.

The shapes of galaxiesγa​b\gamma_{ab},
which is a trace-less spin-2 field, correlates
with the underlying cosmological fields. These correlations,
calledintrinsic alignments(IA)
play an important role in cosmic shear surveys, where
they represent a major source of contamination of the weak
lensing signal. One can use EFT arguments, i.e. symmetries
and dimensional analysis, to model IA. This problem is a generalization
of the galaxy bias expansion to a spin-2 field. In real space
at the lowest (linear) order there is only one spin-2 treeless field
which one can write,γa​b=𝒞s​(∂a∂bΦ^−δa​b3​Δ​Φ^).\gamma_{ab}=\mathcal{C}_{s}\left(\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\hat{\Phi}-\frac{\delta_{ab}}{3}\Delta\hat{\Phi}\right)\,.

EFT thinking allows one to systematically go beyond the linear order[155,156,157].

## Counts-in-cells statistics.

The path integral
formulation of EFT can be used to predict the one-point
probability distribution function of the spherically averaged
density field. This is one of the most basic large-scale
structure observables, which is quite hard to predict
from first principles. The proper
calculation parallels that of tunneling in
quantum field theory[158,62].
The result is an
EFT for density fluctuations
in a non-trivial spherical collapse background.
This formulation is non-perturbative as the spherical
collapse background is a fully non-linear solution.

with some straightforward computations that have been independently

## Appendix AGaussian Random Fields and Wick’s Theorem

In this appendix we collect the basic formalism of Gaussian random
fields and derive Wick’s theorem, which is stated and heavily used
in the main text (Chapters 2 and 3). The same generating-functional
technique is used in the Press–Schechter computation of Chapter 5.

## A.1Warm-Up: the Multivariate Gaussian

Consider first a finite collection of random variablesx=(x1,…,xn)x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})with the Gaussian probability densityP​(x)=1𝒩​e−12​xT​M​x,𝒩=∫dn​x​e−12​xT​M​x=(2​π)n/2det⁡M,P(x)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}\,x^{T}M\,x}\,,\qquad\mathcal{N}=\int d^{n}x\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}\,x^{T}M\,x}=\frac{(2\pi)^{n/2}}{\sqrt{\det M}}\,,(323)

whereMMis a symmetric, positive-definiten×nn\times nmatrix. All
the moments are encoded in the generating functionZ​(J)≡⟨eJT​x⟩=1𝒩​∫dn​x​e−12​xT​M​x+JT​x,⟨xi1​⋯​xim⟩=∂mZ​(J)∂Ji1​⋯​∂Jim|J=0.Z(J)\equiv\langle e^{J^{T}x}\rangle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\int d^{n}x\;e^{-\frac{1}{2}\,x^{T}M\,x+J^{T}x}\,,\qquad\langle x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{m}}\rangle=\frac{\partial^{m}Z(J)}{\partial J_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial J_{i_{m}}}\bigg|_{J=0}\,.(324)

The integral is evaluated by completing the square: the shiftx→x+M−1​Jx\to x+M^{-1}Jremoves the source term,−12​xT​M​x+JT​x⟶−12​xT​M​x+12​JT​M−1​J,-\frac{1}{2}\,x^{T}M\,x+J^{T}x\;\longrightarrow\;-\frac{1}{2}\,x^{T}M\,x+\frac{1}{2}\,J^{T}M^{-1}J\,,(325)

and the remaining integral over the shifted variable cancels against𝒩\mathcal{N}, leavingZ(J)=exp{12JTM−1J}.\boxed{\;Z(J)=\exp\left\{\frac{1}{2}\,J^{T}M^{-1}J\right\}\,.\;}(326)

Differentiating twice,⟨xi​xj⟩=(M−1)i​j≡Ci​j\langle x_{i}x_{j}\rangle=(M^{-1})_{ij}\equiv C_{ij}:
the covariance matrix is theinverseof the matrix in the
exponent. Differentiating four times, each derivative must either
produce a factor(M−1​J)i(M^{-1}J)_{i}from the exponent or remove one
already produced, so atJ=0J=0the result is a sum over complete
pairings,⟨xi​xj​xk​xl⟩=Ci​j​Ck​l+Ci​k​Cj​l+Ci​l​Cj​k,\langle x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{l}\rangle=C_{ij}C_{kl}+C_{ik}C_{jl}+C_{il}C_{jk}\,,(327)

— Wick’s theorem, already at finitenn.

Nothing in this computation refers tonnbeing finite. To describe
a randomfield, promote the discrete label to a continuous
one,i→𝒙∈ℝ3i\to{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}, with the dictionaryxi→ϕ​(𝒙),Ji→J​(𝒙),Mi​j→M​(𝒙,𝒚),∑i→∫d3​x,dn​x→𝒟​ϕ≡lim∏𝒙d​ϕ​(𝒙),x_{i}\to\phi({\bm{x}})\,,\quad J_{i}\to J({\bm{x}})\,,\quad M_{ij}\to M({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\,,\quad\sum_{i}\to\int d^{3}x\,,\quad d^{n}x\to\mathcal{D}\phi\equiv\lim\prod_{{\bm{x}}}d\phi({\bm{x}})\,,(328)

so thatJT​x→∫d3​x​J​(𝒙)​ϕ​(𝒙)J^{T}x\to\int d^{3}x\,J({\bm{x}})\phi({\bm{x}}), matrix
multiplication becomes integration, and the inverse matrix becomes
the inverse kernel,∫d3​y​M​(𝒙,𝒚)​ξ​(𝒚,𝒛)=δD(3)​(𝒙−𝒛)\int d^{3}y\,M({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\,\xi({\bm{y}},{\bm{z}})=\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{x}}-{\bm{z}}). The only genuinely new feature of the
continuum is that the determinant in𝒩\mathcal{N}becomes formally
infinite — but it cancels in everynormalizedexpectation
value, which is why the generating functional below carries the
factor𝒩−1\mathcal{N}^{-1}from the start.

## A.2Gaussian Random Fields

With this dictionary at hand, letϕ​(𝒙)\phi({\bm{x}})be a classical random field in 3d Euclidean space.
Its statistics are fully specified by thegenerating
functionalZ​[J]=𝒩−1​∫𝒟​ϕ​e−W​[ϕ]​e∫d3​x​J​(𝒙)​ϕ​(𝒙),𝒩=∫𝒟​ϕ​e−W​[ϕ],Z[J]=\mathcal{N}^{-1}\int\mathcal{D}\phi\;e^{-W[\phi]}\,e^{\int d^{3}x\,J({\bm{x}})\phi({\bm{x}})}\,,\qquad\mathcal{N}=\int\mathcal{D}\phi\;e^{-W[\phi]}\,,(329)

whereW​[ϕ]W[\phi]is the statistical weight (“effective action”).
The field isGaussianif the weight is quadratic,W​[ϕ]=12​∫d3​x​d3​y​M​(𝒙,𝒚)​ϕ​(𝒙)​ϕ​(𝒚),W[\phi]=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{3}x\,d^{3}y\;M({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})\,\phi({\bm{x}})\,\phi({\bm{y}})\,,(330)

with some positive kernelMM. Allnn-point correlation functions
follow from functional derivatives,⟨ϕ​(𝒙1)​⋯​ϕ​(𝒙n)⟩=δn​Z​[J]δ​J​(𝒙1)​⋯​δ​J​(𝒙n)|J=0.\langle\phi({\bm{x}}_{1})\cdots\phi({\bm{x}}_{n})\rangle=\frac{\delta^{n}Z[J]}{\delta J({\bm{x}}_{1})\cdots\delta J({\bm{x}}_{n})}\bigg|_{J=0}\,.(331)

The integral (329) is computed by completing the square
exactly as in the warm-up, Eq. (326): shiftingϕ​(𝒙)→ϕ​(𝒙)+∫d3​y​ξ​(𝒙,𝒚)​J​(𝒚)\phi({\bm{x}})\to\phi({\bm{x}})+\int d^{3}y\,\xi({\bm{x}},{\bm{y}})J({\bm{y}})withξ≡M−1\xi\equiv M^{-1}, the source term is eliminated and the
normalization cancels, leavingZ[J]=exp{12∫d3x1d3x2J(𝒙1)ξ(𝒙1,𝒙2)J(𝒙2)}.\boxed{\;Z[J]=\exp\left\{\frac{1}{2}\int d^{3}x_{1}\,d^{3}x_{2}\;J({\bm{x}}_{1})\,\xi({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{x}}_{2})\,J({\bm{x}}_{2})\right\}\,.\;}(332)

Everything follows from this expression:
- 1.

Differentiating twice,⟨ϕ​(𝒙1)​ϕ​(𝒙2)⟩=ξ​(𝒙1,𝒙2)≡ξ12\langle\phi({\bm{x}}_{1})\phi({\bm{x}}_{2})\rangle=\xi({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{x}}_{2})\equiv\xi_{12}: the kernel of the weight is the
inverse of the two-point function.
- 2.

Z​[J]Z[J]is even underJ→−JJ\to-J, so all correlators with anoddnumber of fields vanish.
- 3.

Differentiating four times, the three ways of pairing up the
derivatives give⟨ϕ​(𝒙1)​ϕ​(𝒙2)​ϕ​(𝒙3)​ϕ​(𝒙4)⟩=ξ12​ξ34+ξ13​ξ24+ξ14​ξ23,\langle\phi({\bm{x}}_{1})\phi({\bm{x}}_{2})\phi({\bm{x}}_{3})\phi({\bm{x}}_{4})\rangle=\xi_{12}\,\xi_{34}+\xi_{13}\,\xi_{24}+\xi_{14}\,\xi_{23}\,,(333)

which is exactly the four-point contraction identity used for the
one-loop power spectrum in Chapter 3.
- 4.

In general, each derivative must either hit the exponent
(producing a newξ​J\xi J) or an already-producedJJ; atJ=0J=0only completely paired terms survive, givingWick’s theorem:⟨ϕ​(𝒙1)​⋯​ϕ​(𝒙2​N)⟩=∑pairings∏pairs​(i​j)ξi​j,\langle\phi({\bm{x}}_{1})\cdots\phi({\bm{x}}_{2N})\rangle=\sum_{\text{pairings}}\;\prod_{\text{pairs }(ij)}\xi_{ij}\,,(334)

with(2​N−1)!!(2N-1)!!terms in the sum — precisely the statement of
Chapter 2.

Two useful corollaries. First, taking the logarithm of
Eq. (332):ln⁡Z​[J]\ln Z[J]— the generator ofconnectedcorrelators (cumulants) — is exactly quadratic,
so for a Gaussian field all cumulants beyond the second vanish.
Written for a linear combinationX=∫J​ϕX=\int J\phi, this is the
statement⟨eX⟩=e12​⟨X2⟩c,\langle e^{X}\rangle=e^{\frac{1}{2}\langle X^{2}\rangle_{c}}\,,(335)

i.e. the cumulant expansion theorem used in Chapter 7 truncates atN=2N=2— this is what made the Zel’dovich power
spectrum (280) computable in closed form. Second,lineartime evolution preserves Gaussianity: ifϕ​(𝒌,τ)=g​(k,τ)​ϕ0​(𝒌)\phi({\bm{k}},\tau)=g(k,\tau)\,\phi_{0}({\bm{k}})is a linear map of
Gaussian initial conditions, all the correlators above, including
unequal-time ones, still obey Wick’s theorem. Non-Gaussianity in
the late Universe is generated precisely by thenon-linearmode coupling of Chapter 3.

Symmetries and Fourier space.Cosmological ensembles are
statistically homogeneous and isotropic, soξ​(𝒙1,𝒙2)=ξ​(|𝒙1−𝒙2|)\xi({\bm{x}}_{1},{\bm{x}}_{2})=\xi(|{\bm{x}}_{1}-{\bm{x}}_{2}|). In Fourier space (with the
conventions of Eq. (17), and the reality conditionϕ∗​(𝒌)=ϕ​(−𝒌)\phi^{*}({\bm{k}})=\phi(-{\bm{k}})),⟨ϕ​(𝒌)​ϕ​(𝒌′)⟩=∫d3​x1​d3​x2​e−i​𝒌⋅𝒙1−i​𝒌′⋅𝒙2​ξ​(|𝒙1−𝒙2|)=(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌+𝒌′)​∫d3​z​ξ​(z)​e−i​𝒌⋅𝒛≡(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌+𝒌′)​P​(k),\begin{split}\langle\phi({\bm{k}})\phi({\bm{k}}^{\prime})\rangle&=\int d^{3}x_{1}\,d^{3}x_{2}\;e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{x}}_{1}-i{\bm{k}}^{\prime}\cdot{\bm{x}}_{2}}\;\xi(|{\bm{x}}_{1}-{\bm{x}}_{2}|)\\
&=(2\pi)^{3}\,\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}+{\bm{k}}^{\prime})\int d^{3}z\;\xi(z)\,e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{z}}}\equiv(2\pi)^{3}\,\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}+{\bm{k}}^{\prime})\,P(k)\,,\end{split}(336)

recovering the power spectrum of Eq. (18): homogeneity
produces the momentum-conserving delta function, isotropy makesPPa function ofk=|𝒌|k=|{\bm{k}}|only. Wick’s theorem in Fourier space
reads⟨ϕ​(𝒌1)​⋯​ϕ​(𝒌2​N+1)⟩=0,⟨ϕ​(𝒌1)​⋯​ϕ​(𝒌2​N)⟩=∑pairings∏pairs​(i​j)⟨ϕ​(𝒌i)​ϕ​(𝒌j)⟩,\langle\phi({\bm{k}}_{1})\cdots\phi({\bm{k}}_{2N+1})\rangle=0\,,\qquad\langle\phi({\bm{k}}_{1})\cdots\phi({\bm{k}}_{2N})\rangle=\sum_{\text{pairings}}\;\prod_{\text{pairs }(ij)}\langle\phi({\bm{k}}_{i})\,\phi({\bm{k}}_{j})\rangle\,,(337)

which is the form used throughout the loop computations of the main
text.

Relation to the Press–Schechter computation.The
generating functional also underlies the halo statistics of
Chapter 5: the one-point PDF of the smoothed density,
Eq. (173), is nothing butZ​[J]Z[J]evaluated on the
localized sourceJ​(𝒙)=λ​WR​(𝒙)J({\bm{x}})=\lambda\,W_{R}({\bm{x}}), and the joint
two-point PDF (183) corresponds to a source with two
components,J=λ1​WR1+λ2​WR2J=\lambda_{1}W_{R_{1}}+\lambda_{2}W_{R_{2}}shifted by
the separation. Constrained Gaussian statistics of this kind are
the bread and butter of analytic halo models.

## Appendix BGreen’s Functions inη=ln⁡D+\eta=\ln D_{+}

In this appendix we derive the retarded Green’s functions of the
linearized fluid system in the time variableη=ln⁡D+\eta=\ln D_{+}. They are
used in the main text to compute the response of the density field
to the effective stress tensor (Chapters 3, 4). The punchline is
that in the EdS approximationΩm=f2\Omega_{m}=f^{2}the linear system hasconstantcoefficients, so the equations acquire a
time-translation symmetry inη\eta: the Green’s functions depend
only on the differences≡η−η′s\equiv\eta-\eta^{\prime}and can be obtained in
closed form.

## B.1The linear system

It is convenient to collect the fields into a doublet (to avoid confusion, in this appendix we do not use the displacement field which is denoted byΨ\Psias well),Ψa​(𝒌,η)≡(δ𝒌Θ𝒌),a=1,2,\Psi_{a}({\bm{k}},\eta)\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{{\bm{k}}}\\[2.0pt]
\Theta_{{\bm{k}}}\end{pmatrix},\qquad a=1,2\,,(338)

withΘ=−∂ivi/(f​ℋ)\Theta=-\partial_{i}v^{i}/(f\mathcal{H})as in the main text; the
normalization is chosen so that on the linear growing modeΨ1(1)=Ψ2(1)=eη​δ0​(𝒌)\Psi_{1}^{(1)}=\Psi_{2}^{(1)}=e^{\eta}\,\delta_{0}({\bm{k}}). The equations of
motion (61)–(62) then take the form∂ηΨa​(𝒌,η)+Ωa​b​Ψb​(𝒌,η)=∫𝒒1∫𝒒2(2​π)3​δD(3)​(𝒌−𝒒12)​γa​b​c​(𝒒1,𝒒2)​Ψb​(𝒒1,η)​Ψc​(𝒒2,η)+Ja​(𝒌,η),\partial_{\eta}\Psi_{a}({\bm{k}},\eta)+\Omega_{ab}\,\Psi_{b}({\bm{k}},\eta)=\int_{{\bm{q}}_{1}}\int_{{\bm{q}}_{2}}(2\pi)^{3}\,\delta_{D}^{(3)}({\bm{k}}-{\bm{q}}_{12})\;\gamma_{abc}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})\;\Psi_{b}({\bm{q}}_{1},\eta)\,\Psi_{c}({\bm{q}}_{2},\eta)+J_{a}({\bm{k}},\eta)\,,(339)

whereJa=(0,J2)TJ_{a}=(0,J_{2})^{T}collects possible linear sources — such as
the counterterm sources of Chapter 4, which enter the Euler equation
only. The symmetrized vertexγa​b​c​(𝒒1,𝒒2)=γa​c​b​(𝒒2,𝒒1)\gamma_{abc}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})=\gamma_{acb}({\bm{q}}_{2},{\bm{q}}_{1})encodes the mode-coupling kernelsα\alphaandβ\betaof
Eq. (63); its only non-vanishing components areγ121​(𝒒1,𝒒2)=α​(𝒒1,𝒒2)2,γ112​(𝒒1,𝒒2)=α​(𝒒2,𝒒1)2,γ222​(𝒒1,𝒒2)=β​(𝒒1,𝒒2).\gamma_{121}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})=\frac{\alpha({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})}{2}\,,\qquad\gamma_{112}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})=\frac{\alpha({\bm{q}}_{2},{\bm{q}}_{1})}{2}\,,\qquad\gamma_{222}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})=\beta({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})\,.(340)

Indeed, fora=1a=1the twoα\alpha-components add up under the
symmetric integral to reproduce the right-hand side of the
continuity equation,γ121​Ψ2​(𝒒1)​Ψ1​(𝒒2)+γ112​Ψ1​(𝒒1)​Ψ2​(𝒒2)→α​(𝒒1,𝒒2)​Θ𝒒1​δ𝒒2\gamma_{121}\Psi_{2}({\bm{q}}_{1})\Psi_{1}({\bm{q}}_{2})+\gamma_{112}\Psi_{1}({\bm{q}}_{1})\Psi_{2}({\bm{q}}_{2})\to\alpha({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})\,\Theta_{{\bm{q}}_{1}}\delta_{{\bm{q}}_{2}},
while fora=2a=2the single componentγ222\gamma_{222}directly
reproducesβ​(𝒒1,𝒒2)​Θ𝒒1​Θ𝒒2\beta({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})\,\Theta_{{\bm{q}}_{1}}\Theta_{{\bm{q}}_{2}}(no factor of1/21/2is needed here, sinceβ\betais already
symmetric and carries an explicit1/21/2in its definition).
The matrix of linear coefficients isΩa​b=(0−1−3​Ωm2​f23​Ωm2​f2−1)→Ωm=f2(0−1−3212).\Omega_{ab}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\[4.0pt]
-\dfrac{3\Omega_{m}}{2f^{2}}&\dfrac{3\Omega_{m}}{2f^{2}}-1\end{pmatrix}\;\xrightarrow{\;\Omega_{m}=f^{2}\;}\;\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\[4.0pt]
-\dfrac{3}{2}&\dfrac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.(341)

In the EdS approximation the matrix is constant, which is the source
of all the simplifications below.

## B.2Second-Order Equation, Modes and Wronskian

EliminatingΘ\Thetafrom the linearized system (the first equation
givesΘ=∂ηδ\Theta=\partial_{\eta}\delta), we obtain a single second-order
equation with constant coefficients,δ¨+12​δ˙−32​δ=j​(η),˙≡dd​η,\ddot{\delta}+\frac{1}{2}\,\dot{\delta}-\frac{3}{2}\,\delta=j(\eta)\,,\qquad\dot{\phantom{x}}\equiv\frac{d}{d\eta}\,,(342)

wherejjis a generic linear source. Substitutingδ=eλ​η\delta=e^{\lambda\eta}into the homogeneous equation gives the
characteristic polynomialλ2+12​λ−32=0⟺(2​λ+3)​(λ−1)=0⟹λ+=1,λ−=−32,\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\lambda-\frac{3}{2}=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(2\lambda+3)(\lambda-1)=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\lambda_{+}=1\,,\quad\lambda_{-}=-\frac{3}{2}\,,(343)

so the two independent solutions are the growing and decaying modes,D+​(η)=eη,D−​(η)=e−3​η/2=D+−3/2.D_{+}(\eta)=e^{\eta}\,,\qquad D_{-}(\eta)=e^{-3\eta/2}=D_{+}^{-3/2}\,.(344)

The first is tautological (η=ln⁡D+\eta=\ln D_{+}by definition), while the
second is the familiar EdS relationD−∝D+−3/2D_{-}\propto D_{+}^{-3/2}, here
valid for generalΛ\LambdaCDM within theΩm=f2\Omega_{m}=f^{2}approximation. Their Wronskian isW​(η)=D+​D˙−−D−​D˙+=−32​e−η/2−e−η/2=−52​e−η/2,W(\eta)=D_{+}\dot{D}_{-}-D_{-}\dot{D}_{+}=-\frac{3}{2}\,e^{-\eta/2}-e^{-\eta/2}=-\frac{5}{2}\,e^{-\eta/2}\,,(345)

consistent with Abel’s identity,W∝exp⁡[−∫η12​𝑑η′]=e−η/2W\propto\exp[-\int^{\eta}\tfrac{1}{2}\,d\eta^{\prime}]=e^{-\eta/2}.

## B.3The Scalar Green’s Function

The retarded Green’s function of Eq. (342)
satisfiesg¨η+12​g˙η−32​gη=δD​(η−η′),gη=0​for​η<η′,\ddot{g}_{\eta}+\frac{1}{2}\,\dot{g}_{\eta}-\frac{3}{2}\,g_{\eta}=\delta_{D}(\eta-\eta^{\prime})\,,\qquad g_{\eta}=0\;\;\text{for}\;\;\eta<\eta^{\prime}\,,(346)

and is given by the standard two-solution formulagη​(η,η′)=ΘH​(η−η′)​D+​(η′)​D−​(η)−D−​(η′)​D+​(η)W​(η′).g_{\eta}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})=\Theta_{H}(\eta-\eta^{\prime})\,\frac{D_{+}(\eta^{\prime})\,D_{-}(\eta)-D_{-}(\eta^{\prime})\,D_{+}(\eta)}{W(\eta^{\prime})}\,.(347)

The numerator equalse−η′/2​[e−3​s/2−es]e^{-\eta^{\prime}/2}\big[e^{-3s/2}-e^{s}\big]withs=η−η′s=\eta-\eta^{\prime}, and
dividing byW​(η′)=−52​e−η′/2W(\eta^{\prime})=-\tfrac{5}{2}e^{-\eta^{\prime}/2}theη′\eta^{\prime}-dependence cancels completely — the promised translation
invariance:gη​(s)=25​ΘH​(s)​[es−e−3​s/2],s=η−η′\boxed{\;g_{\eta}(s)=\frac{2}{5}\,\Theta_{H}(s)\left[e^{s}-e^{-3s/2}\right]\,,\qquad s=\eta-\eta^{\prime}\;}(348)

Basic checks: continuity,gη​(0)=0g_{\eta}(0)=0; unit derivative jump,g˙η​(0+)=25​(1+32)=1\dot{g}_{\eta}(0^{+})=\tfrac{2}{5}\big(1+\tfrac{3}{2}\big)=1;
positivity fors>0s>0(a positive source enhances the growth ofδ\delta). At late times the growing mode dominates,gη​(s)≈25​es=25​D+​(η)/D+​(η′)g_{\eta}(s)\approx\tfrac{2}{5}\,e^{s}=\tfrac{2}{5}\,D_{+}(\eta)/D_{+}(\eta^{\prime}),
while at early separationsgη​(s)≈sg_{\eta}(s)\approx s, the short-“time”
linear-response regime.

whose solution is the matrix exponentialga​b​(s)=ΘH​(s)​[e−𝛀​s]a​bg_{ab}(s)=\Theta_{H}(s)\,[e^{-{\bm{\Omega}}s}]_{ab}. The eigenvalues

or, component by component,g11=15​(3​es+2​e−3​s/2),g12=25​(es−e−3​s/2),g21=35​(es−e−3​s/2),g22=15​(2​es+3​e−3​s/2).g_{11}=\tfrac{1}{5}\big(3e^{s}+2e^{-3s/2}\big),\quad g_{12}=\tfrac{2}{5}\big(e^{s}-e^{-3s/2}\big),\quad g_{21}=\tfrac{3}{5}\big(e^{s}-e^{-3s/2}\big),\quad g_{22}=\tfrac{1}{5}\big(2e^{s}+3e^{-3s/2}\big).(352)

One easily checks thatga​b​(0)=δa​bg_{ab}(0)=\delta_{ab}and that each column

## B.5Rederivation ofF2F_{2}andG2G_{2}

As a consistency check — and to expose the origin of the famous5/75/7-type coefficients — let us recompute the second-order SPT
kernels from Eq. (354). Evaluating the
mode-coupling term of Eq. (339) on the linear
solutionΨa(1)=eη′​δ0​(1,1)T\Psi^{(1)}_{a}=e^{\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{0}\,(1,1)^{T}, i.e. contracting
the vertex (340) with(1,1)T(1,1)^{T}in both slots,Sb=∑c,dγb​c​dS_{b}=\sum_{c,d}\gamma_{bcd}, the source doublet for the mode𝒌=𝒒1+𝒒2{\bm{k}}={\bm{q}}_{1}+{\bm{q}}_{2}isSb​(𝒒1,𝒒2;η′)=e2​η′​(α(s)​(𝒒1,𝒒2)β​(𝒒1,𝒒2))b​δ0​(𝒒1)​δ0​(𝒒2),α(s)​(𝒒1,𝒒2)≡α​(𝒒1,𝒒2)+α​(𝒒2,𝒒1)2,S_{b}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2};\eta^{\prime})=e^{2\eta^{\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{(s)}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})\\[2.0pt]
\beta({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})\end{pmatrix}_{b}\delta_{0}({\bm{q}}_{1})\,\delta_{0}({\bm{q}}_{2})\,,\qquad\alpha^{(s)}({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})\equiv\frac{\alpha({\bm{q}}_{1},{\bm{q}}_{2})+\alpha({\bm{q}}_{2},{\bm{q}}_{1})}{2}\,,(355)

where the symmetrizedα(s)\alpha^{(s)}arises asγ121+γ112\gamma_{121}+\gamma_{112}.
Convolving with the propagator and usinge2​η′=e2​η​e−2​se^{2\eta^{\prime}}=e^{2\eta}e^{-2s}, the time integral factorizes into a
pure matrix of numbers:∫0∞𝑑s​ga​b​(s)​e−2​s=P+​∫0∞𝑑s​e−s+P−​∫0∞𝑑s​e−7​s/2=P++27​P−=17​(5234).\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\;g_{ab}(s)\,e^{-2s}=P_{+}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,e^{-s}+P_{-}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,e^{-7s/2}=P_{+}+\frac{2}{7}\,P_{-}=\frac{1}{7}\begin{pmatrix}5&2\\
3&4\end{pmatrix}\,.(356)

Reading off thea=1a=1anda=2a=2rows,F2=57​α(s)+27​β,G2=37​α(s)+47​β,F_{2}=\frac{5}{7}\,\alpha^{(s)}+\frac{2}{7}\,\beta\,,\qquad G_{2}=\frac{3}{7}\,\alpha^{(s)}+\frac{4}{7}\,\beta\,,(357)

which reproduces exactly the kernels of Eq. (71): the
coefficients5/75/7,2/72/7,3/73/7,4/74/7are nothing but the entries
of the time-integral matrix above.

## B.6Response to a Counterterm Source

Finally, consider the linear counterterm source of Chapter 4,Ja=(0,J2)TJ_{a}=(0,\,J_{2})^{T}withJ2​(𝒌,η′)=−k2​ℓ02​e(m+1)​η′​δ0​(𝒌)J_{2}({\bm{k}},\eta^{\prime})=-k^{2}\ell_{0}^{2}\,e^{(m+1)\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{0}({\bm{k}})(cf. Eq. (128)). Since only the second slot is
sourced, the density and velocity responses are governed byg12g_{12}andg22g_{22}respectively:Δ​δ(cs2)\displaystyle\Delta\delta^{(c_{s}^{2})}=∫𝑑η′​g12​(s)​J2​(η′)=−k2​ℓ02​e(m+1)​ηm​(m+52)​δ0​(𝒌),\displaystyle=\int d\eta^{\prime}\,g_{12}(s)\,J_{2}(\eta^{\prime})=-\frac{k^{2}\ell_{0}^{2}\,e^{(m+1)\eta}}{m\left(m+\tfrac{5}{2}\right)}\,\delta_{0}({\bm{k}})\,,(358)Δ​Θ(cs2)\displaystyle\Delta\Theta^{(c_{s}^{2})}=∫𝑑η′​g22​(s)​J2​(η′)=(m+1)​Δ​δ(cs2),\displaystyle=\int d\eta^{\prime}\,g_{22}(s)\,J_{2}(\eta^{\prime})=(m+1)\,\Delta\delta^{(c_{s}^{2})}\,,(359)

reproducing the result used in §4.1. The constant ratioΔ​Θ(cs2)/Δ​δ(cs2)=m+1\Delta\Theta^{(c_{s}^{2})}/\Delta\delta^{(c_{s}^{2})}=m+1(as opposed toΘ(1)/δ(1)=1\Theta^{(1)}/\delta^{(1)}=1for the pure growing mode) is a direct
measure of the decaying-mode admixture generated by the effective
stress tensor.

## Appendix CEquivalence of LPT and SPT at One Loop

In this appendix we start from the exact Lagrangian
formula (272) and show, by direct expansion, that
at one-loop order it reproduces the SPT result,P=P11+P22+2​P13P=P_{11}+P_{22}+2P_{13}, withP22P_{22}andP13P_{13}given by
Eqs. (81)–(83). Besides confirming the
statement made in Chapter 7, the computation is instructive: it
shows explicitly how the IR-sensitive pieces are distributed
differently between the terms of the two expansions.

Throughout the appendixP≡P11P\equiv P_{11}, and it is convenient to
abbreviate the longitudinal kernel contractionsa​(𝒑)≡𝒌⋅𝑳1​(𝒑)=𝒌⋅𝒑p2,b​(𝒑1,𝒑2)≡12​𝒌⋅𝑳2​(𝒑1,𝒑2),a({\bm{p}})\equiv{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{1}({\bm{p}})=\frac{{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{p}}}{p^{2}}\,,\qquad b({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2})\equiv\frac{1}{2}\,{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{2}({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2})\,,(360)

so that the kernel relation (291) readsF2=12​a​(𝒑1)​a​(𝒑2)+b​(𝒑1,𝒑2)F_{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}a({\bm{p}}_{1})a({\bm{p}}_{2})+b({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2}). We will
repeatedly use three elementary properties, all following from the
parity of the kernels: (i)a​(−𝒑)=−a​(𝒑)a(-{\bm{p}})=-a({\bm{p}})andb​(−𝒑1,−𝒑2)=−b​(𝒑1,𝒑2)b(-{\bm{p}}_{1},-{\bm{p}}_{2})=-b({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2}); (ii)b​(𝒑,−𝒑)=0b({\bm{p}},-{\bm{p}})=0(the
second-order displacement vanishes for collinear momenta, cf. the
tidal structure of Eq. (287)); and (iii) for any fixed𝒌{\bm{k}},b​(−𝒌,𝒑)=−b​(𝒌,−𝒑),b(-{\bm{k}},{\bm{p}})=-\,b({\bm{k}},-{\bm{p}})\,,(361)

since flipping both arguments flips𝑳2{\bm{L}}_{2}while the tidal
factor1−μ21-\mu^{2}is even.

## C.1Expansion of the Master Formula

LetX≡𝒌⋅[𝚿​(𝒒1)−𝚿​(𝒒2)]X\equiv{\bm{k}}\cdot\left[{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}}_{1})-{\bm{\Psi}}({\bm{q}}_{2})\right]and𝒒=𝒒1−𝒒2{\bm{q}}={\bm{q}}_{1}-{\bm{q}}_{2}. The cumulant expansion theorem gives⟨e−i​X⟩=exp⁡[−12​⟨X2⟩c+i6​⟨X3⟩c+124​⟨X4⟩c+⋯].\langle e^{-iX}\rangle=\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\langle X^{2}\rangle_{c}+\frac{i}{6}\langle X^{3}\rangle_{c}+\frac{1}{24}\langle X^{4}\rangle_{c}+\cdots\right]\,.(362)

Power counting inP11P_{11}:⟨X2⟩c\langle X^{2}\rangle_{c}starts atO​(P)O(P)and
receives one-loop corrections atO​(P2)O(P^{2});⟨X3⟩c\langle X^{3}\rangle_{c}starts atO​(P2)O(P^{2})(it needs one second-order displacement);⟨X4⟩c\langle X^{4}\rangle_{c}starts atO​(P3)O(P^{3})and can be dropped. Expanding the exponential toO​(P2)O(P^{2}),⟨e−i​X⟩−1=−12​⟨X2⟩c+i6​⟨X3⟩c+18​(⟨X2⟩c)2+O​(P3).\langle e^{-iX}\rangle-1=-\frac{1}{2}\langle X^{2}\rangle_{c}+\frac{i}{6}\langle X^{3}\rangle_{c}+\frac{1}{8}\left(\langle X^{2}\rangle_{c}\right)^{2}+O(P^{3})\,.(363)

Using statistical homogeneity, the second cumulant is⟨X2⟩c=2​ki​kj​∫𝒑Ci​j​(𝒑)​[1−cos⁡(𝒑⋅𝒒)],\langle X^{2}\rangle_{c}=2\,k_{i}k_{j}\int_{\bm{p}}C^{ij}({\bm{p}})\left[1-\cos({\bm{p}}\cdot{\bm{q}})\right]\,,(364)

withCi​jC^{ij}from Eq. (300) — note that at linear
order this is exactly the exponent of the Zel’dovich
formula (280). For the third cumulant, expandingX3=(𝒌⋅𝚿1−𝒌⋅𝚿2)3X^{3}=({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}_{1}-{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}}_{2})^{3}, the zero-lag pieces⟨(𝒌⋅𝚿)3⟩\langle({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{\Psi}})^{3}\ranglecancel between the two ends, and using
Eq. (301),⟨X3⟩c=3​i​ki​kj​kl​∫𝒑1∫𝒑2Ci​j​l​(𝒑1,𝒑2,−𝒑12)​[ei​𝒑1⋅𝒒−ei​𝒑12⋅𝒒],\langle X^{3}\rangle_{c}=3i\,k_{i}k_{j}k_{l}\int_{{\bm{p}}_{1}}\!\int_{{\bm{p}}_{2}}C^{ijl}({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2},-{\bm{p}}_{12})\left[e^{i{\bm{p}}_{1}\cdot{\bm{q}}}-e^{i{\bm{p}}_{12}\cdot{\bm{q}}}\right]\,,(365)

where the first (second) phase comes from the configurations with
one (two) displacement(s) at the point𝒒1{\bm{q}}_{1}.

## C.3Assembly

TheP22P_{22}part.Collecting the terms with two
loop-momentum spectra from
Eqs. (366), (369)
and (370):∫𝒑[12​a12​a22+2​a1​a2​b+2​b2]​P1​P2=2​∫𝒑[a1​a22+b]2​P1​P2=2​∫𝒑F22​(𝒑,𝒌−𝒑)​P1​P2=P22​(k),\int_{\bm{p}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\,a_{1}^{2}a_{2}^{2}+2\,a_{1}a_{2}\,b+2\,b^{2}\right]P_{1}P_{2}=2\int_{\bm{p}}\left[\frac{a_{1}a_{2}}{2}+b\right]^{2}P_{1}P_{2}=2\int_{\bm{p}}F_{2}^{2}({\bm{p}},{\bm{k}}-{\bm{p}})\,P_{1}P_{2}=P_{22}(k)\,,(371)

with(𝒑1,𝒑2)=(𝒑,𝒌−𝒑)({\bm{p}}_{1},{\bm{p}}_{2})=({\bm{p}},{\bm{k}}-{\bm{p}}): the three contributions are
precisely the(F2ZA)2(F_{2}^{\mathrm{ZA}})^{2}, cross, and(𝒌⋅𝑳2)2({\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{2})^{2}pieces of the squared kernel relation (291).

TheP13P_{13}part.The remaining terms are all proportional
toP​(k)P(k):P​(k)​∫𝒑[𝒌⋅𝑳3​(𝒌,𝒑,−𝒑)+a​(𝒑)​(3​b​(𝒌,−𝒑)−b​(𝒌,𝒑))]​P​(p)−k2​σd2​P​(k).P(k)\int_{\bm{p}}\Big[{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{3}({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}},-{\bm{p}})+a({\bm{p}})\big(3\,b({\bm{k}},-{\bm{p}})-b({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}})\big)\Big]P(p)\;-\;k^{2}\sigma_{d}^{2}\,P(k)\,.(372)

Two massages are needed. First, split3​b​(𝒌,−𝒑)−b​(𝒌,𝒑)=2​[b​(𝒌,−𝒑)−b​(𝒌,𝒑)]+[b​(𝒌,−𝒑)+b​(𝒌,𝒑)]3b({\bm{k}},-{\bm{p}})-b({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}})=2\left[b({\bm{k}},-{\bm{p}})-b({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}})\right]+\left[b({\bm{k}},-{\bm{p}})+b({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}})\right]and note thata​(𝒑)​[b​(𝒌,−𝒑)+b​(𝒌,𝒑)]a({\bm{p}})\left[b({\bm{k}},-{\bm{p}})+b({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}})\right]isoddunder𝒑→−𝒑{\bm{p}}\to-{\bm{p}}, so it integrates to zero. Second,
recognize the zero-lag term as the Zel’dovich part of the1313configuration:k2​σd2​P​(k)=P​(k)​∫𝒑a2​(𝒑)​P​(p)=−6​P​(k)​∫𝒑F3ZA​(𝒌,𝒑,−𝒑)​P​(p)k^{2}\sigma_{d}^{2}P(k)=P(k)\int_{\bm{p}}a^{2}({\bm{p}})P(p)=-6P(k)\int_{\bm{p}}F_{3}^{\mathrm{ZA}}({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}},-{\bm{p}})P(p),
sinceF3ZA​(𝒌,𝒑,−𝒑)=13!​a​(𝒌)​a​(𝒑)​a​(−𝒑)=−16​a2​(𝒑)F_{3}^{\mathrm{ZA}}({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}},-{\bm{p}})=\tfrac{1}{3!}a({\bm{k}})a({\bm{p}})a(-{\bm{p}})=-\tfrac{1}{6}a^{2}({\bm{p}}). On the
other hand, the symmetrized kernel relation (292),
evaluated in the(𝒌,𝒑,−𝒑)({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}},-{\bm{p}})configuration and usingb​(𝒑,−𝒑)=0b({\bm{p}},-{\bm{p}})=0, readsF3​(𝒌,𝒑,−𝒑)=−16​a2​(𝒑)+13​a​(𝒑)​[b​(𝒌,−𝒑)−b​(𝒌,𝒑)]+13!​𝒌⋅𝑳3​(𝒌,𝒑,−𝒑).F_{3}({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}},-{\bm{p}})=-\frac{1}{6}\,a^{2}({\bm{p}})+\frac{1}{3}\,a({\bm{p}})\left[b({\bm{k}},-{\bm{p}})-b({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}})\right]+\frac{1}{3!}\,{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{L}}_{3}({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}},-{\bm{p}})\,.(373)

Comparing term by term, the expression (372) is
exactly6​P​(k)​∫𝒑F3​(𝒌,𝒑,−𝒑)​P​(p)=2​P13​(k).6\,P(k)\int_{\bm{p}}F_{3}({\bm{k}},{\bm{p}},-{\bm{p}})\,P(p)=2P_{13}(k)\,.(374)

Adding the tree-level piece from Eq. (366), we obtainP(k)=P11(k)+P22(k)+2P13(k),\boxed{\;P(k)=P_{11}(k)+P_{22}(k)+2P_{13}(k)\,,\;}(375)

completing the proof of the one-loop equivalence.

## C.4Remarks

First, note where the IR-sensitive pieces reside. In the Lagrangian
organization,boththe+k2​σd,IR2+k^{2}\sigma^{2}_{d,\mathrm{IR}}piece
ofP22P_{22}(the soft limit of the12​a2​a2\tfrac{1}{2}a^{2}a^{2}term) and the−k2​σd,IR2-k^{2}\sigma^{2}_{d,\mathrm{IR}}piece
of2​P132P_{13}(the zero-lag cross term) originate from one and the
same object — the square of the second
cumulant (370), whose[1−cos]​[1−cos][1-\cos][1-\cos]structure
guarantees their cancellation before any expansion is performed.
The equal-time IR cancellation of Chapter 3, which in SPT requires a
conspiracy between different diagrams, is manifest term by term in
LPT: the exponent only ever contains the relative displacement.

Second, the derivation used nothing but the parity of the kernels
and the kernel relations (291)–(292), which
are themselves consequences of expanding the same exponential at the
field level. The pattern therefore persists at all orders: expanding
the cumulant representation of Eq. (272) reproduces
the Eulerian loop expansion order by order, with the partitions of
the displacement cumulants reassembling into products of SPT
kernels.
